Lectures notes on number theory for computer science
Alexandros V. Gerbessiotis

Abstract




Preface

This brief, in the form of an e-book,
is a collection of notes
that cover elementary and medium
level number theory with a target audience of
primarily computer science students.
It can be used in the number
theory portion of a discrete mathematics course,
or a course on the mathematical foundations of
computer science, or as background material
for a cryptography course.

Thematically it is split into five areas that
map to chapters.
The first chapter is introductory and covers
topics including divisibility,
prime numbers, and modular arithmetic including modular
linear equations. The second chapter covers additional
topics such as Euler’s totient function,
units and inverses, the Chinese remainder theorem,
and Fermat’s and Euler’s theorems.
The following chapter covers primitive roots, quadratic
residues, the Jacobi and Legendre symbols, Gauss’s lemma
and Eisenstein’s theorem, and briefly discusses applications
of number theory to cryptography.
The fourth chapter is focused on traditional primality
testing methods covering Miller’s algorithms,
Rabin’s conversion of a Miller algorithm
into a probabilistic primality test algorithm,
Solovay-Strassen’s algorithm and several other
peripheral results including Carmichael
numbers and the equivalence of Miller’s two algorithms.
Finally the last brief chapter can be
viewed as an introduction to more advanced elements
of number theory and its coverage includes multiplicative
functions, the Möbius function, Dirichlet products and
Dirichlet and Möbius inversions.

Different parts of this e-book are for
freshman to senior undergraduate students in computing
and in particular computer science.
Graduate students with limited exposure to number
theory can use it to acquire a background suitable
for typical cryptography courses at the master’s level.

Chapter 1
Introductory Number Theory

1.1
Numbers

Definition 1.1
(Integers)

The set of integers
is denoted as
\mathbb{Z}
.

\mathbb{Z}=\{\ldots,-3,-2,-1,0,1,2,3,\ldots\}.

Definition 1.2
(Natural integer numbers)

A
natural integer number
, or natural number,
or ordinal number is a non-negative integer number.
The set of natural numbers is denoted as
\mathbb{N}
.

\mathbb{N}=\{0,1,2,3,\ldots\}.

This definition varies in different textbooks.
Although we define a natural integer number as a
non-negative integer number,
several alternative sources describe a natural integer
number as a positive integer number thus excluding zero.

Definition 1.3
(Positive and negative integers)

The set of positive integers
is denoted as
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,
the set of negative integers
is denoted as
\mathbb{Z}_{-}
,
and the set of non-zero integers
is denoted as
\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.

\mathbb{Z}_{+}=\{1,2,3,\ldots\}.

\mathbb{Z}_{-}=\{-1,-2,-3,\ldots\}.

\mathbb{Z}^{*}=\{\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 3,\ldots\}.

For the positive and negative integers one may also
use
\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{*}
and
\mathbb{Z}_{-}^{*}
.

\mathbb{Z}
is the set of integers (positive, negative or zero).
For a positive integer
n>0
the set

\mathbb{Z}_{n}
sometimes denoted as
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
or
\mathbb{Z}/n

is the set of integers modulo
n
, thus representing the
n

equivalence classes that the integers of
\mathbb{Z}
can be
split into depending on the remainder of their division by
n
.

Integer one is an integer, a rational and algebraic and
thus a real number.
Number
4/5
is rational and algebraic and thus a real number.
Number
\sqrt{2}
is irrational, algebraic and thus a real number.
Number
\pi
is irrational, transcendental and thus a real number.

1.2
Divisibility and compositeness

Definition 1.4
(Divisibility)

The symbol for divide is
|
.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z},b\in\mathbb{Z},a\neq 0
.
We write
a|b
,
a\neq 0
, and read

a
divides
b
,
if there exists an integer
q\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
b=aq
.

Integer
a
is then a divisor or factor of
b

and
b
is a multiple of
a
.
When
a
divides
b
we can also say
b
is divided (evenly)
by
a
or
b
is divisible by
a
,
or equivalently the division of
b
by
a
leaves a remainder of zero.
For
a|b
,
a
is never a zero;
b
can be zero.

For a division of
b
divided by
a
,
integer
b
is the dividend in such a division.
Integer
a
is the divisor of
b
also known as a
factor of
b
.
The quotient
q
is
q=b/a=\lfloor b/a\rfloor
, and the integer remainder

r
is
r=b-a*q
. The remainder is unique if
0\leq r<|b|
.
If
a|b
then
q=b/a
is an integer and
r=0
.

We say
a
does not divide
b
if there is no
such
q\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
b=aq
.
We then write
a\nmid b
.

Definition 1.5

If
a
does not divide
b
we write
a\nmid b
instead.

Example 1.2.1

The following integers divide 50.

1,2,5,10,25,50.

We usually write down the positive divisors. The negative
divisors are implied. Therefore a complete list of divisors
would be as follows.

\pm 1,\pm 2,\pm 5,\pm 10,\pm 25,\pm 50.

Definition 1.6
(Trivial divisor)

The trivial divisors of
n
are
1
and
n
.
If one includes negative numbers, they are
1
,
-1
,
n
and
-n
.

Definition 1.7
(Odd, even)

An integer
n
is even if it is a multiple of two.
Otherwise it is an odd integer.

Example 1.2.2

Every integer
a
is a divisor of
0
that is,

\forall a\in\mathbb{Z}
we
have
a|0
.

0
is a divisor of itself and only itself.

Solution.

Since
0=a\cdot 0
the first claim follows:
a
is a divisor of
0
.
Since
0=0\cdot q
, integer 0 is a divisor of 0.
There is no way for any
d\neq 0
to have
d=0\cdot q
.
Thus 0 cannot be the divisor of any
d\neq 0
.
Thus 0 only divides 0; moreover, 0 is a multiple of every integer!
∎

Example 1.2.3

Both
5
and
-5
are divisors of
5
.
Both
1
and
-1
are divisors of
5
.

Solution.

It is
5=5\cdot 1
and
5=(-5)(-1)
. Replacing
5
by
a
,
for every integer
a
, both
\pm a
and
\pm 1
are divisors of
a
.
Thus integer
5
has four divisors
+1,-1,+5,-5
.
And so so does
-5
. And in fact any
b\neq 0,1,-1
has at least
four divisors which are
\pm b,\pm 1
.
0 has an infinite number of divisors (in facts its
set of divisors is
\mathbb{Z}
).
Only
+1,-1
have two divisors each (including the other one of the
pair).
+1
is the positive unit and
-1
the negative unit
and collectively are known as the units of
\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Example 1.2.4

If
a|b
then
-a|b,\quad a|-b,\quad a|-b
.

Solution.

If
b=aq
then
b=(-a)(-q)
and
-b=a(-q)
and
-b=a(-q)
.
∎

Example 1.2.5

If
a|b
and
a|c
,
then
a|b\pm c
.

Solution.

We have from
a|b
that
b=am
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Likewise
c=an
for some
n\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Adding or subtracting we have
b\pm c=a(m\pm n)
and
the result follows.
∎

Example 1.2.6

If
a|b
and
b\neq 0
then
|a|\leq|b|
.

Solution.

Since
a|b
we have that
b=am
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
b\neq 0
we have
a\neq 0
as well. Furthermore
m\neq 0

as well. Thus
|m|\geq 1
. And thus
|b|=|am|=|a||m|\geq|a|
.
∎

Theorem 1.1

(Properties of divisibility)

If
a,b,c,d,k,m\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

(a)

1|a
.
a|0
and
0|0
.
Moreover,
0|a
implies
a=0
.

(b1)

a|a
and of course
\pm a|\pm a
.

(b2)

a|b\wedge b|c\Rightarrow a|c
.

(c1)

If
a|b
then for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a|kb
.

(c2)

If
a|b
then for every
k\in\mathbb{Z}
,
ka|kb
.

(d1)

if
a|b
and
a|c
then
a|b+c
.

(d2)

if
a|b
and
a|c
then
a|b-c
.

(d3)

if
a|b
and
a|c
then
a|kb\pm mc
.

(f1)

if
a|b
and
b\neq 0
then
|a|\leq|b|
.

(f2)

if
a|b
and
a,b>0
then
a\leq b
.

(f3)

if
a|b
and
b|a
then
|a|=|b|
.

(g)

if
ka|kb
and
k\neq 0
then
a|b
.

Proof.

(a)
a=1\cdot a
implies
1|a
.

0=a\cdot 0
implies
a|0
.

0=0\cdot 0
implies
0|0
,
and
a=0\cdot q
implies
a=0
and this concludes the case.

(b1)
a=a\cdot 1
implies
a|a
.
Moreover

-a=a\cdot(-1)
concludes the case.

(b2) If
a|b
, then
b=aq
, for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
b|c
, then
c=br
, for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.
This implies
c=b\cdot r=(a\cdot q)\cdot r=a\cdot(qr)

i.e.
a|c
.

(c1) If
a|b
, then
b=aq
, for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then

kb=kaq=a(kq)
i.e.
a|kb
.

(c2) If
a|b
, then
b=aq
, for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
. Then

kb=kaq=(ka)q
i.e.
ka|kb
.

(d1) If
a|b
, then
b=aq
, for some
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.
If
a|c
, then
c=ar
, for some
r\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then
b+c=a(q+r)
implies
a|b+c
.

(d2) Moreover for the
a,b,c
of (d1) we have

b-c=a(q-r)
implies
a|b-c
.

(d3) Furthermore for the
a,b,c
of (d1) we have

kb\pm mc=a(kq\pm mr)
implies
a|kb\pm mc
.

(f1) If
a|b
then
b=aq
. Then
|b|=|aq|=|a|\cdot|q|
.
If
b\neq 0
then
|b|>0
.
(Absolute values are positive or zero.)
This implies that
|a|>0
and
|q|>0
.
The latter is equivalent to
|q|\geq 1
.
Then
|b|=|a|\cdot|q|\geq|a|\cdot 1\geq|a|
.
Equivalently
|a|\leq|b|
.

(f2) If all of
a,b
are positive we can drop the absolute values
from (f1) concluding
a\leq b
.

(f3) From (f1) we have
|a|\leq|b|
. If
b|a
we can
likewise conclude that
|b|\leq|a|
thus deriving
|a|=|b|
.

(g) if
ka|kb
and
k\neq 0
then
kb=(ka)q
. For non-zero

k
dividing both sides we have
b=a\cdot q
and thus
a|b
.
∎

Theorem 1.2

(Uniqueness)

For every
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{*},b\in\mathbb{Z}
, if
a|b
,
there is a unique integer
q\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
b=aq
.

Proof.

Suppose that
a|b
i.e.
b=aq
with

q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
i.e.
a\neq 0
. If
q
is not
unique, then there might exist a
q_{1}
such that
b=aq_{1}
and
q\neq q_{1}
.
Then
b=aq=aq_{1}
implies
a(q-q_{1})=0
. Since
a\neq 0
, it must be

q-q_{1}=0
. Then
q=q_{1}
but this contradicts to the existence of

q_{1}\neq q
.
∎

1.3
Primes

Definition 1.8
(Prime numbers)

A natural number
p>1
is a prime number if it is not
the product of two smaller natural numbers.

For example for natural number
5
the only product that gives

5
is
5\cdot 1
. In that product
5
is not a smaller
natural number than
5
and thus
5
is prime.
Natural number
6
is not a prime number:
6=2\cdot 3

and both
2,3
are smaller (natural numbers) than
6
.

Definition 1.9
(Prime)

An integer
p\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{*}
is a prime (number) if and only
if
p\neq 1
and its only positive divisors are 1 and
p
.

An integer
p\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
is a prime (number) if and only
if
p\neq\pm 1
and its only divisors are the units
+1

and
-1
of
\mathbb{Z}
and
p
, and
-p

A number that is not prime and not a unit (
+1
or
-1
) is
a composite number.

Definition 1.10
(Composite)

An integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
such that
n\neq\pm 1
, it is either
a prime or a composite (integer) number.

Definition 1.11
(1 is a unit)

1 is neither a prime number nor a composite number.
It is a unit.

Lemma 1.1
(Composite)

An integer
n\in\mathbb{Z_{+}}^{*}
is composite
if and only if it has a factor
a
such that
1<a<n
.
Then there is another factor
q
such that
1<q<n
,
and
q=n/a
.

Proof.

If
n
is composite, then
n
is a multiple of integer

a
that is neither
1
nor
n
.
Then there exists
q
such that
n=aq
for some integer
q
.
If
a
is neither
1
nor
n
then
1<a<n
. (We also
used (f2) from Theorem
1.1
.)
Since
n=aq
,
q
is also positive and
q>1
.
Since
a>1
, we have
n=aq>q
implies

q<n
. And since
a
is not
n
then
q
cannot be
1
.
∎

Theorem 1.3

(Composite with a prime factor)

An integer
n\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
with
n>1
has
a prime factor
p
such that
p|n
.

Proof.

Let
Q
be the set of natural integer numbers greater than one, that
have no prime factors. We shall show that
Q=\emptyset
.

Say
Q
is not empty. Then there is a minimum element say
m\in Q
.
Since
m|m
, and
m
has no prime factors,
m
cannot be a prime
number. Thus
m
is composite and let
m=qr
, where
1<q,r<m
.
Since either
q
or
r
is
<m
, and
m
is the smallest element of

Q
it means
q\not\in Q
. By definition
q
has a prime factor
p

i.e.
p|q
and
q|m
. By transitivity
p|m
.
The latter contradicts the fact that
m
being in
Q
it should
not have prime factors (such as
p
). Thus
Q
has no minimum
element
m
and thus it is empty!
∎

Theorem 1.4

(A factor less than
\sqrt{n}
for
n
)

For a composite integer
n>1
one of its prime
factors(divisors) is less than or equal to
\sqrt{n}
.

Proof.

Let
a,b
be such that
n=ab
.
We have
1<a\leq b<n
. If
a>\sqrt{n}
then
b\geq a>\sqrt{n}

and therefore
n=ab>\sqrt{n}\cdot\sqrt{n}=n
, which is impossible.
It follows that
1<a\leq\sqrt{n}
.
Thus
a
has a prime factor
p

by Lemma
1.3
and
p\leq a
.
Since
p|a
and
a|n
we conclude that
p|n
,
with
p\leq a\leq\sqrt{n}
.
∎

Example 1.3.1

Show that if
2|ab
for
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

2|a
or
2|b
.

Solution.

If neither
2|a
nor
2|b
, then

a,b
are odd numbers and thus
a=2n+1
and
b=2m+1
.
Then since
ab
is an even number by way
of
2|ab
we obtain

ab=(2n+1)(2m+1)=4mn+2m+2n+1=2(2mn+m+n)+1,

which is clearly an odd integer number and thus
not even contradicting the fact that
ab
is even!
∎

Example 1.3.2

Prime numbers are irreducible.
Thus if
p
is a prime number then
p
cannot be written
in the form
p=qr
where
q,r
are both non units.
(In other words for an irreducible number either
q

or
r
is a unit.)

Solution.

Note 0 cannot be a prime number therefore
p\neq 0
.
Say prime number
p
is not irreducible.
If a prime number
p
is not irreducible, then
p=qr
where
neither
q
nor
r
is a unit.
Then
p|qr
.
Moreover
q|p
and
r|p
,
and therefore
|q|\leq|p|
and
|r|\leq|p|
.
Then
|q|\leq|p|
and
|r|\leq|p|
.
We claim
p\nmid q
and
p\nmid r
.
If this was not the case (say the former)
then
p|q
i.e.
|p|\leq|q|
which with
|q|\leq|p|

implies
q=\pm p
and then
r=\pm 1
. This contradicts
the assumption that
r
is not a unit.
∎

1.3.1
Inifinitely many primes

Lemma 1.2
(A theorem by Euclid)

There are infinitely many prime numbers.

Proof.

If there are only finite prime numbers and let them ALL
be
p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
, where
p_{1}=2
.
Then form integer
n=p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{m}+1
.
There are two possibilities for
n
: (a) it is a prime number,
(b) it is not a prime number.

Case 1.
If
n
is a prime number
Then
n=2\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{i}\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{m}+1>>2p_{i}+1>p_{i}

given that all
p_{i}>1
. We have just found one more prime number
beyond the
m
ones that were declared ALL that there are: a
contradiction.

Case 2.
If
n
is a composite number, let
p
be
a prime factor of
n
, i.e.
p|n
with
p>1
.
Such a
p
exists by the previous Lemma (composite
with a prime factor). This
p
cannot be one of the
m

p_{1},\ldots,p_{m}
. Why ? if
p
was say
p=p_{i}
then

p|p_{i}
implies by Theorem
1.1
(c1,d3) that

p|p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{m}
.
Since
p|n
and

n=p_{1}\cdot p_{2}\cdot\ldots\cdot p_{m}+1

the
p
divides their difference i.e.
p|1
by
Theorem
1.1
(d2).
This means
p
is one by Theorem
1.1
(f2).
But one is a unit not a prime number (plus also the fact
that
p>1
).
∎

1.4
Integer division

Theorem 1.5

(Division)

For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and
b\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
there exist unique integers

q\in\mathbb{Z}
and
r\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

a=bq+r\quad,\quad 0\leq r<|b|.

Proof.

Case 1 (
a\geq 0,b>0
).

Let
A
be the set of all non-negative integers
a-bi
, where

i
is such that
a-bi\geq 0
.
For
i=0
,
a
belongs to
A
, and thus
A
is not empty.
By the Well-ordered set principle
A
has a
minimum and let it be
r
. Since
r
is in
A
, we
have
r\geq 0
and
r=a-bi
for some integer
i
.
All it remains to show is that

r<|b|=b
. Say that
r\geq b
instead. Then
r-b\geq 0
.
Moreover

r-b=(a-bi)-b=a-b(i+1).

Thus
r-b\geq 0
and is of the form

a-bi^{\prime}
, with
i^{\prime}=i+1
.
Thus it belongs to
A
. Moreover
r-b
is less than

r
,
r-b<r
since
b
is positive. We have found an
element smaller than the minimum
element of
A
. This contradicts to the choice
of
r
as being the minimum;
we reached contradiction because we assume
r\geq b
.
Thus
r<b
. This thus establishes that
0\leq r<b
.

We have yet to prove the pair
(q,r)
is unique.
Let is not be and let another pair be

(q^{\prime},r^{\prime})
. Then

a=bq+r=bq^{\prime}+r^{\prime}
,
where
0\leq r,r^{\prime}<b
.
This gives
b(q-q^{\prime})=r^{\prime}-r
, and

|b(q-q^{\prime})|=|r^{\prime}-r|
.
Adding
0\leq r
and
r^{\prime}<b
we get
r^{\prime}<r+b
or
equivalently
r^{\prime}-r<b
. Thus
|b(q-q^{\prime})|<b
.
As
b>0
we have
|q-q^{\prime}|<1
. This can only be possible,
for
q=q^{\prime}
since
q,q^{\prime}
are integer.
Then from
a=bq+r=bq^{\prime}+r^{\prime}
if
q=q^{\prime}
,
we deduce also that
r=r^{\prime}
.
This answers positively the question about
the uniqueness of the pair
(q,r)
for case 1.

Case 2 (
a<0,b>0
).
Similarly as before
A

is not empty because
a-bi\geq 0
contains at least
one element
a-ba\geq 0
, and the
rest of the discussion is similar to case 1.

Case 3 (
a\in\mathbb{Z},b<0
).
Then
|b|>0
,
and of course
|b|=-b
.
By way of cases 1 and 2 for
a
and
|b|
we have that

a=|b|q+r
, where
0\leq r<|b|
. This is
equivalent to
a=(-b)q+r
, where
0\leq r<|b|
. This is
equivalent to
a=b(-q)+r
, where
0\leq r<|b|
. The
claim is satisfied for case 3 as well.

Conclusion.
For a pair
(a,b\neq 0)
there is a unique pair

(q,r)
with
a=bq+r
and
0\leq r<|b|
.
∎

Example 1.4.1

Find the quotient and the remainder of the division
of 37 by 5.
Then find the quotient and the remainder of the division
of -37 by 5.

Solution.

(a) For 37 we have the following

37=5\cdot 7+2.

37 is the divident, 5 is the divisor and 7 the quotient.
The remainder is 2. Note that
0\leq 2<|5|
.

(b) For
-37
we have the following

-37=5\cdot(-8)+3.

-37
is the divident, 5 is the divisor and
-8
the quotient.
The remainder is 3. Note that
0\leq 3<|5|
.
One can also observe that

-37=5\cdot(-7)-2.

However only the remainder
3
is one that satisfies
the
0\leq 3<|5|
requirement and is unique.
∎

Theorem 1.6

(Division results)

For
a,b
as in division (
b\neq 0
), where

a=bq+r
and
0\leq r<|b|
, we have the following.

(i)

if
d|a
and
d|b
then
d|r
.

(ii)

if
d|r
and
d|b
then
d|a
.

Proof.

(i) If
d|aandd|b
then from the latter

d|bq
and thus from the former

d|a-bq
leading (given that
a=bq+r
) to
d|r
.

(ii) If
d|r,d|b
the
d|bq
and thus

d|bq+r
which leads to
d|a
.
∎

Theorem 1.7

(Remainder of division by
b
)

Let
a,A\in\mathbb{Z}
with
b|a
and
b|A
.
The remainders of the divisions of
a,A
with
b

are the same if
and only if
a-A
is a multiple of
b
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
.
If
a=bq+r
and
A=bQ+R
, and we
are given
r=R
, the
a-A=bq+r-qQ-r=b(q-Q)
. Since
q-Q\in\mathbb{Z}
,
we conclude that
b|a-A
.

\Leftarrow
.

Another way to prove
\Leftarrow
it is directly as follows.
Say that
b|a-A
, i.e.
a-A=bm
for some
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Then let
a=bq+r
and
A=bQ+R
, where
0\leq r,R<|b|
.
From the former
a-A=bm
and
A=bQ+R
we have

a-(bQ+R)=bm
implying
a=b(m+Q)+R
. This latter equality
implies
q=m+Q
and
r=R
as
R
is such that
0\leq R<|b|)
.
Result is proven.
∎

Example 1.4.2

Show the following: if
a^{2}|b^{2}
then
a|b
,
for every
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

Solution.

A simple solution that relies on rational numbers vs
irrational numbers is as follows.
If
a^{2}|b^{2}
then there exists an integer
k
such
that
b^{2}=a^{2}k
. Then,
a^{2}/b^{2}=k
and therefore

(b/a)=\sqrt{k}
. Therefore since
a,b
are integer,
then
b/a
is a rational number.
\sqrt{k}
is algebraic
but also rational; it can’t be irrational since that
would make
b/a
irrational contradicting to the rationality
of
b/a
. Since
\sqrt{k}
is rational, and
k
integer,
there can only be that
\sqrt{k}
is also integer.
Then
b=a\sqrt{k}
and thus
a|b
.
∎

Example 1.4.3

Show the following: if
a^{2}|b^{2}
then
a|b
,
for every
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

Solution.

Let for the sake of contradiction that
a\nmid b
.

a^{2}|b^{2}\Rightarrow\exists a_{2}:b^{2}=a^{2}a_{2}.

(1.1)

Consider,

B(a)=\{bt:t\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},a^{2}|b^{2}\wedge a|bt\}.

Let
k
be the smallest value such that
bk\in B(a)
.

B(a)
is not empty.
Since
a|b^{2}
for
t=b
we have
bt=b^{2}\in B(a)
.
The former is true because
because
a^{2}|b^{2}
implies

b^{2}=a^{2}r
and thus
a|a^{2}r
implies

a|b^{2}
i.e.
a|bt
for
t=b
.
Since
k
is the smallest
t
that generates the min
element of
B(a)
by the W.O.S.P.
we have by way of
a|bt
,
t=k
.

bk=aa_{1}

(1.2)

Consider

aba_{1}=ab\frac{bk}{a}=b^{2}k=a^{2}a_{2}k.

(1.3)

We further conclude by dividing by
a\neq 0
,
the following

aba_{1}=a^{2}a_{2}k\Rightarrow ba_{1}=aa_{2}k.

(1.4)

By the division theorem for
a_{1}
and
k
we have the following.

a_{1}=kq+r,0\leq r<k\Rightarrow r=a_{1}-kq\Rightarrow br=ba_{1}-bkq\Rightarrow br=aa_{2}k-aa_{1}q\Rightarrow br=a(a_{2}k-a_{1}q)\Rightarrow a|br

(1.5)

In the previous derivation we uses in sequence
Eq.(
1.4
),
Eq.(
1.2
).
Therefore
br
is in
A
since
a^{2}|b^{2}

and
a|br
. Since
r<k
we have that

br
is smaller than the smallest element of
B(a)

which is
bk
. This leads to contradiction.
∎

Lemma 1.3

Integer
d
such that
d|n
if and only if the
remainder of the division of
n
by
d
is
0
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
(only-if).

If
d
is such that
d|n
this means

n=qd
that is
n=qd+0
. Given the
uniqueness of
q,r
from Theorem
1.5
we
conclude
q=q
and
r=0
.

\Leftarrow
(if).

If
d
is such that
n=d\cdot q+r
with
r=0
,
then
n=d\cdot q
which implies
d|n
.
∎

1.5
Greatest common divisor

Definition 1.12
(Greatest common divisor)

For integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\gcd(a,b)
is the
greatest
common divisor
of
a
and
b
if and only if
\gcd(a,b)|a

and
\gcd(a,b)|b
and every other divisor
c
of
a,b
is such
that
c\leq\gcd(a,b)
.
Therefore,

(i)

\gcd(a,b)|a
and
\gcd(a,b)|b
,

(ii)

c|a
and
c|b

\Rightarrow

c\leq\gcd(a,b)
.

Moreover,
\gcd(a,b)\leq|a|
and
\gcd(a,b)\leq|b|
.

Note that if
c|a
then by Theorem
1.1
(v) we have
|c|\leq|a|
.
Likewise if
c|b
we have
|c|\leq|b|
. Combining the two we have

|c|\leq\max(|a|,|b|)
. Thus the set of common divisors of
a,b
is finite.
A finite set of integers always has a maximum, and thus there is a
uniquest largest integer
d>0
such that
d|a
and
d|b
. We
call
d
the
greatest common divisor
of
a,b
and denote it by

\gcd(a,b)
thus
d=\gcd(a,b)
.

Example 1.5.1

(a) The greatest common divisor of
1
and
n
is
1
.

(b) If
a|b
then
\gcd(a,b)=b
.

(c)
\gcd(5,15)=5
.
\gcd(30,105)=15
.

(d) The common divisors of 30 and 105 are

\{1,3,5,15,\}
. If we include negative numbers then it is

\{\pm 1,\pm 3,\pm 5,\pm 15,\}
, twice as many.

Note 1.1

The
\gcd(0,0)
is not defined as the set of common divisors is an infinite
set and it does not have a maximum.
In the remainder when
\gcd(a,b)
is considered, we would assume
that
a\neq 0
or
b\neq 0
(or both). Thus it cannot be that
both
a
and
b
are zero.
One way to express this is by having

|a|+|b|\neq 0
.

Definition 1.13

Let
S(a)
be the set of divisors of
a
.

Fact 1.1
(Simple GCD facts)

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
|a|+|b|\neq 0
. Then
the following apply.

(i)

\gcd(a,b)>0
and also
\gcd(a,b)\geq 1
.

(ii)

\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(|a|,|b|)
.

(iii)

\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(b,a)
.

(iv)

\gcd(a,1)=1
.

(v)

\gcd(a,0)=|a|
for all
a\neq 0
.

(vi)

\gcd(a,b)=|a|
if and only if
a|b
.

Proof.

(i) Obviously.
gcd(a,b)
is the maximum of the
common divisors of
a
and
b
i.e. the maximum elements of

S(a)\cap S(b)
. One positive element of this set of common
divisors is 1 and thus
gcd(a,b)\geq 1
in addition to
\gcd(a,b)>0
.

(ii) Since
S(a)=S(|a|)
and
S(b)=S(|b|)
we have that

S(a)\cap S(b)=S(|a|)\cap S(|b|)
. Thus

\gcd(a,b)
is equal to
\gcd(|a|,|b|)
since the set
of common divisors are equal to each other.

(iii) It is a consequence of the fact that
S(a)\cap S(b)=S(b)\cap S(a)
.

(iv) Since
1|a
trivially, and the largest divisor of 1 is 1
itself the result follows. (Note that
S(1)=\{-1,+1\}
.)

(v)
S(a)\cap S(0)=S(a)\cap\mathbb{Z}=S(a)
. The result follows as
the largest element of
S(a)
is
|a|
.

(vi)

\Rightarrow
. If
\gcd(a,b)=|a|
then
|a||a
and
|a||b
.
For the latter there exist
q
such that
b=q|a|
. If
a
is non-negative,
the
b=qa
as well. If
a
is negative
b=q(-a)=(-q)a
.
The former concludes
a|b
and so does the latter.

\Leftarrow
. If
a|b
then
-a|b
and thus
|a||b
.
Since trivially
a|a
we conclude that

-a|a
and thus
|a||a
.
Then
|a||\gcd(a,b)
.
This by Theorem
1.1
(v) implies
|a|\leq\gcd(a,b)

(the gcd is always positive thus no absolute value sign around it is needed).
By definition
\gcd(a,b)|a
and thus
\gcd(a,b)|-a
.
Thus
|\gcd(a,b)|\leq|a|
.
Combining
|\gcd(a,b)|\leq|a|
and
|a|\leq\gcd(a,b)
the result follows.
∎

Theorem 1.8

(GCD divided)

If
d=\gcd(a,b)
then
\gcd(\frac{a}{d},\frac{b}{d})=1
.

Proof.

If
d=\gcd(a,b)
then by Theorem
1.10
we have that
d=r_{k}
.
By the last equation of
\gcd
’s we have
\gcd(r_{k-1},r_{k})=r_{k}=d
.
Given that
d
divides itself (
r_{k}=d
) we also have that
d|r_{k-1}
.
From the prior expression, now that we have
d|r_{k}
and
d|r_{k-1}

using
\gcd(r_{k-2},r_{k-1})=\gcd(r_{k-1},r_{k})=r_{k}=d
we have that

d|r_{k-2}
as well. Continuing likewise we have
d|r_{2}
and

d|r_{1}
.
Likewise from the third equation of Theorem
1.10
we have
d|r

and from the second
d|b
.
Concluding from the first (division) equation we have

d|a
. We can thus divide by
d
all equations. This shows that

\gcd(\frac{a}{d},\frac{b}{d})=\frac{d}{d}=1
as all
remainders will be divided by
d=r_{k}
as well.
∎

Theorem 1.9

If
x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
, then

\gcd(y,x)=\gcd(x,y)=\gcd(x,xq+y)

for all integers
q\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Let
d=\gcd(x,y)
.
Let
d_{1}=\gcd(x,xq+y)
.
If
d|x
and
d|y
by divisilibity
we have
x=dx_{1}
and thus
xq=dx_{1}q
and then
we have
d|xq
and
d|y
. Thus
d|xq+y

in addition to
d|x
.
Thus
d
is a common divisor of
xq+y
and
x
.
Thus
d\leq d_{1}=\gcd(x,xq+y)
.
For
d_{1}=\gcd(x,xq+y)
we have
d_{1}|x
and
d_{1}|xq+y
.
From the former, we conclude that
d_{1}|xq
; combining
it with the latter we have
d_{1}|xq+y-xq
i.e.

d_{1}|y
. Thus
d_{1}|x
and
d_{1}|y
and
therefore
d_{1}
is a common
divisor of
x,y
. Thus
d_{1}\leq d=\gcd(x,y)
.
By way of
d_{1}\leq d
and
d\leq d_{1}
we conclude
d=d_{1}
.
∎

From Theorem
1.5
in order to compute the

\gcd(a,b)
we formulate the division operation.

a=bq+r

where
0\leq r<|a|
.
Then by way of Theorem
1.9

\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(b,a)=\gcd(b,bq+r)\overset{Th.~\ref{t17}}{=}\gcd(b,r).

For calculating the
\gcd(a,b)
at a minimum
|a|+|b|\neq 0
that is,
we can’t determine the gcd of two numbers that are both 0.
The set of common divisors is
then
\mathbb{Z}
and has no maximum.
If
b=0
then
\gcd(a,b)=a
.
If
a=b
then
\gcd(a,b)=a
.
Thus in general we need to
compute
\gcd(a,b)
for
a>b
or
b>a
.
If
b<a
then we rename (swap)
a
and
b
so
that they become
a>b
.
We may assume without loss of generality that
a>b
.

1.5.1
The gcd algorithm by Euclid

Theorem 1.10

(GCD: Euclid’s algorithm)

For
a>b
,
b\neq 0

the
\gcd(a,b)
can be calculated using
iterated division, as follows.

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle=bq+r

\displaystyle 0\leq r<|b|

\displaystyle\gcd(a,b)

\displaystyle=\gcd(b,r)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=rq_{1}+r_{1}

\displaystyle 0\leq r_{1}<r

\displaystyle\gcd(b,r)

\displaystyle=\gcd(r,r_{1})

\displaystyle r

\displaystyle=r_{1}q_{2}+r_{2}

\displaystyle 0\leq r_{2}<r_{1}

\displaystyle\gcd(r,r_{1})

\displaystyle=\gcd(r_{1},r_{2})

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle r_{k-2}

\displaystyle=r_{k-1}q_{k}+r_{k}

\displaystyle 0\leq r_{k}<r_{k-1}

\displaystyle\gcd(r_{k-2},r_{k-1})

\displaystyle=\gcd(r_{k-1},r_{k})

\displaystyle r_{k-1}

\displaystyle=r_{k}q_{k+1}+0

\displaystyle\gcd(r_{k-1},r_{k})

\displaystyle=\gcd(r_{k},0)=r_{k}

The
last non-zero remainder
is the gcd:

\gcd(a,b)=r_{k}.

Moreover, for
a>b
the sequence of
steps is finite as the sequence of remainders is a
decreasing sequence of positive numbers eventually
reaching zero since

|b|>r>r_{1}>r_{2}>\ldots>r_{k-1}>r_{k}>0
.

Proof.

Finite number of steps.

The sequence of remainders
is a decreasing sequence starting below
|b|
. Thus after
no more that
|b|
steps it will reach a remainder of
0
.

Special and trivial cases.

If the numbers are negative we consider their absolute values
and continue.
If
a=b=0
, there is no
\gcd(a,b)
as
S(0)=\mathbb{Z}
.
If
a>b=0
, then
\gcd(a,b)=a
.
If
b>a=0
, then
\gcd(a,b)=b
.
We deal with some more trivial cases next.
Otherwise, if
a=b
then
\gcd(a,b)=a=b
.

Bottom-to-top proof.

Starting from the bottom
r_{k}|r_{k-1}
.
Then from the penultimate equation we have
r_{k}
dividing
both itself and
r_{k-1}
and thus
r_{k}|r_{k-1}q_{k}+r_{k}
.
Thus
r_{k}|r_{k-2}
. Working likewise we
show that
r_{k}
divides
r
and
b
of the first equation
and thus also divides
a
. Thus
r_{k}
divides both
a,b
.
Therefore
r_{k}\leq\gcd(a,b)
.
Pick an arbitrary integer
d
dividing
a
and
b
.
Working downwards we show that
d
divides
r_{k}
as well.
Thus
d\leq r_{k}
. Set
d=\gcd(a,b)
. This translates
into
\gcd(a,b)\leq r_{k}
; combined with
r_{k}\leq\gcd(a,b)

leads to
r_{k}=\gcd(a,b)
, as needed.

Top-to-bottom proof: start with
a>b>0
.

If
b>a>0
, we swap
a
and
b
.
Thus for all remaining cases we assume that
a>b>0
in the remainder.

Therefore, the algorithm computes
\gcd(a,b)

for
a>b
and
b\neq 0
. Furthermore,
|a|+|b|\neq 0
.
It is obvious from the statement that
by using Theorem
1.9
the following apply.

\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(b,r)=\gcd(r,r_{1})=\gcd(r_{1},r_{2})=\ldots=\gcd(r_{i-1},r_{i})=\ldots=\gcd(r_{k-1},r_{k})=\gcd(r_{k},0)=r_{k}.

That is, the last, non-zero remainder, is the gcd of
a,b
.
Moreover the sequence of remainders purely decreases i.e.

|b|>r>r_{1}>r_{2}>\ldots>r_{k-1}>r_{k}
.
Thus after a finite number of no more than
b
steps

r_{k}
will be determined.

We use Theorem
1.5
repeteadly starting with

x=b
and
y=a
.

\gcd(x,y)=\gcd(y,x)\Rightarrow\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(b,a)\Rightarrow\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(b,bq+r),

and continuing with
x=b
and
y=r
and proceeding
similarly.

\gcd(x,y)=\gcd(x,xq+y)\Rightarrow\gcd(b,r)=\gcd(b,bq+r)\Rightarrow

It is obvious from the statement of the theorem and the
previous derivations that

\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(b,r)=\gcd(r,r_{1})=\gcd(r_{1},r_{2})=\ldots=\gcd(r_{i-1},r_{i})=\ldots=\gcd(r_{k-1},r_{k})=\gcd(r_{k},0)=r_{k}

That is, the last, non-zero remainder, is the gcd.
Moreover the sequence of remainders purely decreases

|b|>r>r_{1}>r_{2}>\ldots>r_{k-1}>r_{k}
.
Thus after a finite number of no more than
|b|
steps

r_{k}
will be determined.
∎

For the golden ration
\phi
we have that

\phi\approx 1.6183
, and
\lg{\phi}\approx 0.6942
and

1/\lg{\phi}\approx 1.44
.

Lemma 1.4

(a) In Euclid’s algorithm the maximum number of division
steps
k+2
is for
a,b
that are Fibonacci numbers.

(b) For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
a>b>0

the number of divisions in Euclid’s Algorithm is
at most
\lg{b}/\lg{\phi}+2\approx 1.44\lg{b}+2
,
where
\phi=(1+\sqrt{5})/2
is the golden ratio.

Proof.

The worst-case number of division steps is for two
consecutive Fibonacci numbers, say
a=F_{n}
and
b=F_{n-1}
,
where
F_{0}=0
and
F_{1}=1
.
Then the first division is,

F_{n}=F_{n-1}+F_{n-2}
,
n>1
,
and the last division

F_{2}=F_{1}+F_{0}
, where
F_{0}=0
and
F_{1}=1
.
All quotients generated are one, the smallest possible
quotient.
The total number of divisions is
n-1
.
A bound would be provided for
n-1
with respect to
b
.

Note that
F_{n}\geq\phi^{n-2}
for all
n\geq 2
.
This follows from
\phi^{2}=\phi+1
and induction on
n
.
Clearly
F_{2}=1\geq\phi^{0}
. Moreover

F_{n}=F_{n-1}+F_{n-2}\geq\phi^{n-3}+\phi^{n-4}\geq(1+\phi)\phi^{n-4}\geq\phi^{2}\phi^{n-4}\geq\phi^{n-2}.

If the first division involves
a>b
, this means

b\geq F_{n-1}\geq\phi^{n-3}\Leftrightarrow n-1\leq\lg{b}/\lg{\phi}+2.

Then the number of division steps
n-1
is upper bounded
by
1.44\lg{b}+2
.
Of course the last division step is trivial, so the
bound is in fact at most

1.44\lg{b}+1
.
∎

The worst-case (i.e. longest) division is for
two consecutive Fibonacci numbers

F_{n}=F_{n-1}+F_{n-2}
,
n>1
, with
F_{0}=0

and
F_{1}=1
generate a quotient
of 1 every iteration! For
F_{n}
the number
of divisions is
n-2
as the last
division is
F_{2}=F_{1}+F_{0}=F_{1}+0
. The

n
-th Fibonacci number exceeds

\phi^{n-2}
.

Corollary 1.1

For
a,b<N
the number of divisions in Euclid’s Algorithm
will
be less than
\lg{N}/\lg{\phi}\approx 1.48\lg{N}
.

Proof.

We can do a bit better by bounding the number of divisions in terms of

b
only.
In the worst case, for an
n
step division
a\geq F_{n+2}
and

b\geq F_{n+1}
. But
F_{n+1}\geq\phi^{n-1}
. Thus

n-1\leq\lg{b}/\lg{\phi}
.
\lg{\phi}\approx 0.687
and thus

n-1\leq 1.471\lg{b}
i.e.
n\leq 1.471\lg{N}+1
.
∎

1.6
Extended GCD

Theorem 1.11

(Extended-GCD)

Bézout identity.

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
|a|+|b|\neq 0
.
Let
d=\gcd(a,b)
then there exist integers
x,y
such that

d=ax+by

(1.6)

Moreover,
d
is the smallest positive integer that can
be written as a linear combination of
a,b
.

Proof.

Consider

A=\{au+bv|u,v\in\mathbb{Z}\ \wedge\ au+bv>0\}.

Note that for
u=a
,
v=b
,
a\cdot a+b\cdot b>0

since
|a|+|b|\neq 0
. Set
A
has thus at least one
element and it is not empty. Thus from the well-ordered
set principle there must exist a minimum element for
A
and
let it be
d^{\prime}
. Since
d^{\prime}\in A
there exist

x,y
such that

ax+by=d^{\prime}.

A. Show
d^{\prime}
is a divisor of
a
.
We first show that
d^{\prime}|a
. Let
us form the division operation for the two integers i.e.

a=d^{\prime}q^{\prime}+r^{\prime}

where
0\leq r^{\prime}<d^{\prime}
. Then we have that

r^{\prime}=a-d^{\prime}q^{\prime}=a-(ax+by)q^{\prime}=a\cdot(1-xq^{\prime})+b\cdot(-yq^{\prime}).

Note that because of the division of
a
by
d^{\prime}
we
know that
0\leq r^{p}rime
.
We examine two cases.

Case 1:
0<r^{\prime}
.

If
r^{\prime}>0
then it is a
member of
A
since

r^{\prime}=a\cdot(1-xq^{\prime})+b\cdot(-yq^{\prime})
.
But this cannot happen since
r^{\prime}<d^{\prime}
and

d^{\prime}
is the minimum element of
A
.
It would imply the existence of an element of
A
(i.e.
r^{\prime}
)
smaller than the minimum element of
A
(i.e.
d^{\prime}
)!

Case 2:
0=r^{\prime}
. The only other possibility is that

r^{\prime}=0
. But then,
a=d^{\prime}q^{\prime}+r^{\prime}

implies
a=d^{\prime}q^{\prime}
i.e.

d^{\prime}
is a divisor of
a
.

B. Show
d^{\prime}
is a divisor of
b
. Similar to
the case involving
a
.

Conclusion.

Thus
d^{\prime}
divides
a
and
b
and is a common
divisor of
a,b
. It should be that
d^{\prime}\leq d

since
d
is the gcd of
a
and
b

and is the greatest common divisor of
a,b
.
Furthermore,
d|a
and
d|b
and therefore

d|ax+by
and thus
d|d^{\prime}
. This
implies
d\leq d^{\prime}

This
d\leq d^{\prime}
along with the
d^{\prime}\leq d
shows
that
d=d^{\prime}
.
In conclusion

d=\gcd(a,b)=d^{\prime}=ax+by.

∎

1.6.1
Some corollaries

Corollary 1.2

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
|a|+|b|\neq 0
.
Let
d=\gcd(a,b)
.
Show that set
A
defined as follows

A=\{au+bv|u,v\in\mathbb{Z}\ \wedge\ au+bv>0\},

contains multiples of
d
.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.11
,
the smallest positive element of
A

was proven to be
d
.
Moreover every element
z
of
A
is of the form
z=au+bv
.
Since
d|a
and
d|b
we have that
d|au+bv

that is
d|z
. Thus every element of
A
is a multiple of
d
.
∎

Corollary 1.3

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
|a|+|b|\neq 0
.
Let
a,b
be such that
\gcd(a,b)=1
. Then there exist

x,y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

ax+by=1.

Proof.

From Theorem
1.11
and Corollary
1.2

we know that there exist
x,y

such that
ax+by=\gcd(a,b)
. Since
a,b
are such that

\gcd(a,b)=1
, we obtain
ax+by=1
, as needed.
∎

Corollary 1.4

Let
a|bc
and
\gcd(a,b)=1
. Then

a|c
.

Proof.

From the previous problem we have that there exist

x,y
such that
ax+by=1
. Then,

acx+bcy=c.

Since we are given
a|bc
we conclude
a|bcy
.
Moreover,
a|acx
. Then

a|acx+bcy=a|c
, as needed.
∎

Corollary 1.5

If
d=\gcd(a,b)
then
S(a)\cap S(b)=S(d)
.
(In other words,
m|a\mathrm{\;and\;}m|b\iff m|d
.)

Proof.

Every common divisor of
a
and
b
is a divisor of
d=\gcd(a,b)
since

d=d^{\prime}=ax+by
derived and used in Theorem
1.11
.
That is
S(a)\cap S(b)\subseteq S(d)
.

Moreover for
t\in S(d)
then
t|d
and since
d|a
and

d|b
by transitivity we have
t|a
and
t|b
.
The former show that
t\in S(a)
and the latter that
t\in S(b)
.
Both of them show that
t\in S(a)\cap S(b)
.
Thus
S(d)\subseteq S(a)\cap S(b)
.
∎

Corollary 1.6

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
|a|+|b|\neq 0
.
Let
m\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}^{*}
.
Then

\gcd(ma,mb)=m\gcd(a,b).

Proof.

Let
d=\gcd(a,b)
. Since
d|a
we have
a=dq
and thus

ma=(md)q
. Thus
md|ma
. Likewise
md|mb
.
Thus
md\leq\gcd(mb,ma)
.

Since
m|ma
and
m|mb
we have
m|\gcd(ma,mb)
.
Thus
\gcd(ma,mb)=mq
for some
q
.
Moreover
\gcd{ma,mb}|ma
implies
mq|ma
i.e
q|a
.
Likewise
q|b
. Thus
q|d
i.e.
q\leq d
.
We conclude that
\gcd(ma,mb)=mq\leq md
.
From
md\leq\gcd(mb,ma)
previously and

\gcd(ma,mb)=mq\leq md
the corollary follows.
∎

Example 1.6.1

Calculate the
\gcd(30,105)
.

Solution.

We note that
30<105
so swapping takes place

\begin{array}[]{lll}&\gcd(30,105)=&:\gcd(105,30)=\\
&105=30\cdot 3+15&:\gcd(30,15)=\\
&30=15\cdot 2+0&:\gcd(15,0)=\\
&&:15\end{array}

Therefore
\gcd(30,105)=15
.

∎

Example 1.6.2

For
a=105
and
b=30
determine
x,y
such that

ax+by=\gcd(a,b).

Solution.

We observe that
\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(280,151)=1
.
We use Euclid’s algorithm for the gcd as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lll}105&=&30\cdot 3+15\\
30&=&15\cdot 2+0\\
\end{array}

The first non-zero remainder is
15
.
Thus
\gcd(105,30)=15
.
We then use the extended GCD to find
x,y
as follows.

\begin{array}[]{lll}15&=&105-30\cdot 3\\
\end{array}

We work from the last equation upwards, one step and one level
at a time.

\displaystyle 15

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 105-30\cdot 3

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 105\cdot 1+30\cdot(-3).

Therefore
x=1
and
y=-3
.
∎

1.6.2
More gcd results

Corollary 1.7

For three
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
\gcd(a,b,c)=\gcd((a,b),c)=\gcd(d,c)
, where
d=\gcd(a,b)
.

Proof.

\gcd(a,b,c)
belongs to
S(a)\cap S(b)\cap S(c)=\left(S(a)\cap S(b)\right)\cap S(c)
. The result
follows.
∎

Corollary 1.8

For
p
a prime, and
a\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
,
p\nmid a

if and only if
\gcd(a,p)=1
.

Proof.

If
p
is a prime
S(p)=\{-1,+1,-p,+p\}
,
then
p
does not divide
a
means that neither
p
nor
-p
are
divisors of
a
. The only possible divisors are +1 and -1.
Thus
S(a)\cap S(p)=\{+1,-1\}
. Thus the gcd is 1, and
thus
\gcd(a,p)=1
as needed.

If
\gcd(a,p)=1
, then
p
cannot divide
a
. This is because if

p|a
since obviously
p|p
we would have from a prior
property that
p|\gcd(a,p)
and (in fact
\gcd(a,p)=|p|
).
For this to happen
p
should be
-1
or
+1
. But
p
is prime
and this can’t happen.
∎

Corollary 1.9

For
a,b,m\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and
\gcd(a,b)=1
and
a|bm
then

a|m
.

Proof.

Since
\gcd(a,b)=1
we have
1=ax+by
for some
x,y
.
Multiplying by
m
we get
m=axm+bmy
. Obviously

a|axm
. Since
a|m
we have
a|bmy
as well.
Thus
a|axm+bmy
i.e.
a|m
.
∎

Corollary 1.10

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
and prime
p
is such that
p|ab
,
then
p
divides either
a
or
b
.

Proof.

Say
p
does not divide
a
. By a prior theorem since
p
is
prime this means
\gcd(p,a)=\gcd(a,p)=1
. Since
p|ab

by the previous theorem we have
p|b
.
∎

Corollary 1.11

If
\gcd(a,b)=1
and
a|c
and
b|c
then
ab|c
.

Proof.

If
\gcd(a,b)=1
we have
1=ax+by
. Then
c=acx+bcy
.
We have
a|c
i.e.
c=aq
. Then
cb=abq
and thus
bcy=ab(qy)
.
The latter implies
ab|bcy
.
We also have
b|c
i.e.
c=br
. Then
ac=abr
. Thus
acx=ab(rx)
.
The latter implies
ab|acx
.
Thus
ab|bcy
and
ab|acx
imply
ab|acx+bcy
.
Therefore
ab|c
.
∎

1.6.3
Relatively prime integers

Definition 1.14

For two integers
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
if
\gcd(a,b)=1
the
two integers
a
and
b
are called
relatively prime
.

1.6.4
Extended GCD calculation

Lemma 1.5

Let
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
b\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

a>b
,
b\neq 0
.

Then algorithm ExtendedGCD(a,b) returns
(x,y) such that

ax+by=\gcd(a,b)=d.

Furthermore show that
if
d=1
and
b>0
then

y\bmod a
and

x\bmod b
are the inverses
of

b\bmod a
and

a\bmod b
respectively.

Proof.

Input:

a,b
, where
a\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
b\in\mathbb{Z}
,
a>b
,
b\neq 0

Output:

(x,y,d)

(x,y,d,k,l,m)=(1,0,a,0,1,b)
;

1

while

m>0

do

2

t=d/m
;

3

(x,y,d,k,l,m)=(k,l,m,x-t\cdot k,y-t\cdot l,d-t\cdot m)
;

4

end while

5

return

(x,y,d)
;

Algorithm 1

Extendedgcd(a,b)

Details Omitted.
∎

Example 1.6.3

Trace Extendedgcd(a,b) for
a=280
and
b=105
.

Solution.

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\gcd(280,105)&&\\
(x,y,d,k,l,m)&=&(1,0,280,0,1,105)&,&t=280/105=2.\\
(x,y,d,k,l,m)&=&(0,1,105,1-2*0,0-2*1,280-2*105)&,&\\
&=&(0,1,105,1,-2,70)&.&\\
(x,y,d,k,l,m)&=&(0,1,105,1,-2,70)&,&t=105/70=1.\\
(x,y,d,k,l,m)&=&(1,-2,70,0-1*1,1-1*(-2),105-1*70)&,&\\
&=&(1,-2,70,-1,3,35)&.&\\
(x,y,d,k,l,m)&=&(1,-2,70,-1,3,35)&,&t=70/35=2.\\
(x,y,d,k,l,m)&=&(-1,3,35,1-2*(-1),-2-2*(3),70-2*35)&,&\\
&=&(-1,3,35,3,-8,0)&.&\\
(x,y,d)&=&(-1,3,35)&.&\\
\end{array}

∎

1.7
Least common multiples

Definition 1.15
(Least common multiple :lcm)

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
we denote by
\lcm(a,b)
the

least common multiple
of
a
and
b
,
the least positive integer divisible by both
a,b
.

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
only
the positive multiples are then considered.

Proposition 1.1

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
are such that
|a|+|b|\neq 0
,
and let
n=\lcm(a,b)
.
If
m
is a common multiple of
a,b
show
that
n|m
.

This says that any common multiple of
a,b

is a multiple of the least common multiple (of
a,b
).

If
T(a)
is the set of (positive) multiples of
a
,
then
T(a)=T(-a)=T(|a|)
.
Moreover
T(a)\cap T(b)=T(|a|)\cap T(|b|)
.
Therefore
\lcm(a,b)=\lcm(|a|,|b|)
.

Proof.

If one of
a,b
is equal to zero, then all multiples
of zero is zero, common muliples can only be zero
and
the statement is true trivially.

Otherwise divide
m
by
n
.

m=nq+r,0\leq r<n.

We are given that
m
is a common multiple of
a,b

that is
a|m
and
b|m
.
Moreover
n
is the lcm of
a,b

that is
a|n
and
b|n
.
Therefore
a|r
and
b|r
.
Thus
r
is a common multiple of
a,b
.
Moreover
r<n
and the common multiple
r
is positive
and LESS than the least common multiple
a,b
,
a contradiction unless
r=0
.
Then
m=nq
and thus
n|m
.
∎

Moreover
\lcm(ma,mb)=m\lcm(a,b)
.

Proposition 1.2

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}
we have

\lcm(am,bm)=m\lcm(a,b)
.

Proof.

(a)
Let
X=\lcm(a,b)
and
Y=\lcm(am,bm)
. We are to show
that
Y=Xm
. It suffices to show
Y\geq Xm
and
Y\leq Xm
.

Since
X=\lcm(a,b)
then
a/X
and
b/X
.
Therefore
X=ad_{1}
and
X=bd_{2}
for some
d_{1},d_{2}
.
Consequently
Xm=mad_{1}
and
Xm=mbd_{2}
respectively.
Furthermore
ma|Xm
and
mb|Xm
. Thefore,
since
Y=\lcm(am,bm)
, we have
Xm\geq Y
.

Consider
Y=\lcm(am,bm)
. Them
m|Y
, and
therefore there exists a
y
such that

Y=my
.

Y=\lcm(am,bm)\Rightarrow m|Y\Rightarrow\exists y:Y=my.

Combining the latter with
Y=my
we obtain

Y=my,Y=amd_{3},Y=bmd_{4}\Rightarrow my=amd_{3},\quad my=bmd_{4}\Rightarrow y=ad_{3},\quad y=bd_{4}\Rightarrow a|y,\quad b|y,

\Rightarrow a|y,\quad b|y,\Rightarrow y\geq X\Rightarrow my\geq mX\Rightarrow Y\geq Xm.

From
Xm\geq Y
and
Y\geq mX
we conclude

Y=Xm
as needed.
∎

Theorem 1.12

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\gcd(a,b)=1
then
\lcm(a,b)=ab
.

Proof.

Let
m=\lcm(a,b)
. Since
\gcd(a,b)=1
we have
1=ax+by
.
Then
m=axm+bym
. Since
a|m
and
b|m
.
Then
m=ap
and
m=bq
. Moreover substituting to the previous
equation we have
m=ax(bq)+by(ap)=ab(xq)+ab(yp)=ab(xq+yp)
.
Thus
ab|m
. This implie
ab\leq m
.
Moreover
m
is the least common multiple of
a,b
.
One such multiple is
ab
. Thus
ab\geq m
.
The
ab\leq m
and the just shown
ab\geq m
implies

m=ab
.
∎

Example 1.7.1

Proof Theorem
1.12
using other arguments.

Solution.

Let
d=\gcd(a,b)
. Let
a_{1}=a/d
and
b_{1}=b/d
.
Then
1=d/d=\gcd(a/d,b/d)=\gcd(a_{1},b_{1})
.
From the previous problem, part (b), we have that
for
\gcd(a/d,b/d)=1
,

\frac{1}{d}\lcm(a,b)=\lcm(a_{1},b_{1})=\lcm(a/d,b/d)=ab/d^{2}=\frac{a\cdot b}{\gcd(a,b)}\Leftrightarrow\lcm(a,b)\cdot\gcd(a,b)=a\cdot b.

∎

Theorem 1.13

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
. Then
\gcd(a,b)\cdot\lcm(a,b)=ab
.

Proof.

Let
d=\gcd(a,b)
. Let
a_{1}=a/d
and
b_{1}=b/d
.
Then
\gcd(a/d,b/d)=\gcd(a_{1},b_{1})=1
.
From the previous theorem we have
\lcm(a_{1},b_{1})=\lcm(a/d,b/d)=ab/d^{2}
.
∎

If
T(a)
is the set of (positive) multiples of
a
, then

T(a)=T(-a)=T(|a|)
. Moreover
T(a)\cap T(b)=T(|a|)\cap T(|b|)
.
Therefore
\lcm(a,b)=\lcm(|a|,|b|)
.

Corollary 1.12

For
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
if
a|b
, then
\lcm(a,b)=b
.

Proof.

If
a|b
, then any element
t\in T(b)
is
t\geq b
.
Moreover
b
or
|b|
is in
T(a)
. The result follows.
∎

Theorem 1.14

(lcm of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
)

Let
a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Let

\displaystyle\lcm(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{1}

\displaystyle\lcm(r_{1},a_{3})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{2}

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ldots

(1.7)

\displaystyle\lcm(r_{n-3},a_{n-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{n-2}

\displaystyle\lcm(r_{n-2},a_{n})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle r_{n-1}

Then show that

\lcm(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})=r_{n-1}.

(1.8)

Proof.

We first work top to bottom.
It is the case that

r_{1}|r_{2}
,

r_{2}|r_{3}
,

\ldots
,

r_{n-1}|r_{n}
.
It is clear that
a_{1}|r_{1}
and since

r_{1}|r_{2}
we have
a_{1}|r_{2}
.
Proceeding similarly we have

a_{1}|r_{i}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n-1
.
Likewise we can show
a_{i}|r_{n-1}
,
and thus
r_{n-1}
is a common multiple of

a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
and thus by the previous
problem

\lcm(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})
divides

r_{n-1}
or equivalently,

\lcm(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})\leq r_{n-1}.

Let
c
be a common multiple of
a_{1},\ldots a_{n}
.
Then
a_{i}|c
for all
i
.
In particular
a_{1}|c
and
a_{2}|c
.
Thus
c
is a common multiple of
a_{1},a_{2}
.
From the previous problem this implies
that
\lcm(a_{1},a_{2})
which is
r_{1}
divides

c
. Thus
r_{1}|c
.
From the following equation we have

r_{1}|c
just established and
a_{3}|c

since
c
is a common multiple of all
a_{i}
.
With a similar argument as before we conclude

r_{2}|c
.
By induction we have
r_{i}|c
including

r_{n-1}|c
, which generates a

r_{n-1}\leq c.

Combining the two inequalities derived we have

\lcm(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})\leq r_{n-1}\leq c,

for every common multiple
c
of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
,
and
r_{n-1}
is one such common multiple.
The only possibility is that

r_{n-1}=\lcm(a_{1},a_{2},\ldots,a_{n})
,
as needed,
that is there is no common multiple smaller than

r_{n-1}
because
r_{n-1}
is the least common
multiples of
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
.
∎

1.8
Diophantine equations

Theorem 1.15

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
The linear Diophnatine equation

ax+by=c

(1.9)

has an integer solution
x,y
if and only if

d|c
, where
d=\gcd(a,b)
.
If a particular solution
(x_{0},y_{0})
exists, then there
are infinitely
many solution of the form

x=x_{0}+\frac{mb}{d},\quad\quad y=y_{0}-\frac{ma}{d},

where
m\in mb{Z}
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
. Let
x,y
be a solution for
ax+by=c
.
Then since
d=\gcd(a,b)
we have
d|a
and
d|b

and thus
d|ax+by
implying
d|c
. If
d\nmid c

obviously there are no
x,y
.

\Leftarrow
.
Let
d|c
. By way of Theorem
1.11

of the extended GCD algorithm or the definition of
\gcd(a,b)

there exist
x,y
such that

ax+by=d

(1.10)

where
d=\gcd(a,b)
. Since
d|c
there exists a
q
such that

c=dq
. Multiplying Equation
1.10
by
q
we get

a(xq)+b(yq)=dq=c

(1.11)

Thus a solution has been found through Theorem
1.11
.
The solution is
x_{0}=xq
and
y_{0}=yq
.
Let
(x_{0},y_{0})
be a solution pair. Suppose there is another
solution pair
(x,y)
. Then

ax_{0}+by_{0}=c\quad\quad,\quad\quad ax+by=c

(1.12)

Subtracting one from the other we get

a(x_{0}-x)+b(y_{0}-y)=0\Rightarrow a(x_{0}-x)=b(y-y_{0}).

(1.13)

For
d=\gcd(a,b)
,
a_{1}=a/d
and
b_{1}=b/d
are both integer.
Moreover
\gcd(a_{1},b_{1})=1
from a prior result.
Dividing by
d
equation
1.13
we have.

\frac{a}{d}(x_{0}-x)=\frac{b}{d}(y-y_{0})\Rightarrow a_{1}(x_{0}-x)=b_{1}(y-y_{0})

(1.14)

Since
\gcd(a_{1},b_{1})=1
, since
a_{1}|a_{1}(x_{0}-x)
it
means
a_{1}|b_{1}(y-y_{0})
. Being relatively prime
a_{1},b_{1}
this
is equivalent to
a_{1}|y-y_{0}
. Likewise
b_{1}|x-x_{0}
.
From the latter we obtain that there exists,
m
such that

\displaystyle b_{1}|x-x_{0}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle x-x_{0}=mb_{1}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle x=x_{0}+m\frac{b}{d}

From Eq.(
1.13
) and
x-x_{0}=mb_{1}
we obtain the following

\displaystyle x-x_{0}=mb_{1},\quad a_{1}(x_{0}-x)=b_{1}(y-y_{0})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle-ma_{1}b_{1}=b_{1}(y-y_{0})

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle y=y_{0}-m\frac{a}{d}.

The result thus follows.
∎

Corollary 1.13

If
\gcd(a,b,c)=1
and
\gcd(a,b)=d>1
then Equation
1.9
has
no solution.

Proof.

Say that Equation
1.9
has an integer solution
(x,y)
i.e.

ax+by=c
. Since
d=\gcd(a,b)
it means
d|a
and
d|b
.
Then
d|ax+by
i.e.
d|c
. If
d
is a common divisor of

a,b,c
it means
d|1
. Then it can only be
d=1
. This contradicts
the fact
d>1
. Thus Equation
1.9
cannot have any solution.
∎

Corollary 1.14

If
\gcd(a,b)=1
then Equation
1.9
has one solution.

Corollary 1.15

Let
a,b\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
.
The linear Diophantine equation

ax+by=c

(1.15)

has an integer solution
x,y
if and only
if
d|c
, where
d=\gcd(a,b)
.
If a particular solution
(x_{1},y_{1})
exists for
ax+by=d
,
many solutions of the form

x=\frac{c}{d}x_{1}+m\cdot\frac{b}{d},\quad\quad y=\frac{c}{d}y_{1}-m\cdot\frac{a}{d},

exist, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Proof.

Using the extended GCD algorithm,
by way of Eq.(
1.11
),
x_{1},y_{1}
are solutions
of
ax+by=\gcd(a,b)
. If
d|c
then there exists
a
q\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
c=dq
.
Multiplying Eq.(
1.11
) by
q
we derive
Eq.(
1.12
) and thus
x_{0}=x_{1}q
and
y_{0}=y_{1}q
are
solutions of Eq.(
1.12
).
Therefore a solution
(x_{1},y_{1})
of Eq.(
1.11
)
gives rise to a solution

x_{0}=x_{1}q=\frac{c}{d}x_{1},\quad y_{0}=y_{1}q=\frac{c}{d}y_{1}

of Eq.(
1.12
), and using the previous
problem many solutions of the form

x=x_{0}+m\cdot\frac{b}{d}=\frac{c}{d}x_{1}+m\cdot\frac{b}{d},\quad\quad y=y_{0}-m\cdot\frac{a}{d}=\frac{c}{d}y_{1}-m\cdot\frac{a}{d},

exist, where
m\in\mathbb{Z}
.
∎

Corollary 1.16

If
\gcd(a,b,c)=1
and
\gcd(a,b)=d>1

then Equation
1.9
has no solution.

Proof.

Say that Equation
1.9
has an integer solution
(x,y)
i.e.

ax+by=c
. Since
d=\gcd(a,b)
it means
d|a
and
d|b
.
Then
d|ax+by
i.e.
d|c
. If
d
is a common divisor of

a,b,c
it means
d|1
. Then it can only be
d=1
. This contradicts
the fact
d>1
. Thus Equation
1.9
cannot have any solution.
If
\gcd(a,b)=1
then Equation
1.9
has one solution.
∎

Example 1.8.1

Show
\gcd(1024,640)=128
we have

Proof.

\displaystyle 1024

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 640\cdot 1+384

\displaystyle 640

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 384\cdot 1+256

\displaystyle 384

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 256\cdot 1+128

\displaystyle 256

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 128\cdot 2+0

Obviously
\gcd(1024,640)=128
, the last non zero remainder.
Reversing the order of the equation we have.

\displaystyle 128

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 384+256\cdot(-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 384+(640+384\cdot(-1))\cdot(-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 640\cdot(-1)+384\cdot(2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 640\cdot(-1)+(1024+640\cdot(-1))\cdot 2

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 640\cdot(-3)+1024\cdot 2

Therefore
\gcd(1024,640)=128=1024\cdot 2+640\cdot(-3)
.
∎

Example 1.8.2

Find the solutions, if any, of
1024x+640y=256
.

Proof.

By the previous example
\gcd(1024,640)=d=128
. It is
128|256
.
Thus one solution of the Diophantine is

x_{0}=2\cdot(256/128)=4
and
y_{0}=(-3)\cdot(256/128)=-6
.

Other solutions are

x=4+m(640/128)=4+5m\quad,\quad y=-6-8m

A simple calculation confirms the latter solutions

1024(4+5m)+640(-6-8m)=256.

∎

The previous method outlined in Corollary
1.14
is tedious.
A better approach for solving
ax+by=d
, where
d=\gcd(a,b)
starts with.

\left[\begin{array}[]{c|cc}a&1&0\\
b&0&1\\
\end{array}\right]

The first column are the the Dividend (
a
) and Divisor (
b
) of the
division operation. Eventually through repeated division with remainder
(the previous vertical operations become horizontal) will generate a
matrix such as the one below. Its first row entries contains
the
\gcd(a,b)
and
x,y
.

\left[\begin{array}[]{c|cc}\gcd(a,b)&x&y\\
0&?&?\\
\end{array}\right]

Example 1.8.3

Show this for
a=1024,b=640
and division

\displaystyle 1024

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 640\cdot 1+384

\displaystyle 640

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 384\cdot 1+256

\displaystyle 384

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 256\cdot 1+128

\displaystyle 256

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 128\cdot 2+0

Proof.

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r|rr}1024&1&0\\
640&0&1\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r|rr}384&1&-1\\
640&0&1\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r|rr}384&1&-1\\
256&-1&2\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r|rr}128&2&-3\\
256&-1&2\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\rightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r|rr}128&2&-3\\
0&-5&-8\\
\end{array}\right]

In the first transition, we subtract the second row from the first per
the first division.
In the second transition, we subtract the first row from the second per
the second division.
In the third transition, we subtract the second row from the first per
the third division.
In the fourth transition, we subtract twice the first row from the second per
the fourth division. (It is twice because the quotient in this case is a 2.)

∎

There is yet another matrix form representation of the Extended-Euclid’s
algorithms (i.e. Extended GCD). For this in Theorem
1.10
we rewrite
the first line as in
a=bq+r=bq_{0}+r_{0}
,
and the second line
b=rq_{1}+r_{1}=r_{0}q_{1}+r_{1}
.
The remaining lines remain the same.
The product of
k+2
matrices can be computed as a

2\times 2
matrix with entries
x_{1},\ldots x_{4}
.

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r}a\\
b\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{0}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}b\\
r_{0}\\
\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{0}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{1}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}r_{0}\\
r_{1}\\
\end{array}\right]=\ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{0}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{1}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\ldots\times\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{k+1}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}r_{k}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=0}^{i=k+1}\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}q_{i}&1\\
1&0\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}r_{k}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{3}&x_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}r_{k}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r}r_{k}\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}x_{1}&x_{2}\\
x_{3}&x_{4}\\
\end{array}\right]^{-1}\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}a\\
b\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{r}\gcd(a,b)\\
0\\
\end{array}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left[\begin{array}[]{rr}x&y\\
&*\\
\end{array}\right]\times\left[\begin{array}[]{r}a\\
b\\
\end{array}\right]\Rightarrow\gcd(a,b)=ax+by.

Theorem 1.16

For
a,p\in\mathbb{Z}^{*}
such that
\gcd(a,p)=1
, there is a unique

x
such that
aa^{\prime}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.

Proof.

Since
\gcd(a,p)=1
we have by Theorem
1.10
that
there exists
x,y
such that
ax+py=1=\gcd(a,p)
.
Furthermore
-py=ax-1
. Since
p|py
we have
p|ax-1
.
Thus
ax\equiv 1\pmod{p}
. Consider
a^{\prime}=x\pmod{p}
.
It is still
aa^{\prime}\equiv\pmod{p}
. Furthermore any divisor
of
a^{\prime}
and
m
must also divide
1
, i.e.
\gcd(a^{\prime},p)=1
.
∎

1.9
Fundamental theorem of arithmetic

1.9.1
Unique factorization

Theorem 1.17

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
with
n>1
.
Then
n
can be expressed as the product of prime
numbers (natural numbers with two divisors one, and itself).
The representation is unique up to a permutation of the
ordering of the primes.

We usually represent the prime numbers from the
smallest (left-most side) to the largest (right-most side).
If a prime number
p
appears
k
times we also write
p^{k}
.
If
n
itself is a prime number the expression
”product” is used degeneratively. The product of one prime is
the prime itself.

Proof.

We prove the result by induction:
n>1
becomes
n\geq 2
.

Base case
n=2
.
Integer
n=2
is the degenerate case
of
n=2
where there is only one prime factor of
n=2
and this
is itself.

Induction hypothesis:
H(m)
,
2\leq m<n
.

For
n\geq 2
we have that
for every integer
m
such that
2\leq m<n
, then
m

can be expressed as the products of primes as noted in the
statement of the problem.

Inductive step:
n
Consider integer
n
.
We distinguish two cases: (a)
n
is a prime number
and we are done, (b)
n
is not a prime number.
In the latter case there exists by Proposition
1.3
a
prime factor of
1<p<n
. Call then
P=n/p
. Then

1<P<n
. By the induction hypothesis
P
can be expressed
as a product of primes, and so does
n
, since
n=P\cdot p
,
where
P
is a product of primes then, and
p
is also a prime.

Uniqueness of the representation.

Let
p_{1}p_{2}\ldots p_{r}
is a representation and

q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{s}
is another representation of
n
.

n=p_{1}p_{2}\ldots p_{r-1}p_{r}=q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{s-1}q_{s},

and let

1<p_{1}\leq p_{2}\leq\ldots\leq p_{r}
,
and

1<q_{1}\leq q_{2}\leq\ldots\leq q_{r}
,
and furthermore
1\leq r\leq s
.
By using induction we will show that

r=s
and
p_{i}=q_{i}
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,r(=s)
.
Induction is on
r
.

Base case
r=1
.
We have

n=p_{1}=q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{s}.

On the left-hand side we have
p_{1}
a prime number.
If
s>1
on the right-hand side we have the product of
at least two primes. This is a contradiction. Therefore
s=1
.
Then
p_{1}=q_{1}
and we are done.

Induction hypothesis.

We assume that the result is true for
r-1
, where
r\geq 2
.
Consider the following.
Consider the following.

n=p_{1}p_{2}\ldots p_{r-1}p_{r}=q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{s-1}q_{s},

p_{r}
is a factor of
n
. It also thus divides the
q
product.
Since
p_{r}
is a prime number and all
q_{i}
are prime numbers
it can’t by that
p_{r}|q_{i}
and
p_{r}<q_{i}
because then

q_{i}
would have a prime factor and could not be a prime number
itself. Thus the only way
p_{r}|q_{i}
is for
p_{r}=q_{i}
.
In other words
p_{r}|q_{i}
implies
p_{r}\leq q_{i}
.
We might also work out similarly to show that
q_{s}\leq p_{j}
.
Then

q_{s}\leq p_{j}\leq p_{r}\leq q_{i}\leq q_{s}

implies
p_{r}=q_{s}
. Since both
p_{r}>1,q_{s}>1
we
divide the expression for
n
by
p_{r}=q_{s}
. We then
obtain

p_{1}p_{2}\ldots p_{r-1}=q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{s-1},

and by the induction hypothesis
r-1=s-1

and
p_{1}=q_{1}
,
\ldots
,
p_{r-1}=q_{s-1}
.
∎

1.9.2
Fundamental theorem

Lemma 1.6

For a prime number
p
if
p|p_{1}p_{2}
then

p|p_{1}
or
p|p_{2}
. This can be generalized
for
p|p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}\ldots
.

Proof.

If
p|p_{1}p_{2}
given that
p
is prime its only
positive divisors are
1,p
. If
p\nmid p1
, then

\gcd(p,p_{1})=1
. Thus from a prior result
p|p_{2}
.
By induction we can prove its generalization.
∎

Theorem 1.18

(Fundamental theorem of arithmetic)

If
n>1
there there exists unique prime numbers

p_{1}<\ldots<p_{k}
and natural integers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{k}>0

such that

n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}.

Proof.

If
n
is prime this is true easily.
Let
n
be a composite natural number. Then by Theorem
1.3

it has a prime factor and let its smallest one be
q_{1}
i.e.

n=q_{1}\cdot a_{1},\quad\mbox{\ with\ }a_{1}<n.

If
a_{1}
is prime we have decomposed
n
into two prime numbers.
If
a_{1}
is composite and by Theorem
1.3
it has
a prime factor and let its smallest one be
q_{2}
i.e.

a_{1}=q_{2}\cdot a_{2},\quad\mbox{\ with\ }a_{2}<a_{1}.

n=q_{1}a_{1}=q_{1}(q_{2}a_{2})=q_{1}q_{2}a_{2}.

If
a_{2}
is composite we repeat this until he hit a
a_{k-1}=q_{k}
.
That way

n=q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{k-1}q_{k}.

Let us assume that there is another factorization of
n

n=r_{1}r_{2}\ldots r_{t-1}r_{t}.

The we have

q_{1}q_{2}\ldots q_{k-1}q_{k}=r_{1}r_{2}\ldots r_{t-1}r_{t}.

r_{1}
divides the right hand side. It also divides the left-hand side.
By Lemma
1.6
one of
q_{i}
divided by
r_{1}
, i.e.
r_{1}|q_{i}
.
Because all of
r_{1},q_{i}
are prime numbers this can only mean

r_{1}=q_{i}
for some
i
. The smallest
r_{i}
is
r_{1}
.
The smallest
q_{i}
is
q_{1}
. Thus
r_{1}=q_{1}
.
Because primes are
\neq 0
, we can factor out
r_{1}
.

q_{2}\ldots q_{k-1}q_{k}=r_{2}\ldots r_{t-1}r_{t}.

Continuing likewise if without loss of generality
k<t
we
will eventually have

1=r_{k+1}\ldots r_{t-1}r_{t}.

Prime numbers are
>1
. Their product cannot be equal to
1
.
Thus this can only mean that
k=t
as well.
∎

1.9.3
Finding the gcd and lcm

Theorem 1.19

(GCD UF)

Let
a_{i},b_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
fo all
i
.
Moreover
a,b\geq 1
.
If

a=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}},\quad b=p_{1}^{b_{1}}p_{2}^{b_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{b_{k}},\quad a_{k},b_{k}\geq 0.

then in order to find
d=\gcd(a,b)
we have

d=\gcd(a,b)=p_{1}^{c_{1}}p_{2}^{c_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{c_{k}}=p_{1}^{\min(a_{1},b_{1})}p_{2}^{\min(a_{2},b_{2})}\ldots p_{k}^{\min(a_{k},b_{k})},

where
c_{i}=\min(a_{i},b_{i})\geq 0
,
and then in
order to find
m=\lcm(a,b)
we have

m=\lcm(a,b)=p_{1}^{d_{1}}p_{2}^{d_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{d_{k}}=p_{1}^{\max(a_{1},b_{1})}p_{2}^{\max(a_{2},b_{2})}\ldots p_{k}^{\max(a_{k},b_{k})},

where
d_{i}=\max(a_{i},b_{i})\geq 0
,

Proof.

We shall show that
D=p_{1}^{c_{1}}p_{2}^{c_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{c_{k}}
satisfy
the GCD properties. Because
c_{i}\leq a_{i}
for all
i
,
we conclude
D|a
. Because
c_{i}\leq b_{i}
for all
i
,
we conclude
D|b
.
Let
g
by any common divisor of
a
and
b
. If
g
divides
a
,then
Then
g=p_{1}^{g_{1}}p_{2}^{g_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{g_{k}}
with
g_{i}\leq a_{i}
.
If
g
divides
b
,then also
g_{i}\leq b_{i}
, i.e.
g_{i}\leq\min(a_{i},b_{i})
.
This means that
g|D
. This is equivalent to also having
g\leq D
.
Thus any common divisor
g
of
a,b
is
g\leq D
. This means

D
is
d=\gcd(a,b)
.

We work similarly for the lcm.
∎

Corollary 1.17

Let
a_{i}\geq 0,b_{i}\geq 0
. Moreover
a,b\geq 1
.
Let

a=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}},\quad b=p_{1}^{b_{1}}p_{2}^{b_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{b_{k}},\quad a_{i},b_{i}\geq 0,i=1,\ldots,k.

Then

\lcm(a,b)\cdot\gcd(a,b)=a\cdot b.

Proof.

By the previous problem
we have

c_{i}=\min(a_{i},b_{i})\geq 0
, and

d_{i}=\max(a_{i},b_{i})\geq 0
, and

p_{i}^{c_{i}}p_{i}^{d_{i}}=p_{i}^{\min(a_{i},b_{i})}p_{i}^{\max(a_{i},b_{i})}=p_{i}^{a_{i}}p_{i}^{b_{i}}.

Therefore

\lcm(a,b)\gcd(a,b)=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{c_{i}}p_{i}^{d_{i}}=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{a_{i}}p_{i}^{b_{i}}=\prod_{i}p_{i}^{a_{i}}\prod_{i}p_{i}^{b_{i}}=a\cdot b.

∎

Example 1.9.1

Find the gcd and lcm of 256, and 8192

Solution.

We have
a=256=2^{8}=p_{1}^{8}
and
b=8192=2^{13}=p_{1}^{13}
,
where
p_{1}=2
and
a_{1}=8
and
b_{1}=13
.
Then

c_{1}=\min(a_{i},b_{i})=8
, and

d_{1}=\max(a_{i},b_{i})=13
, and

\gcd(a,b)=2^{c_{1}}=2^{8}=256

and

\lcm(a,b)=2^{d_{1}}=2^{13}=8192
.
∎

Example 1.9.2

Find the gcd and lcm of 60, and 630.

Solution.

We have

a=60=2^{2}\cdot 3\cdot 5,\quad b=630=2\cdot 3^{2}\cdot 5\cdot 7

Then

\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(60,630)=2^{\min(1,2)}3^{\min(1,2)}5^{\min(1,1)}7^{\min(0,1)}=2^{1}3^{1}5^{1}=30.

\lcm(a,b)=\lcm(60,630)=2^{\max(1,2)}3^{\max(1,2)}5^{\max(1,1)}7^{\max(1,1)}=2^{2}3^{2}5^{1}7^{1}=1260.

∎

1.9.4
Prime number theorem

Euclid’s theorem reproved.

Theorem 1.20

(Infinitely many primes)

There are infinitely many prime numbers distinct from each other.

Proof.

Suppose that there are finitely many prime numbers i.e.

p_{1}<p_{2}<\ldots<p_{n}

that is
n
distinct prime numbers exist. Then form
the product
N=p_{1}p2\ldots p_{n}+1
. Since
N>1

by Theorem
1.3
there is at least one
prime
p
dividing
N
. This
p
cannot be any of the

p_{1}\ldots p_{n}
. Why ? Say
p=p_{i}
for some
i
.
Then
p|N
and
p|p_{1}\ldots p_{n}
which
would imply
p|N-p1\ldots p_{n}
. The latter
implies
p|1
i.e.
p\leq 1
but given
p

is a prime number we must have
p>1
. A contradiction.
Thus
p
is a prime number other than the ones
of the finite group
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
.
∎

Theorem 1.21

(Prime number theorem)

Let
\pi(n)
be the number of prime numbers less than or equal
to
n
. Then
\pi(n)\Rightarrow\infty
as
n\Rightarrow\infty
.
It is
\pi(n)\approx n/\ln{n}
, or equivalently the following
holds.

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)}{n/\ln{n}}=1.

(1.27)

Proof.

Omitted.
∎

Theorem 1.22

Let
\pi(n)
be the number of prime numbers less than or equal
to
n
.
Let
p_{n}
be the
n
-th prime number.
The following applies.

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)}{n/\ln{n}}=1\Leftrightarrow\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{p_{n}}{n\ln{n}}=1

(1.28)

Proof.

\Rightarrow
.
We start with Eq.(
1.27
).

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)}{n/\ln{n}}=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)\ln{n}}{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ln{\left(\frac{\pi(n)\ln{n}}{n}\right)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ln{1}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\left(\ln{\left(\pi(n)\right)}+\ln{\left(\ln{n}\right)}-\ln{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ln{n}\cdot\left(\frac{\ln{\pi(n)}}{\ln{n}}+\frac{\ln{\left(\ln{n}\right)}}{\ln{n}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ln{n}\cdot\left(\frac{\ln{\pi(n)}}{\ln{n}}-1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ln{\pi(n)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\ln{n}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\ln{\pi(n)}}{\ln{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

(1.29)

We multiply Eq.(
1.27
) with Eq.(
1.9.4
) to derive the following.

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\ln{\pi(n)}}{\ln{n}}=1

\displaystyle\wedge

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)}{n/\ln{n}}=1\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)\cdot{\ln{\pi(n)}}}{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1

(1.30)

In the latter Eq.(
1.9.4
) we substitute

n=p_{n}
and
\pi(n)=\pi(p_{n})=n
.
We then obtain the following.

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\pi(n)\cdot{\ln{\pi(n)}}}{n}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\frac{n\cdot\ln{n}}{p_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1.

(1.31)

This concludes this case, after an inversion.

\Leftarrow
.
Shown similarly.
∎

1.9.5
Mersenne numbers

Definition 1.16
(Mersenne numbers)

The Mersenne number of order
k
is denoted as
M_{k}=2^{k}-1
,
where
k
is a prime number.

Theorem 1.23

(Mersenne prime numbers)

If
M_{k}=2^{k}-1
is a prime number, then

M_{k}
is a Mersenne prime (number).

Example 1.9.3

Find the first eight Mersenne numbers.

Solution.

The first eight Mersenne numbers are as follows.

2^{2}-1=3,\quad 2^{3}-1=7,\quad 2^{5}-1=31,\quad 2^{7}-1=127,\quad

2^{11}-1=2047,\quad 2^{13}-1=8191,\quad 2^{17}-1=131071,\quad 2^{19}-1=524287,\quad

∎

Example 1.9.4

Find the first five Mersenne prime numbers.

Solution.

The first five Mersenne primes are as follows.

2^{2}-1=3,\quad 2^{3}-1=7,\quad 2^{5}-1=31,\quad 2^{7}-1=127,\quad 2^{13}-1=8191,\quad

Note that 2047 is missing from the second list.
This is because
2047=23\cdot 89
.
One can verify 131071 and 524287 are both
Mersenne primes as well.
∎

1.9.6
Fermat numbers

Definition 1.17
(Fermat numbers)

The Fermat number of order
k
is denoted as
F_{k}=2^{2^{k}}+1
,

k\geq 0
.

Theorem 1.24

(Fermat prime numbers)

If
F_{k}
is a prime number, then

F_{k}
is a prime Fermat number,
otherwise it is a composite Fermat number.

Example 1.9.5

Find the first five Fermat numbers.

Solution.

F_{0}=3,\quad F_{1}=5,\quad F_{2}=17,\quad F_{3}=257,

F_{4}=65537,\quad F_{5}=4294967297.

All first give are prime Fermat numbers (to mychecking).
But for the last one

4294967297=641*6700417,

we conclude
F_{5}
is a composite Fermat number.
∎

Example 1.9.6

Find the first five prime Fermat numbers.

Solution.

Consider,

(F_{n}-1)^{2}+1=(2^{2^{n}}+1-1)^{2}+1=2^{2\cdot 2^{n}}+1=F_{n+1}.

Moreover

F_{n+1}-2=2^{2^{n+1}}-1=(2^{2^{n}}+1)(2^{2^{n}}-1)=F_{n}(F_{n}-2).

By induction

F_{n+1}-2=F_{n}\cdot(F_{n}-2)=F_{n}\cdot\left(F_{n-1}(F_{n-1}-2)\right)=F_{n}\cdot F_{n-1}(F_{n-1}-2)=F_{n}F_{n-1}\ldots F_{0}.

Therefore

F_{k}|F_{n+1}-2,\quad 0\leq k\leq n.

∎

1.10
A short review of algebra definitions

We recite some definitions.
This section can be skipped as needed.

Definition 1.18
(Equivalence relation)

A binary relation
R
on
X
is an equivalence relation on
X

if for every
x,y,z\in X
the following properties are
satisfied.

•

(a) Reflexivity
xRx
for every
x\in X
,

•

(b) symmetry
xRy
then
yRx
, and

•

(c) transitivity
xRy
and
yRz
then
xRz
.

Definition 1.19
(Equivalence classes)

In an equivalence relation we can decompose
X
into
disjoint subsets known as equivalence classes.
Then for each
x\in X
the set of elements equivalent to
x

define

[x/R]=\{y\in X:xRy\}.

Then

p:X\rightarrow X/R\ \mathrm{with}\ p(x)=[x].

For an equivalence relation
R
if
aRb
the
[a]=[b]
.
(It is the same as
[a/R]=[b/R]
.)

Definition 1.20
(Residue classes modulo
n
)

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
The set of all residue classes modulo
n
or congruences
mod
n
(or modulo
n
) is
denoted in simple form by
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
.

\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}=\{0,1,2,\ldots,n-1\}.

This is supposed to mean the following.

\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}=\{(0)_{n},(1)_{n},(2)_{n},\ldots,(n-1)_{n}\}.

We read this set “
\mathbb{Z}
modulo
n
” or “
\mathbb{Z}
mod
n
”.
Sometimes we write
\mathbb{Z}_{n}
instead of
\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
.
The latter notation explains set
\mathbb{Z}
is split by
n
into

n
equivalence classes. Two integer
x,y
belong to the same
equivalence class if

(x-y)
or
(y-x)
is a
multiple of
n
. The equivalence class of

x
is
x\bmod n
.

1.10.1
Binary operators and operations

Definition 1.21
(Binary operator)

A binary operator defines a binary operation.

A binary operation is the operation implied by a binary operator.

Definition 1.22
(Binary operation)

For a set
S
and a binary operator
\oplus
, a
binary operation is a rule that maps to each
pair
(x,y)\in S\times S
a unique
z\in S
.

Operations
+
,
*
are binary operations for a
set
S=\mathbb{N}
or
S=\mathbb{R}
.
In all definitions below we assume
S
is non-empty.

1.10.2
Groupoids and semigroups

Definition 1.23
(Magma/Groupoid)

Let
S
be a set, and
\oplus
be a
closed operator
S\times S\mapsto S
defining
a total function as follows.

\forall(x,y)\in S\times S:(x,y)\mapsto x\oplus y\in S.

The structure
S
equipped with
\oplus
as structured
is a magma. We then say
(S,\oplus)
defines a magma,
also known as a groupoid.

Operator
\oplus
, and when introduced operator
\otimes
,
are closed operators
S\times S\mapsto S
defining
a total function as follows.

\forall(x,y)\in S\times S:(x,y)\mapsto x\oplus y\in S.

This is also known as a closure property.

Definition 1.24
(Semigroup)

Let
S
be a non-empty set, and
\oplus
be an operator

\oplus:S\times S\mapsto S
.
If operator
\oplus
has the closure property and is associative,
then the structure
S
equipped with
\oplus
as structured
is a semigroup.

1.

Closure property:
\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y\in S
.

2.

Associativity :
(x\oplus y)\oplus z=x\oplus(y\oplus z)\quad\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S
,

We then say equivalently that
(S,\oplus)
defines a semigroup.

A monoid is a semigroup with an identity element.

Definition 1.25
(Monoid)

An
(S,\oplus)
that is a semigroup it becomes a monoid if
there exists an element
e
in
S
such that

\forall x\in S:e\oplus x=x\oplus e=x.

Then
(S,\oplus)
is called a monoid.

A monoid equipped with an inverse (every element has
an inverse element) is a group.

1.10.3
Groups

Definition 1.26
(Group)

An
(S,\oplus)
that is a monoid it becomes a group
if every element of
S
has an inverse element.

\forall x\in S,\exists y\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x=e,

where
e
is the identity element of the underlying monoid.
Then
(S,\oplus)
is called a group.

Below we give a standalone definition of a group.

Definition 1.27
(Group)

An
(S,\oplus)
with
S
non-empty is a group if it has the
following properties.

1.

Closure property:
\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y\in S
.

2.

Associativity :
(x\oplus y)\oplus z=x\oplus(y\oplus z)\quad\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S
,

3.

Identity: there exists an
e\in S
such that

\forall x\in S:e\oplus x=x\oplus e=x
.

4.

Inverse:

\forall x\in S,\exists y\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x=e
.

Then
(S,\oplus)
is called a group.

Example 1.10.1

For
S=\mathbb{Z}
and
\oplus=+
we define
e=0
.
The inverse
y
of
x
is then denoted as
-x
.

(\mathbb{Z},+)
is a group. It is also an Abelian group,
defined next.

Definition 1.28
(Commutative or Abelian group)

An
(S,\oplus)
that is a group becomes a commutative
group if

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x.

Then
(S,\oplus)
is called a commutative group,
also known as an Abelian group.

(\mathbb{R},+)
is also a commutative (abelian) group.

Example 1.10.2

For
S=\mathbb{R}
and
\oplus=+
we define
e=0

The inverse
y
of
x
is then denoted as
-x
.

(\mathbb{Z},+)
is a group.

Example 1.10.3

For an
\oplus=+
operator we use 0 as its identity
element that is,
e=0
. We denote the inverse of
a

as
-a
.
For an
\oplus=*
operator we use 1 as its identity
element that is,
e=1
.
We denote the inverse of an
a\neq 0
as

a^{-1}
or
1/a
.

A group with operator
\oplus=+
is known as
an additive group.
A group with operator
\oplus=*
is known as
a multiplicative group.

Definition 1.29
(Order of a group)

The order of a group
(S,\oplus)
is the number of its elements,
and it is denoted
|S|
(cardinality symbol).

Definition 1.30
(Subgroup)

For a group
(S,\oplus)
and a non-empty subset
T
of

S
that is
T\subseteq S
, we say
T
a sugroup of
S

if
(T,\oplus)
is a group.

The order of a group is the cardinality of its underlying
set
S
.

(\mathbb{R},+)
is also an abelian group.

(\mathbb{Z}_{+},+)
is not an abelian group. There is
no identity element then.

(\mathbb{Z}_{+},*)
is not an abelian group. There is
an identity element then which is
1
, but there is no inverse
for most elements (other than one)!

(\mathbb{Q}_{+},*)
is an abelian group.

(\mathbb{R}_{+},*)
is an abelian group.

(\mathbb{Q}^{*},*)
is an abelian group.

(\mathbb{R}^{*},*)
is an abelian group.

Example 1.10.4

For
S=\mathbb{R}-\{0\}=\mathbb{R}^{x}

and
\oplus=\cdot
we define
e=1

The inverse
y
of
x
is then denoted as
1/x
.

(\mathbb{R}^{x},\cdot)
is a group.

(\mathbb{R}^{x},\cdot)
is also an abelian group.

Definition 1.31
(Cyclic group)

For a group
(S,\otimes)
and
a\in S
,
the elements
a^{i}
, where
i
is an integer, form a subgroup
of
S
, called the subgroup generated by
a
.
A group is cyclic if there is an element
a\in S
that
the subgroup generated by
a
is
S
itself.
We can the write
S
as follows.

S=\{e,a,a^{2},\ldots,a^{k-1}\},

where
e
is the identity of the group,
and
k
is the smallest (positive) integer such that

a^{k}=e
.

If
S
is infinite,

S=\{\ldots a^{-2},a^{-1},e,a,a^{2},\ldots\},

1.10.4
Rings and Integral domains

Definition 1.32
(Ring)

A ring is an algebraic structure consisting of a
set
S
in which two binary closed operations
are defined say
\oplus,\otimes
.
Then,
(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is a ring
if (a)
(S,\oplus)
is an abelian group,
(b)
(S,\otimes)
is a semigroup,
and
(c)
\otimes
is distributive over
\oplus
,
with
e
being the identity element of
(S,\oplus)
.
A ring denoted as
(S,\oplus,\otimes)

has the following properties.

1.

Closure property for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y\in S
.

2.

Associativity for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S,(x\oplus y)\oplus z=x\oplus(y\oplus z)

3.

Commutativity for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x
.

4.

Identity for
\oplus
: there exists an
e=0\in S
such that

\forall x\in S:0\oplus x=x\oplus 0=x
.

5.

Additive inverse for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\exists y=-x\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x=e
.

6.

Closure property for
\otimes
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\otimes y\in S
.

7.

Associativity for
\otimes
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S,(x\otimes y)\otimes z=x\otimes(y\otimes z)
.

8.

Distribution of
\otimes
with respect to
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S:x\otimes(y\oplus z)=(x\otimes y)\oplus(x\otimes z)\wedge(x\oplus y)\otimes z=(x\otimes z)\oplus(y\otimes z).

Then
(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is called a ring.

All of
\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Q},\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
are rings;
in fact they are commutative rings for
\otimes
being commutative
as well.
Non-commutative rings are
n\times n
matrices
whose elements are from the rings above.

Definition 1.33
(Commutative ring)

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is called a commutative ring if

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is a ring and

\otimes
is also commutative.

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\otimes y=y\otimes x.

Definition 1.34
(Ring with identity)

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is called a ring with identity if

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is a ring and

\otimes
supports an identity element let us call it
1
.

\text{Identity for}\ \otimes:\exists e=1\in S:\forall x\in S:1\otimes x=x\otimes 1=x.

Definition 1.35
(Integral domain)

Let
(S,\oplus,\times)
be a non-zero
commutative ring with
0=e\neq 1
, where

e
or
0
is the identity of
\oplus
and

1
is the identity of
\otimes
,
that has no zero divisors which is equivalent
to the following.

a\neq 0,b\neq 0\Rightarrow c=ab\neq 0\ \text{or equivalently}\ c=ab=0\Rightarrow a=0\vee b=0.

Then the structure

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is called an
integral domain.

Definition 1.36
(Division ring)

A division ring
(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is a
ring with identity
1\neq 0

with an inverse element over
\otimes
, that is

\forall x\in S,x\neq 0,\exists a\in S:ax=xa=1.

Definition 1.37
(Ideal)

For a ring
(S,\oplus,\otimes)
,
a left ideal
I
of
S
is a subset of
S
that
is a subgroup of the structure
(S,\oplus)
of the ring
that is closed under left multiplication over
elements of
S
. Thefore
e\in I
,
e=0
being
the identity of
(S,\oplus)
, and for every

a,b\in I
and
x\in S
the following are true.

a+b\in I,\quad-a\in I,\quad xa\in I.

A right ideal is defined similarly.
A two-sided ideal is a left and right ideal;
oftentimes it is called an ideal.
If the ring is commutative, then the left, right, and
two-sided ideals coincide and the term ideal identifies
all three of them. Then the ideal
I
is an abelian subgroup.

1.10.5
Fields

A field can be defined as an extension of a ring as follows.

Definition 1.38
(Field from ring)

A field

(S,\oplus,\otimes)

is a division ring that supports a commutative
\otimes
.

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\otimes y=y\otimes x.

A field can also be defined as an extension of two groups as follows.

Definition 1.39
(Field from groups)

For a field
(S,\oplus,\otimes)
,

(S,\oplus)
is an abelian group,

(S,\otimes)
is an abelian group but with an inverse
defined only for
S-\{0\}
.
Moreover
\otimes
is distributive with respect to
\oplus
.

An equivalent standalone definition of a field follows.

Definition 1.40
(Field)

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is a field with two binary
operators
\oplus,\otimes
if
S
has
at least two elements, there exist
1\neq 0

where
0
is the identity of
\oplus
,
and
1
is the identity of
\otimes

and it supports the following properties.

1.

Closure property for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y\in S
.

2.

Associativity for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S,(x\oplus y)\oplus z=x\oplus(y\oplus z)

3.

Commutativity for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x
.

4.

(Additive) Identity for
\oplus
: there exists an
e=0\in S
such that

\forall x\in S:0\oplus x=x\oplus 0=x
.

5.

Additive inverse for
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\exists y=-x\in S:x\oplus y=y\oplus x=e
.

6.

Closure property for
\otimes
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\otimes y\in S
.

7.

Associativity for
\otimes
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall z\in S,(x\otimes y)\otimes z=x\otimes(y\otimes z)
.

8.

Commutativity for
\otimes
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S:x\otimes y=y\otimes x
.

9.

(Multiplicative) Identity for
\otimes
: there exists
an
e=1\in S
,
1\neq 0
such that

\forall x\in S:x\otimes 1=1\otimes x=x
.

10.

(Multiplicative) Inverse for
\otimes
:

\forall x\in S,\exists y=-x\in S:x\otimes y=y\otimes x=e
.

11.

Distribution of
\otimes
with respect to
\oplus
:

\forall x\in S,\forall y\in S,\forall zinS
:

x\otimes(y\oplus z)=(x\otimes y)\oplus(x\otimes z),

(x\oplus y)\otimes z=(x\otimes z)\oplus(y\otimes z).

Then
(S,\oplus,\otimes)
is called a field.

Roughly speaking for a field

(S,\oplus,\otimes)
,

(S,\oplus)
is an abelian group,

(S,\otimes)
is short of an abelian group, with
“short” defined that only elements
x\neq e
i.e.

x\neq 0
have a multiplicative inverse
y=1/x=x^{-1}
,
that is,
(S-\{e\},\otimes)
is a group.

Definition 1.41
(Finite field)

A finite field is a field that has a finite
number of elements or equivalently
|S|<\infty
.
The order of a field is the number of its elements.

Theorem 1.25

(Galois)

Finite fields exists for an order
q
that is a prime
power that is
q=p^{k}
