\mathbb{QR}_{n}
be
the set of quadratic residues mod
n
.
We define function
\mathbb{Z}^{x}\mapsto\mathbb{QR}_{n}

with
x\mapsto x^{2}\pmod{n}
.

If
a\in\mathbb{QR}_{n}
then there exists an
x
such
that

x^{2}\equiv a\pmod{n}.

Then since
n=pq
as defined, we also have the following.

x^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p},

x^{2}\equiv a\pmod{q}.

Thus
x
is also a square root of
a
mod
p
and mod
q
.
Consider now a
b,c
such that

b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p},

c^{2}\equiv a\pmod{q},

i.e. one of the square roots of
a
mod
p
,
and
one of the square roots of
a
mod
q
.
By the Chinese Remainder Theorem,
there is a
A
such that

A\equiv B\pmod{p},

A\equiv C\pmod{q},

which imply

A^{2}\equiv a\pmod{pq}

since
\gcd(p,q)=1
. (See also previous problem.)

Integer
a
has two square roots mod
p
.
Integer
a
has two square roots mod
q
.
Thus combining the choices to denote
B
, and
C
we have
four possibilities for a pair
(B,C)
above.
Thus
a
has four square roots mod
pq=n
.

∎

Proposition 3.13

Let
p>2
be an odd prime number. Let
a<p
or in general

p\nmid a
and thus
\gcd(p,a)=1
.
Then for
k\geq 2
,
a
is a q.r
mod
p^{k}
if and only if
a
is a q.r. mod
p
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
.
If
a
is a q.r. mod
p^{k}
, then there exists a
b
such
that

b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p^{k}}
. That is,

p^{k}|b^{2}-a
. Then
p|b^{2}-a
and the
result follows for the forward
direction:
a
is then a q.r
\pmod{p}
as well.

\Leftarrow
.
For the converse, let
b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p}
. The
proof resembles a prior proof and is by induction on
k
.

Base case.
Obviously true for
k=1
.

Inductive step: from
k
to
k+1
.

Let
a
be a q.r
\pmod{p^{k}}
for

k\geq 1
. We shall show that
a
is a q.r.
\pmod{p^{k+1}}
.

By the induction hypothesis (inductive assumption) he have

b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p^{k}}
i.e.

b^{2}-a=p^{k}r
, for some integer
r
.
Let us form
c=b+dp^{k}
, where
c,d
are yet to be
determined in full. We observe first that
2k\geq k+1
for

k\geq 1
. (It will be used to obtain the third derivation
below.)
We then have the following.

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(b+dp^{k})^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle b^{2}+2bdp^{k}+d^{2}p^{2k}-a\pmod{p^{k+1}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle rp^{k}+2bdp^{k}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle p^{k}(r+2bd)\pmod{p^{k+1}}

For
c^{2}-a\equiv 0\pmod{p^{k+1}}
we
need
p|r+2bd
in other words

(2b)d\equiv-r\pmod{p}
. Since
p
is an
odd prime
b<p
and
\gcd(2b,p)=1
,
there is a solution for
d
of the modular equation

(2b)d\equiv-r\pmod{p}
.
Thus
c^{2}-a\equiv 0\pmod{p^{k+1}}
as
needed and this completes the
inductive step.

In conclusion if
p
is a prime and
a
is a q.r
\pmod{p}
.
Let
b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p^{k}}
or
r=(b^{2}-a)/p^{k}
, for some
integer
r
.
The
c=b+dp^{k}
is such that
c^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p^{k+1}}

if and only if
c
is defined as follows after defining
d
.
Let
t\equiv(2b)^{-1}\pmod{p}
and thus
d\equiv-rt\pmod{p}
.
We have from above
c=b+dp^{k}=b-\frac{b^{2}-a}{p^{k}}p^{k}t
,
and thus

c^{2}\equiv\left(b-t(b^{2}-a)\right)^{2}\equiv a\pmod{p^{k+1}}
.
Thus a square root
b
of
a
mod
p^{k}
can be used to
construct a square root
c
of
a
mod
p^{k+1}
.
∎

Proposition 3.14

For
k\geq 3
,
a>0
is a q.r
mod
2^{k}
if and only if
a
is an odd integer number
and
a\equiv 1\pmod{8}
.

In fact if
a\equiv 1\pmod{8}
there are four square
roots of
a
mod
2^{k}
: if
b
is one of them, so is

-b
mod
2^{k}
, so is
b+2^{k-1}
and also
-(b+2^{k-1})
.

Proof.

Base case
k=3
.

We establish the base case
k=3
i.e. show that an
odd integer
a
with
a\equiv 1\pmod{2}^{3}
is a quadratic
residue. For
1,2,\ldots,2^{3}-1
, there is only one integer

a=1
such that
a\equiv 1\pmod{8}
, and this is obviously
a=1
.
It is straightforward to confirm that
1
is a q.r. mod
8
.
One has four square roots: 1,3,5, 7 mod
8
.
We can describe the four square roots as
b=1\pmod{8}
,

-b\equiv 7\pmod{8}
,
b+4=5
, and
-(b+4)\equiv 3\pmod{8}
.

Inductive step from
2^{k}
to
2^{k+1}
,
k\geq 3
.

By the inductive hypothesis,
say
a
is a q.r mod
2^{k}
,
k\geq 3
and also
a\equiv 1\pmod{8}
.
Then there exists a
b
such that

b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{2^{k}}

(3.58)

or equivalently
2^{k}|b^{2}-a
,
or equivalently
b^{2}-a=q2^{k}
, for some integer
k
.
We will find a square root
c
of
a
mod
2^{k+1}
then.

c^{2}\equiv a\pmod{2^{k+1}}

(3.59)

Case 1:
b^{2}\equiv a\pmod{2^{k+1}}
. If it is
so we are done:
b
is a square root of
a
mod
2^{k}
and
also mod
2^{k+1}
.

Case 2:
b^{2}\not\equiv\pmod{2^{k+1}}
.
We have
a
is odd; this mean
b
is also odd and thus
b+1

is an even number and multiple of two.
Just like a prior problem we form
c
from
b
as follows

c=b+q2^{k-1}.

We note that
2k-2\geq k+1
for
k\geq 3
.
Also
b+1
is even i.e.
b+1=2B
for some integer
B
.
We then obtain the following.

\displaystyle c

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b+q2^{k-1}

\displaystyle c^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(b+q2^{k-1})^{2}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle b^{2}-a+q^{2}2^{2k-2}+2bq2^{k-1}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle b^{2}-a+q^{2}2^{2k-2}+2bq2^{k-1}\pmod{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle b^{2}-a+0+2bq2^{k-1}\pmod{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 2^{k}q+0+bq2^{k}\pmod{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 2^{k}q\cdot(b+1)\pmod{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 2^{k+1}qB\pmod{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle c^{2}-a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{2^{k+1}}

\displaystyle c^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle a\pmod{2^{k+1}}

A square root mod
2^{k+1}
of
a
has been found.
It is straightforward to show that
-c
is also a square root mod
2^{k+1}
.
Furthermore,

(c+2^{k})^{2}=c^{2}+2^{k+1}c+2^{2k}\equiv c^{2}\equiv a\pmod{2^{k+1}},

indicates that
c+2^{k}
is a third square root of
a
, and likewise

-(c+2^{k})
is a fourth square root of
a
mod
2^{k+1}
.
∎

Lemma 3.27

Let
p
be an odd prime number.
Let
g
be a primitive root of
\mathbb{U}_{p}
and let
k

be an even and positive natural number.
Then
g^{k}
is a q.r. if and only if
k
is an even
number.

Proof.

\Leftarrow
.

Since
k
is an even number, then
k/2
is an integer.
Therefore

g^{k}\equiv(g^{\frac{k}{2}})^{2}\pmod{p},

after which we conclude
g^{k}
is a quadratic residue
mod
p
.

\Rightarrow

If
g^{k}
is a qudratic residue then there exists
an
a
such that

a^{2}\equiv g^{k}\pmod{p}.

Since
g
is a generator there exists an
i
such that

a\equiv g^{i}\pmod{p}
.
Therefore

g^{k}\equiv(g^{i})^{2}\equiv g^{2i}\pmod{p}.

Then
k-2i\equiv 0\mod{\phi(p)}
,
or equivalently

p-1|k-2i
.
Since
p
is an odd prime,
p-1
is even.
So is
2i
i.e.
k
must also be even.
∎

Corollary 3.8

If
g
is a primitive root
\pmod{p}
the
g^{k}
is a quadratic
residue if
k
is even.

Proof.

The
g^{2},g^{4},g^{6},\ldots,g^{p-1}
are the
(p-1)/2
quadratic
residues of Theorem
3.12
.
∎

Proposition 3.15

Let
p
be an odd prime number.
Let
g
be a generator of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
and let
k

be an even and positive natural number.
As shown earlier,
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
has
(p-1)/2

q.r. and thus
\mathbb{Z}=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}\cup\{0\}

has
(p+1)/2
. Since
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
is cyclic it
has a generator
g
.
The following then apply.

(a)

Show that
g
is a q.nr.

(b)

Show that
g^{2},g^{4},\ldots,g^{p-1}

are q.r and distinct.

(c)

Show that
g^{1},g^{3},\ldots,g^{p-2}

are q.nr and distinct.

Proof.

(a)
Say that
g
is a q.r. and let
a
be such that

\gcd(a,p)=1
and
g\equiv a^{2}\pmod{p}
.
It is the case that
a^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}

by Fermat’s Little Theorem.
Therefore

g^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv(a^{2})^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv a^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}.

The latter implies that
g
can be a generator
since
ord_{p}(g)\leq\frac{p-1}{2}
instea of

ord_{p}(g)=\phi(p)=p-1
as it should be.

(b) It is immediate from the previous problem.
The fact that they are distinct is also obvious.
Let

g^{2i}\equiv g^{2j}\pmod{p}
for
2i\neq 2j\leq p-1
,
and
i-j<p-1
leads to

2i-2j\equiv 0\pmod{\phi(p)}\equiv 0\pmod{p-1}

This implies
p-1|2i-2j
i.e.
p-1\leq|2i-2j|
.
The only possibility for these to happen is
i=j
.

(c) From (b) and the previous problem.
By Euler’s criterion we know that the number of
q.r. is at most
(p-1)/2
and by (b) it is exactly

(p-1)/2
. Thus the remaining are the q.nr.
∎

3.10
Gauss lemma

Theorem 3.13

(Gauss lemma)

Let
p>2
be an odd prime number number.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
be such that
a>0
and
\gcd(a,p)=1
.
Consider

I(a)=\{1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2:ia\pmod{p}\}=\{1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2:R(i)\},

and

T=\{1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2:R(i)<0\},

where
R(i)
is the absolute least residue of
ai\pmod{p}
,
and thus
-(p-1)/2\leq r(i)\leq(p-1)/2
,
with

R(i)\equiv i\cdot a\equiv a(i)r(i)\pmod{p},

and
a(i)\equiv\pm 1\pmod{p}
.
Then the following holds

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{|T|}.

Proof.

Diversion first.

In the remainder,
1\leq i,j\leq(p-1)/2
and thus

2\leq i+j\leq p-1
, and
i-j
or
j-i
is at least

-(p-1)/2
and at most
(p-1)/2
.
Consider set
A
and set
B
defined as follows.

A=\{1,2,\ldots,\frac{p-1}{2}\},

B=\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}-A=\{\frac{p-1}{2}+1,\frac{p-1}{2}+2,\ldots,p-1\}=\{-1,-2,\ldots,-\frac{p-1}{2}\}.

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
per problem’s statement. Consider the set

I(a)=a\cdot A=\{a,2a,\ldots,\frac{p-1}{2}a\}\pmod{p}.

For every
i\in A
, i.e.
1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2
we
have the following

a\cdot i\equiv R(i)=a(i)\cdot r(i)\pmod{p}

where
a(i)=\pm 1
and
r(i)
defines
a mapping
A\mapsto A
that is an injective function (injection)
and

r(i)\equiv r(j)\pmod{p}\Rightarrow i\equiv j\pmod{p}

(or equivalently,

i\not\equiv j\pmod{p}\Rightarrow r(i)\not\equiv r(j)\pmod{p}

).

The

r(i)\equiv r(j)\pmod{p}\Rightarrow i\equiv j\pmod{p}
,
is derived by the following observations,
noting that
if
r(i)\equiv r(j)\pmod{p}
, then
ai\equiv aj\pmod{p}
,
or
ai\equiv-aj\pmod{p}

Observation 1:

ai\equiv aj\pmod{p}
implies
i=j
.

If
R(i)\equiv R(j)\pmod{p}
with
ai\equiv aj\pmod{p}
,
then
i\equiv j\pmod{p}

since
ai\equiv aj\pmod{p}
implies
(i-j)a=kp
for some
k
and
since
\gcd(p,a)=1
we have
p|i-j
; for the range
of
i,j
this can be true only for
i=j
.

Observation 2:

ai\not\equiv-aj\pmod{p}
.

If
ai\equiv-aj\pmod{p}
then
a(i+j)=kp
for some
k

and since
\gcd(p,a)=1
we have
p|i+j
, We know
however that
i+j\leq p-1
and thus
p\nmid i+j
.

Therefore
r(.)
is a permutation of
A
.
We then have the following

\displaystyle a\cdot i

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle a(i)r(i)\pmod{p}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\prod_{i}a\cdot i

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\prod_{i}a(i)r(i)\pmod{p}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)!

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\prod_{i}a(i)\prod_{i}r(i)\pmod{p}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)!

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\prod_{i}a(i)\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)!\pmod{p}.

Since
\gcd(p,\frac{p-1}{2}!)=1
and by
Euler’s criterion
\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\equiv a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\pmod{p}
,
we obtain the following.

\displaystyle a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)!

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\prod_{i}a(i)\left(\frac{p-1}{2}\right)!\pmod{p}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i}a(i)\pmod{p}\Leftrightarrow\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{|T|}\bmod p\Leftrightarrow\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{|T|}.

∎

Theorem 3.14

(Gauss lemma version two)

Let
p>2
be an odd prime number.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
be such that
a>0
and

\gcd(a,p)=1
.
Consider

I(a)=\{1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2:ia\pmod{p}\}.=\{1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2:R(i)\},

and

T=\{1\leq i\leq(p-1)/2:R(i)<0\},

where
R(i)
is the absolute least residue of
ai\pmod{p}
,
and thus
-(p-1)/2\leq r(i)\leq(p-1)/2
,
with

R(i)\equiv i\cdot a\equiv a(i)r(i)\pmod{p},

and
a(i)\equiv\pm 1\pmod{p}
.
Then the following holds.

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{|T|}.

Proof.

Pick two elements of
I(a)
.
If two multiples of
a
say
ia
and
ja
,
1\leq i\neq j\leq(p-1)/2
,
are congruent
\pmod{p}
i.e.

ia\equiv ja\pmod{p}
then
i\equiv j\pmod{p}
, which leads
to
i=j
.
Likewise if
ia\equiv-ja\pmod{p}
then
i\equiv-j\pmod{p}
,
or
(i+j)\equiv 0\pmod{p}
, which leads to
p|i+j
which
is impossible since
p
is prime and
i+j\leq p-1<p
.
Thus the absolute values of the multiples should be distinct.
That is
|a|,|2a|,\ldots,|(p-1)/2a|
are distinct, and form
a permutation of
1,\ldots,\frac{p-1}{2}
.
Multiplying the multiples the first way we get
a^{(p-1)/2}((p-1)/2)!
.
Multiplying them together the other way we get
(-1)^{|T|}((p-1)/2)!
.
Equating the two we get
a^{(p-1)/2}\equiv(-1)^{|T|}\pmod{p}
, i.e.

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{|T|}
.
∎

Example 3.10.1

Let
a=2\in\mathbb{Z}_{7}^{*}
and
p=7
. Use Gauss’s lemma
to calclulate
\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)
.

Solution.

I(a)=\{2\cdot 1,2\cdot 2,2\cdot 3\}=\{2,4,6\}=\{2,4-7,6-7\pmod{7}\}=\{2,-3,-1\pmod{7}\}

T=\{-3,-1\pmod{7}\}=\{R(i):R(i)<0,1\leq i\leq\frac{p-1}{2}\}

and
|T|=2
.
Furthermore,

\prod_{x\in I(a)}R(i)=\prod_{1\leq i\leq\frac{p-1}{2}}ai=a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\cdot\frac{p-1}{2}!=2^{3}\cdot 3!==2^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\cdot 3!=\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)\cdot 3!.

Moreover,

\prod_{x\in I(a)}R(i)\equiv\prod_{x\in I(a)}a(i)r(i)\equiv(-1)^{|T|}\frac{p-1}{2}!=(-1)^{2}\cdot 3!=(-1)^{|T|}\cdot 3!

Equating the two products and cancelling

\frac{p-1}{2}!
since
\gcd(p,\frac{p-1}{2}!)=1
, the
result follows.
∎

Proposition 3.16
(Gauss lemma reformulation)

Let
p
be an odd prime.
For
a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p}
consider
M(a)=\{a,2a,\ldots,((p-1)/2)a\}
.
Let
q
be the number of values of
M(a)
that are greater than
p/2
.
Then

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{q}

The original set of values of
M(a)
can be reduced to belong to an interval

(-p/2,p/2)
. Then the number
q
of values greater than
p/2
becomes equal
to the number of negative values.

Proof.

If two multiples of
a
say
ia
and
ja
are congruent
\pmod{p}
i.e.

ia\equiv ja\pmod{p}
then
i\equiv j\pmod{p}
. Likewise if

ia\equiv-ja\pmod{p}
then
i\equiv-j\pmod{p}
.
Thus the absolute values of the multiples should be distinct.
That is
|a|,|2a|,\ldots,|(p-1)/2a|
are distinct.
Multiplying the multiples the first way we get
a^{(p-1)/2}((p-1)/2)!
.
Multiplying them together the other way we get
(-1)^{q}((p-1)/2)!
.
Equating the two we get
a^{(p-1)/2}\equiv(-1)^{q}\pmod{p}
, i.e.

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{q}
.
∎

Example 3.10.2

Let
a=2\in\mathbb{Z}_{7}^{*}
and
p=7
. Use Gauss’s lemma (other form)
to calclulate
\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)
.

Solution.

For
\mathbb{Z}_{7}
, all the multiples of 2 are

\{2,4,6,1,3,5\}
and
M(2)=\{2,4,6\}
.
Two of them are greater than
p/2
i.e. greater than or equal to 4.
This leads to
\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{2}=1
.
∎

A more comprhensive proof is provided later.

Theorem 3.15

If
p
is an odd prime then

\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)=1\mathbf{\;if\;}p\equiv\pm 1\pmod{8},\quad\quad\quad\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)=-1\mathbf{\;if\;}p\equiv\pm 3\pmod{8}\\

Proof.

Let
a=2
and consider
M(2)
.
There are
(p-1)/2
multiples and
\lfloor(p-1)/4\rfloor
are less than
p/2

and thus
(p-1)/2-\lfloor(p-1)/4\rfloor
are greater than
p/2
or negative.
If
p\equiv 1\pmod{8}
i.e.

p=8k+1
the
(p-1)/2=4k
. Then
0<2i\leq(p-1)/2
if and only if

0<2i\leq 4k
i.e.
0<i\leq 2k
. Then
q=2k
.
So
\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)=(-1)^{2k}=1
.
Other cases are proven similarly.
∎

3.10.1
Eisenstein’s theorem and the Legendre symbol

Theorem 3.16

(Eisenstein)

If
p>2
is an odd prime number and
a\neq p
is odd and
thus
\gcd(a,p)=1
, then show

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor},

(3.60)

using Gauss’s Lemma.

Proof.

For each
i=1,2,\ldots,(p-1)/2

we have by the division theorem the following.

ia=q(i)p+r(i),

(3.61)

where

q(i)=\lfloor ia/p\rfloor
and
0\leq r(i)<p
.
If
0\leq r(i)\leq(p-1)/2
then let
s(i)=r(i)
.
If
(p-1)/2<r(i)<p
then let
s(i)=r(i)-p
or
equivalently
r(i)=s(i)+p
.
Then,
R(i)
of Gauss’s Lemma is
R(i)=s(i)
for
all
i=1,2,\ldots,(p-1)/2
.
By Gauss’s Lemma we obtain the following.

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)=(-1)^{|T|},

where
|T|
is the number of
R(i)<0
or equivalently
the number of
r(i)>(p-1)/2
.
We now add-up Eq.(
3.61
) for all relevant
i
.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}(ia)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}r(i)\Leftrightarrow

(3.62)

\displaystyle a\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}i

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}s(i)+p|T|\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}i\pmod{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle p\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}s(i)+p|T|\pmod{2}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}i\pmod{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}s(i)+|T|\pmod{2}

(3.63)

We note above that
a,p
are odd and thus
a\equiv 1\pmod{2}

and
p\equiv 1\pmod{2}
.
Furthermore, in Eq.(
3.63
) the
\sum_{i}s(i)
values are

\sum_{i}(\pm i)
. But mod
2
we have
i\equiv i\pmod{2}
and
also
i\equiv-i\pmod{2}
. Thus the two sums cancel out mod
2
.
So we can rewrite Eq.(refeisen2) as follows.

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}(ia)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}r(i)\Leftrightarrow

(3.64)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}i\pmod{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}s(i)+|T|\pmod{2}\Leftrightarrow

(3.65)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor+0+|T|\pmod{2}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle|T|

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\lfloor\frac{ia}{p}\rfloor\pmod{2}.

(3.66)

We plus the right hand expression of Eq.(
3.66
) into Gauss’s
Lemma and the result follows.
∎

The sum of the exponent has a nice
geometric interpretation.
It is the number of lattice points under the
line
y=\frac{a}{p}x
that are
over the
x
axis between

x=0
and
x=p/2
.

3.10.2
Applications of Eisenstein’s theorem: Legendre symbol

Proposition 3.17

Let
p,q
be odd primes and let
p\neq q>0
, then the
following holds.

\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}\frac{q-1}{2}}

Proof.

The proof uses Eisenstein theorem and its lattice point
interpretation of the sum of its exponent.

Let
p,q
be odd primes.
Let
r=\sum_{i=1}^{(p-1)/2}\lfloor\frac{iq}{p}\rfloor

be the number of lattice points below

y=\frac{q}{p}x
and over the
x
axis and between

x=0
and
x=p/2
.

Similarly,
let
s=\sum_{i=1}^{(q-1)/2}\lfloor\frac{ip}{q}\rfloor

be the number of lattice points below

x=\frac{p}{q}y
and over to the right of the
y
axis
and between
y=0
and
y=q/2
.

The line
y=\frac{q}{p}x
and
x=\frac{p}{q}x

are the same.

None of the two set of points are
double counted as they lie on different
areas of the dividing line.
No point lies on the line as then
xq=py

and thus
x
is a multiple
of
p
and
y
a multiple of
q
.

The number of points are all inside the rectangle
defined by
x=p/2
and
y=q/2

and the two axes. The total number of points
is
(p-1)/2\cdot(q-1)/2
.
Thus
r+s=(p-1)/2\cdot(q-1)/2
.

By Eisenstein’s Theorem

\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)=(-1)^{r}
and
\left(\frac{q}{q}\right)=(-1)^{s}
.
Thus

\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)\left(\frac{q}{q}\right)=(-1)^{r}(-1)^{s}=(-1)^{r+s}=(-1)^{\frac{p-1}{2}\frac{q-1}{2}}

∎

Corollary 3.9

The following conjectured by Euler was proven by Gauss.

\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)=\begin{cases}-\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)&p\equiv q\equiv 3\pmod{4}\\
\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

(3.67)

Proof.

Cases analysis for
p,q
.
If
p=4k+3
and
q=4l+3
, then the product

\frac{p-1}{2}\frac{q-1}{2}
is an odd number.
In any other case, if one or both of
p,q
is
4k+1

the product is an even number. The result then follows.
∎

Equivalently, if either
p
or
q
is of the form
4k+1
then

\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)=\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)
, otherwise
\left(\frac{p}{q}\right)=-\left(\frac{q}{p}\right)
.

Lemma 3.28

For
p
an odd prime
>2
the following applies.

\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)=(-1)^{\frac{p^{2}-1}{8}}.

(3.68)

An Eisenshtein-based proof involving the
n
-th
roots of unity and in particular
8
-th roots of unity.

w_{8}^{8}=1
,
z=\sqrt{i}=w_{8}
, where
i^{2}=-1
,
and
z^{2}=i
and
z^{4}=i^{2}=-1
, and thus
z^{8}=w_{8}^{8}=1
.
Moreover
w=z+1/z
is such that
w^{2}=z^{2}+2+1/z^{2}=2+i-i=2
,
and thus
\sqrt{w}=\sqrt{2}
.

Proof.

By the reciprocity theorem (Euler criterion), we have the following;
note that in the third step we utilize the binomial theorem
(a+b)^{n}

for
a=z
,
b=1/z
and
n=p
.

\displaystyle\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{\frac{p-1}{2}}=(\sqrt{2})^{p-1}=w^{p-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{p}w^{-1}=(z+\frac{1}{z})^{p}w^{-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(z^{p}+z^{-p})w^{-1}\pmod{p}.

From the exponent
\frac{p^{2}-1}{8}
we distinguish the following
cases: (1)
p=\pm 1\pmod{8}
and
(2)
p=\pm 3\pmod{8}
.

Case 1:
p=8k\pm 1
.

Let
p=8k+1
. Noting that
z^{8}=z^{8k}=1
we
have the following.

z^{p}+z^{-p}=z^{8k+1}+z^{-8k-1}=z+1/z=w.

Case 1b.
Let
p=8k-1
.
Similarly we have the following.

z^{p}+z^{-p}=z^{8k-1}+z^{-8k+1}=1/z+z=w.

We then conclude the following

\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)\equiv(z^{p}+z^{-p})w^{-1}\equiv w\cdot w^{-1}\equiv 1\equiv(-1)^{\frac{p^{2}-1}{8}}\pmod{p},

as needed.

Case 2:
p=8k\pm 3
.

Let
p=8k+3
. Noting that
z^{8}=z^{8k}=1
we
have the following.

z^{p}+z^{-p}=z^{8k+3}+z^{-8k-3}=z^{3}+1/z^{3}=-w.

This is because

w=z+1/z\Rightarrow w^{3}=(z+1/z)^{3}=z^{3}+1/z^{3}+3z+3/z\Rightarrow z^{3}+1/z^{3}=w^{3}-3w=w^{2}w-3w=2w-3w=-w.

The
p=8k-3
is proven similarly.
We then conclude the following

\left(\frac{2}{p}\right)\equiv(z^{p}+z^{-p})w^{-1}\equiv-w\cdot w^{-1}\equiv-1\equiv(-1)^{\frac{p^{2}-1}{8}}\pmod{p},

as needed.
∎

3.11
Pythagorean triplets

Let
a^{2}+b^{2}=c^{2}
be the pythagorean identity with
a\leq b\leq c
.
A triplet
(a,b,c)
is a solution to the identity. It is called primitive
if
\gcd(a,b)=1
. If
(a,b,c)
is a triplet so is
(ma,mb,mc)
for every
integer
m
.

One way to generate triplets is to use the identity

(x+y)^{2}=x^{2}+2xy+y^{2}

and

(x-y)^{2}=x^{2}-2xy+y^{2}

which implye

(x+y)^{2}-(x-y)^{2}=4xy
.
Set
x=X^{2}
and
y=Y^{2}
we have

Thus

Theorem 3.17

Thus
a=2XY
,
b=X^{2}-Y^{2}
and
c=X^{2}+Y^{2}
or equivalently

(a,b,c)=(2XY,X^{2}-Y^{2},X^{2}+Y^{2})
is a pythagorean triplet,
since

(X^{2}+Y^{2})^{2}=(2XY)^{2}+(X^{2}-Y^{2})^{2}

Moreover
Z(a,b,c)
are other triplets, for
X,Y,Z\in\mathbb{Z}
.

Theorem 3.18

If
p
is a prime factor of
n
with
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
and

p
divides
n
an odd number of times. Then
n
cannot be expressed
as a sum of squares.

Proof.

Let us assume the theorem is false. Let
n
be the smallest
n
that
is a counterexample and thus
n=a^{2}+b^{2}
.
Since
p|n
we have
a^{2}+b^{2}\equiv 0\pmod{p}
i.e.

b^{2}\equiv-a^{2}\pmod{p}
.
If
p\nmid a
then
p\nmid b
and thus
a^{-1}
and
b^{-1}
exist.Thus

(ba^{-1})^{2}\equiv-1\pmod{p}
. This means
-1
is a q.r
\pmod{p}
.
Which contradicts the non-being so since
p\equiv 3\pmod{4}
.

Thus
p|a
and
p|b
and thus
p^{2}|n
.
The
a=pa_{1},b=pb_{1}

n=p^{2}n_{1}
. We get
that
n_{1}^{2}=a_{1}^{2}+b_{1}^{2}
.
∎

A linear congruential generator (LCG) is one such that

x_{i+1}\equiv ax_{i}+b\pmod{n}
, where
\gcd(a,n)=1
.
If
\gcd(a-1,n)=1
the
x_{0}
should be chosen so that

\gcd(x_{0}-b(1-a)^{-}1,n)=1

The period of LCG is its modulus
n
if and only if

\gcd(b,n)=1
,
a\equiv 1\pmod{p}
for every
prime
p
such that
p|n
,
and
a\equiv 1\pmod{4}
if
4|n
.

A Blum-Blum-Shub (BBS) sequence is one where
n=pq
and
p,q
are primes
such that
p\equiv q\equiv 3\pmod{p}
. A seed
x_{0}
is chosen so that

\gcd(x_{0},n)=1
. Then
x^{2}_{i+1}\equiv x^{2}_{i}\pmod{n}
.
The output is
x_{i}\pmod{2}
. For a long
period
\gcd(\phi(p-1),\phi(q-1))
is
small compared to
n
.

3.12
Public Key Cryptography

A very brief overview of applications of number theory
is given in this section

3.12.1
Diffie-Hellman key exchange

It uses
\mathbb{U}_{p}
exponentiation.
Choose a large prime
p
, and an element
g\in\mathbb{U}_{p}
,
where
g
is preferably a primitive root.
This information is public.
The following two pieces of information
are secret for each party involved.
Alice chooses a secret exponent
1\leq a\leq p-1
.
Bod chooses a secret exponent
1\leq b\leq p-1
.
Alice publishes
g^{a}\pmod{p}
and
Bob
g^{b}

\pmod{p}
.
The other party picks the other’s published info and
compute
g^{a}g^{b}\pmod{p}
. Only they know both
multiplicands and thus their product.
The only way to retrieve from
g^{a},g^{b},g,p
the

a
or
b
is by a slow discrete logarithm process.

3.12.2
RSA

Let
p,q
are two large primes
p\neq q
. Let
n=pq
and choose
an
e
such that

\gcd(e,\phi(n))=\gcd(e,(p-1)(q-1))=1

1. Public key.
It is the pair
(e,n)
.
The modular equation

ed\equiv 1\pmod{(p-1)(q-1)}

is then solved. Because

\gcd(e,(p-1)(q-1))=1
, there exists one and only one
solution
\bmod\phi(n)
.

2. Private key.
It is the pair
(p,q)
or for convenience the
triplet
(d,p,q)

(
d
is not needed as it can be recomputed from the public key
and private
p
,
q
).

The following information is public or private for a a party, say
Alice. (Similarly for the other part, Bob.)

Alice’s Public Information:

(e_{A},n_{A})

Alices’ Private Information:

(d_{A},p_{A},q_{A})
.

Communication from Alice to Bob: sending encrypted message
M
.

In order for Alice to send Bob a message
M
, Alice must
retrieve Bob’s Public information
(e_{B},n_{B})
.
Then for message
M
Alice
computes
C\equiv M^{e_{B}}\pmod{n}_{B}
.
It transmits
C
to Bob, not
M
.

Communication received by Bob: recovery of
M

Bob received
C
from Alice.
He retrieves his private information
(d_{B},p_{B},q_{B})
.
He then performs the following computation (note
n_{B}=p_{B}q_{B}
).

C^{d_{B}}\equiv(M^{e_{B}})^{d_{B}}\equiv M^{e_{B}d_{B}}\equiv M\pmod{n_{B}}

The correctness of the decruption process
B
follows by way of
Euler’s theorem.
Bob chose
n_{B}=p_{B}q_{B}
where

e_{B}d_{B}\equiv 1\pmod{(p_{B}-1)(q_{B}-1)}\Leftrightarrow e_{B}d_{B}\equiv 1\pmod{\phi(n_{B})}

Therefore there exists a
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

e_{B}d_{B}-1=k\phi(n_{B}).

Then from the decryption process, we have

M^{e_{B}d_{B}}\equiv M^{e_{B}d_{B}-1}\cdot M\equiv M^{k\phi(n_{B})}\cdot M\equiv(M^{\phi(n_{B})})^{k}\cdot M\equiv(1)^{k}\cdot M\equiv M\pmod{n_{B}},

where we used Euler’s theorem for

M^{\phi(n_{B})}\equiv 1\pmod{n_{B}}.

RSA’s difficulty relies on the perceived difficulty of factoring
n

into
p,q
and thus computing
d
. Equivalently on computing
d
from

e,n
alone without factoring
n
.

For RSA message
M
must be close to the size of
\phi(n)
.
Thus padding may need to be performed if
M
is small (or an attacker
may rely on brute force techniques).
Because of these, RSA is primarily being used to transmit secret keys,
and other methods are used for transmitting messages such as
M
.

Chapter 4
Primality testing

4.1
Carmichael numbers

Definition 4.1
(Carmichael numbers)

A composite natural number
n>1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
is a Carmichael number
if it satisfies the following.

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n},\quad\forall a\in\mathbb{Z}.

Definition 4.2
(Carmichael numbers; an alternative definition)

A composite natural number
n>1
,
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
is a Carmichael number
if it satisfies the following.

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n},\quad\forall a\in\mathbb{Z}:\gcd(a,n)=1.

Example 4.1.1

Integer
n=561
is a Carmichael number.
Show that
n=561
is not a prime number by finding an
a

such that

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n},

that’s. a violation of Fermat’s little theorem is established.
How much is

a^{n-1}\pmod{n},

then?

Solution.

Consider
n=561
. It is obviously a composite number
as
n
is divisible by 3. Thus
n
is not a prime number.
For a prime number
n
,

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
for all
a=1,2,\ldots,n-1
,
by way of Fermat’s little theorem.

Consider
a=2
. It can be shown that

2^{560}\equiv 1\pmod{561}
.
Obviously then

2^{561}\equiv 2\pmod{561}
.

Consider now
a=3
.

3^{560}\equiv 375\pmod{561}\not\equiv 1\pmod{561}
,
and a violation of Fermat’s little
Theorem is obtained for the premise
n=561
is
a prime number.
Note however that
3^{561}\equiv 3\pmod{561}
.
Even though
561
is a Carmichael number and for
every
a
with
\gcd(a,561)=1
, we have

a^{560}\equiv 1\pmod{561}
, note that

a=3
has
\gcd(3,561)=3\neq 1
.
∎

4.1.1
Korselt’s theorem

It is also known as Korselt’s criterion.
It characterizes Carmichael numbers.
A positive (composite) integer
n
is a
Carmichael number if and only if it is
square free and for all prime divisors
p
of

n
we have
p-1|n-1
.
One can directly and obviously use the Korselt theorem
to show that all Carmichael numbers are odd.

The proof is deferred after the proof of two technical
lemmas.

Lemma 4.1

All Carmichael numbers are odd.

Proof.

(Using Korselt’s theorem.)

Say
n
is a Carmichael number and it is even.
Then
n=2m
, where
m
is odd since a
Carmichael number is square-free and thus
m

can not be even. Then
m
has at least one
prime factor, let it that be
p
, and it must be odd
(including other ones). We thus have

p|m\Rightarrow p|n
. Then by Korselt’s
theorem
p-1|n-1
would imply that an
even number
p-1
divides an odd number
n-1
, a contradiction.

(Another proof without using Korselt’s theorem.)

Say
n
is even.
Consider
a=(-1)
and by Carmichael numbers definition
we have the following.

(-1)^{n}\equiv(-1)\pmod{n}\Rightarrow 1\equiv(-1)\pmod{n}\Rightarrow 2\equiv 0\pmod{n}

This implies
n|2
. Thus
n\leq 2
. For a positive

n
this means
n=1
or
n=2
. A Carmichael number is
n>1
.
Thus the only case left is
n=2
. But a Carmichael number
is composite and two is prime. Thus
n
cannot be even,
and must be odd.
∎

Lemma 4.2

Let
n=p_{1}p_{2}
,
p_{1}\neq p_{2}
,
where
p_{1},p_{2}\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
are prime greater than 2.
Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
such that

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{1}}

and

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{2}}.

Then, the following applies.

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{1}p_{2}}.

The result can be generalized by using induction on integer

n
having more than two prime factors.

Proof.

If
a
is as given then there exist
K,L
such that

a^{n-1}-1=Kp_{1},\quad a^{n-1}-1=Lp_{2}.

Since
p_{1},p_{2}
are prime different from each
other
\gcd(p_{1},p_{2})=1
, and therefore,
there exists
x,y
integer such that

xp_{1}+yp_{2}=1.

Multiply both sides by
a^{n-1}-1
.
We derive the following.

(a^{n-1}-1)(xp_{1}+yp_{2})=a^{n-1}-1\Rightarrow xLp_{1}p_{2}+yKp_{1}p_{2}=a^{n-1}-1,

from which we conclude

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{1}p_{2}}
.
∎

Theorem 4.1

(Korselt)

A positive (composite) integer
n
is a
Carmichael number if and only if it is
square free and for all prime divisors
p
of

n
we have
p-1|n-1
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
. We first prove the sufficient condition.
Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, with
n>1
that is compositive
and a Carmichael number. The following then apply
by definition

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n},\quad\forall a\in\mathbb{Z}:\gcd(a,n)=1,

or

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n},\quad\forall a\in\mathbb{Z}.

We will then show the following:

(1)

n
is odd,

(2a)

for every prime divisor
p
of
n
we have

p^{2}\nmid n
, that is,
n
is square-free, and

(2b)

for every prime divisor
p
of
n
we have

p-1|n-1
.

Part (1) has been proven in another problem. Set
a=-1
for
example.
We then show (2a) and (2b) as follows.
Let by the fundamental theorem of arithmetic

n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}},

(4.1)

be the prime decomposition of
n
, where
a_{i}\geq 1
for
all
i=1,\ldots,k
. We will show that

a_{i}=1
for all
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.
In the remainder
i
runs
i=1,2,\ldots,k
.
So does
j\neq i
if needed.
Note that

\gcd(p_{i},p_{j})=1
for all
i,j
.
By prior results on primitive roots, there exist a primitive
root
b_{i}
mod
p_{i}^{a_{i}}
for all
i
.
Then

ord_{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}(b_{i})=\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})=p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}(p_{i}-1),

(4.2)

where
\gcd(b_{i},p_{i})=1
.
Since
\gcd(p_{i},p_{j})=1
by the C.R.T. we have that there exists
an
a
such that

a\equiv b_{i}\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\ \forall i

(4.3)

Note that
\gcd(a,p_{i})=1
since otherwise

\gcd(b_{i},p_{i})>1
, a contradiction to
\gcd(b_{i},p_{i})=1
.
Since all
p_{i}
are prime numbers and relatively prime to each
other we have the following by a prior problem.

\gcd(a,n)=1.

(4.4)

This will be used to
derive the second equation below.
By the Carmichael number definition we have the following for
all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
and all
i
.

\displaystyle a^{n}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle a\pmod{n}\xRightarrow{\text{by Eq.(\ref{kor2})}}

\displaystyle a^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}\xRightarrow{p_{i}^{a_{i}}|n}

\displaystyle a^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\xRightarrow{p_{i}|n}

\displaystyle b_{i}^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

(4.5)

By way of Eq. (
4.2
) and Eq. (
4.5
) we
obtain the following.

p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}(p_{i}-1)|n-1.

(4.6)

From Eq.(
4.1
) we have
p_{i}^{a_{i}}|n

and given
a_{i}\geq 1
,

p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}|n
.
Furthermore from Eq.(
4.6
) we have

p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}|n-1
.
Combining the two we obtain

p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}|1
.
This is only possible for
a_{i}=1
and this is true for all
i
.
This establishes the square-free consition (2a).

Continuing,
a_{i}=1
and Eq.(
4.6
imply

p_{i}-1|n-1
for all
i
, conditional on
p_{i}|n

and this establishes condition (2b).
The sufficient part of Korstelt’s theorem has been shown.

\Leftarrow
We now show the necessary part.
Assuming (1), (2a) and (2b) we proceed to show that
n
is
a Carmichael number.

By the fundamental theorem of arithmetic, since

p^{2}\nmid n
for every prime factor
p
of
n
we have
the following

n=p_{1}\ldots p_{k},

(4.7)

and of course
p_{i}>2
for all
i=1,\ldots,k
.
Moreover
p_{i}-1|n-1
by way of condition (2b).
We consider two cases.

Case 1.
Pick
a\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\gcd(a,p_{i})=1
.
By Fermat’s little Theorem

\displaystyle a^{p_{i}-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}}\quad\forall i\xLeftrightarrow{p_{i}-1|n-1:(n-1)=(p_{i}-1)K}

\displaystyle a^{(p_{i}-1)K}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}}\quad\forall i

\displaystyle a^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}}\quad\forall i\xLeftrightarrow{\gcd(a,p_{i})=1}

\displaystyle a^{n}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle a\pmod{p_{i}}\quad\forall i

Case 2.
Pick
a\in\mathbb{Z}
with
\gcd(a,p_{i})=d>1
.
Since
d|a
and
d|p_{i}
for all
i
, we from
the latter
d\leq p_{i}
and since
d>1
it can only be

d=p_{i}
for a prime
p_{i}
for all
i
. Then

p_{i}|a
for all
i
. Trivially then,

a^{n}\equiv p_{i}^{n}\equiv p_{i}\equiv a\equiv 0\pmod{p_{i}}

Therefore
a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{p_{i}}
forall
i
using
a prior problem leads to

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n}
since all
p_{i}
are relatively prime
to each other by way each one b eing a prime number.

An alternative proof of
the sufficient condition
\Rightarrow
follows.

For a Carmichael number
n
we have for all a

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n}
.
Pick a
p
, such that
p|n
. Then
by way of
n
being a Carmichael number

p^{n}\equiv p\pmod{n}
, or

n|p^{n}-p
.
Since
p|n
the prior result shows that

p|p^{n}-p
.
Say
p^{2}|n
. Then

p^{2}|p^{n}-p
which would imply

p|p^{n-1}-1
.
Obviously
p|p^{n-1}
,
n>1
.
The last two imply
p|1
and

p=1
a contradiction
to the primality of
p
.
Therefore
p^{2}\nmid n
for
p|n
.

In order to show that for
p|n
we
have
p-1|n-1
we start with the
definition of a Carmichael number.
For
a\in\mathbb{Z}
we have
a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n}
,
and for
a
such that
\gcd(a,n)=1
we
further have than
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
For any
p
such that
p|n
we have
as a result
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
Consider a generator
a
of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{*}
.
We have
a^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
. From this
equivalence and the
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

we conclude
(n-1)-(p-1)=k\phi(p)=k(p-1)

that results into
p-1|n-1
as needed.
∎

Corollary 4.1

Show that the two definitions of Carmichael numbers
are equivalent.

Proof.

\Leftarrow
.

Let

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n},\quad\forall a\in\mathbb{Z}.

If
\gcd(a,n)=1
we obtain
the following.

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n}\Rightarrow a(a^{n-1}-1)\equiv 0\pmod{n}\Rightarrow n|a(a^{n-1}-1)\xRightarrow{\gcd(a,n)=1}n|(a^{n-1}-1)\Rightarrow a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

\Rightarrow
.

Let
n
be a Carmichael number. By Korselt’s theorem it
is squarefree. We need to show

a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{n}
for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Since
n
is squarefree it is the products of mutually
distinct primes. It thus suffices to
show
a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{p}
for all prime divisors

p
of
n
.

Case 1:
a
is divided by
p
.

The
a\equiv 0\pmod{p}
and
a^{n}\equiv a\pmod{p}
is
obviously true.

Case 2:
p\nmid a
.

Since
p
is a prime number we use Fermat’s little theorem.

a^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
Furthermore by Korselt’s theorem
p-1|n-1
and therefore

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
∎

Corollary 4.2

(a)
Show that a Carmichael composite odd number

n
is the product of at least three prime numbers.

(b) Moreover every prime factor is less than
\sqrt{n}
.

Proof.

(a)

Proof 1.

If
n
is a Carmichael number then by Korselt’s
theorem it is square free and for all prime divisors

p
of
n
we have
p-1|n-1
.
All
p,q,n
below are odd integer numbers.

Let
n=pq
be the product of just two primes,
where
p<q
and
p\neq q
since
n
is
squarefree. By Carmichael properties

p-1|n-1
and

q-1|n-1
.

\displaystyle q

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{q-1}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle n=pq

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle p\cdot 1\pmod{q-1}

\displaystyle n-1

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle p-1\pmod{q-1}

Since
p<q
we have
p-1<q-1
. This means
that
p-1
is the remainder of the division of

n-1
by
q-1
i.e.
q-1\nmid n-1
that
contradicts the stated

q-1|n-1
.

(b) From the previous case

q-1|p-1
implies, since
n=pq
, that

q-1|n/q-1
.
Therefore
q\leq n/q
. If
q=n/q
it means

n=q^{2}
contradicting
n
bein square free.
Therefore
q<\sqrt{n}
.
Furthermore
p<q<\sqrt{n}
as well.
Note that then
pq<n
and therefore there must
be a third prime factor
r
for
pqr=n
, providing
sort of a third proof to (a).
∎

Example 4.1.2

Prove part (a) of the Corollary above using another method.

Solution.

Reach the same conclusion as before, assuming
p<q

by noting the following.

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle pq

\displaystyle n-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p(q-1)+(p-1)

\displaystyle\frac{n-1}{q-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle p+\frac{p-1}{q-1}

The
\frac{n-1}{q-1}
is an integer
since
q-1|n-1
,
p
is obviously an
integer, and therefore
\frac{p-1}{q-1}

must be an integer i.e.
q-1|p-1
.
This would imply
q-1\leq p-1
i.e.
q\leq p

that contradicts
p<q
.
∎

4.2
Lucas theorem

Theorem 4.2

(Lucas theorem)

Show that
n>2
is a prime number if
and only if the following holds for some integer
a

such that
1<a<n
.

\displaystyle a^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}\quad\wedge\quad

(4.8)

\displaystyle a^{\frac{n-1}{p}}

\displaystyle\not\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n},

(4.9)

for every prime divisor
p
of
n-1
.

Proof.

∎

If no such
a
exists then
n
is composite or 1 or 2;
the latter two cases are eliminated by way of
n>2
.

Proof.

\Rightarrow

If
n
is a prime number then the
\mathbb{U}_{n}
multiplicative
group is a cyclic one with
n-1
elements. It then has a generator
g
.
Therefore
g^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
, but
g^{i}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
for

i<n-1
. Every element
a
of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
can be written in the form

a\equiv g^{k}\pmod{n}
. Then
a^{n-1}\equiv(g^{n-1})^{k}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
Then
ord_{n}(a)
divides
n-1
and of course
ord_{n}(a)<n-1
unless

a
is a generator. Thus we focus on generators such as
g
in
the remainder. Moreover, consider a prime divisor
p
of
n-1
.
We must have for a generator
g
,
g^{p}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
for every
p

since otherwise if
g^{p}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
,
p\neq n-1
,
then we have the following.

p|n-1\Rightarrow\frac{n-1}{p}>1\text{and is integer}\Rightarrow g^{p}\equiv 1\pmod{p}\Rightarrow ord_{n}(g)=p<n-1,

a contradiction to
g
being a generator and thus

ord_{n}(g)=n-1
.

\Leftarrow

Consider that there exists an element
a
such that

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}.

Then
ord_{n}(a)|n-1
.
Moreover,
\gcd(a,n)=d
shows
d=1
.
Otherwise if
d>1
,
d|a^{n-1}
and
d|n
and
therefore
d|1
and
d\leq 1
. This (
\gcd(a,n)=1
)
implies by Euler’s theorem

a^{\phi(n)}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

and thus
ord_{n}(a)|\phi(n)
.
Let
ord_{n}(a)=k
.
Then from the first equation above we have
the following:
k|n-1
. There are two possibilities:

k=n-1
and
n
is prime, or
k<n-1
.
For the latter case,
(n-1)/k=pK>1
is an integer
and either a prime
p
(and
K=1
) or the product of
a prime
p
and a composite
K>1
.
Consider then

a^{\frac{n-1}{p}}.

By the second condition of the problem we have that for every
prime divisor of
n-1
and thus for the prime
p

a^{\frac{n-1}{p}}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}.

However we also have the following.

a^{\frac{n-1}{p}}\equiv a^{k\cdot K}\equiv(a^{k})^{K}\equiv 1^{K}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

leading to a contradiction.
The only case left is
k=n-1
. If the order of
an element is
n-1
this means
n
is prime.
This is because it implies

n-1|\phi(n)
and
\phi(n)\leq n-1
, i.e.

\phi(n)=n-1
and thus
n
is a prime number.
∎

Theorem 4.3

Let
n>5
be an odd composite number. The following
statements are equivalent.

1.

\forall a\in\mathbb{Z},\gcd(a,n)=1\Rightarrow a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n}.

2.

n
is square-free and for every prime number

p
such that
p|n
we have
p-1|\frac{n-1}{2}
which
is equivalent to

Composite(n),SquareFee(n),\forall p,Prime(p)\wedge p|n\Rightarrow p-1|\frac{n-1}{2}.

Proof.

(1)
\Rightarrow
(2).

If
\gcd(a,n)=1
and
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n}

then
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
and
n
is a Carmichael number.
By Korselt’s theorem
n
is square-free. Moreover for every
prime divisor
p
of
n
we have by Korselt’s theorem also
that
p-1|n-1
. We thus need to show that

p-1|\frac{n-1}{2}
to complete the proof.

Show
p-1|\frac{n-1}{2}
.

Let
n-1=(p-1)q
by Korselt’s theorem and the fact that

p-1|n-1
.
There are two possibilies: (a)
q
is even, or (b)
q
is
odd.

Case (a):
q
is even.
Then
q/2
is integer
and

n-1=(p-1)q\Rightarrow\frac{n-1}{2}=(p-1)\frac{q}{2}\Rightarrow p-1|\frac{n-1}{2},

since
q/2
is an integer.

Case (b):
q
is odd.

We will show that this case is not possible. Therefore
case (a) is the only possible case that leads to the
desired outcome :
p-1|\frac{n-1}{2}
as proven.

If
q
is odd,
then
n-1=(p-1)q
and
n-1
is even for odd
n
,
and thus we have the following.

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv a^{\frac{(p-1)q}{2}}\equiv\left(a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\right)^{q}\equiv\left(\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\right)^{q}\pmod{p}.

Since
q
is odd, the latter part works as follows.

\left(\frac{a^{2}}{p}\right)=1\Rightarrow\left(\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\right)^{q}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\pmod{p}

By transitivity from the previous derivations we have

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\pmod{p}.

For a generator
g
of
\mathbb{U}_{p}
we have

g^{\phi(p)}\equiv g^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
,
and also

g^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{p}
,
by way of Lagrange’s theorem.
Then, by the Chinese Remainder Theorem for
n=pP
and noting
because
n
is square-free
\gcd(p,P)=\gcd(p,\frac{n}{p})=1

we can find an
a

such that the following hold.

a\equiv g\pmod{p},

and

a\equiv 1\pmod{\frac{n}{p}}.

Then

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{g}{p}\right)\equiv g^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{p}.

(4.10)

Moreover
a\equiv 1\pmod{\frac{n}{p}}
and therefore

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{\frac{n}{p}}.

(4.11)

By way of the consequent of part (1) we have
two possibilities: (i)
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
,
(ii)
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.

Case (b1).
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.

We have by part (1)
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
as
one possibility. If it is true then since
p|n
that

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
By way of Eq.(
4.10
) we then conclude that

p|2
that leads to
p=2
an impossibility since
n

is odd, and
p
is a factor of
n
.
The other case left from part (1) is the following one.

Case (b2).
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.

Then considering
\frac{n}{p}=P
, since
P|n
we
then conclude that

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{P}
.
By way of Eq.(
4.11
) we also conclude that

P|2
which leads to
P=2
, an impossibility since
n

is odd.
Therefore case (b) is impossible only case (a) is possible
and by case (a) we have already concluded
p-1|\frac{n-1}{2}
.
Proof completed.

(2)
\Rightarrow
(1).

Since
n
is square free let
n=p_{1}p_{2}\ldots p_{k}
.
By Fermat’s little theorem for every
a
such that

\gcd(a,p_{i})=1
we have
a^{p_{i}-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}}
.
Furthermore
p_{i}|n
implies
p_{i}-1|((n-1)/2)

or equivalently
(n-1)/2=(p_{i}-1)k_{i}
for some integer

k_{i}
.
Then

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv a^{(p_{i}-1)k_{i}}\equiv(a^{p_{i}-1})^{k_{i}}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}}.

By the CRT given that
\gcd(p_{i},p_{j})=1
we have
that

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{n}.

∎

4.3
Preliminary results for primality testing

Let
\mathbb{Z}_{p}=\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z}=\left\{0,1,\ldots,n-1\right\}
.
Let
\mathbb{U}_{p}
be the units of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
p

is a prime number greater than two.
It is also represented by
\mathbb{Z}_{p}^{x}
or

(\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z})^{x}
.
Formally

\mathbb{U}_{p}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{Z}_{p},a\text{\ has an inverse}\right\}.

Lemma 4.3

\mathbb{U}_{p}
is a group under multiplication mod
p
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\mathbb{U}_{p}
is closed under
under multiplication mod
p
.
Consider
a,b\in\mathbb{U}_{p}
.
For prime
p
and
1\leq a,b<p
we have

\gcd(a,p)=\gcd(b,p)=1
and thus
a^{-1},b^{-1}
exist.
Then

(ab)\in\mathbb{U}_{p},

since
(ab)^{-1}
exists and it is
b^{-1}a^{-1}

by way of

(ab)(b^{-1}a^{-1})=a(b(b^{-1}a^{-1}))=aa^{-1}=1,

and

(b^{-1}a^{-1})(ab)=(b^{-1}(a^{-1}a)b=b^{-1}b=1.

All other properties (e.g. associativity) hold for

\mathbb{U}_{p}
by way of holding for
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
.
∎

Corollary 4.3

The result is true for composite
n
as well;

\mathbb{U}_{n}
is then all
i
such that
\gcd(i,n)=1
.

Corollary 4.4

Use Lemma
4.3
to prove Fermat’s little theorem.

Proof.

If
p
is a prime number then

\mathbb{U}_{p}=\left\{1,2,\ldots,p-1\right\}.

If
p\nmid a
then if
a>p-1
, consider
b
such that

b=a\bmod p\Leftrightarrow a\equiv b\pmod{p},\text{\ where\ }b\in\{1,2,\ldots,p-1\}.

By Lagrange’s theorem

ord_{p}(b)|ord(\mathbb{U}_{p})

i.e.

ord_{p}(b)|p-1
, or
p-1=ord_{p}(b)l
.
If
ord_{p}(b)=k<p-1
, then

b^{k}\equiv 1\pmod{p}\Rightarrow b^{p-1}=b^{kl}=(b^{k})^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{p}.

Since
a\equiv b\pmod{p}
, we have

a^{p-1}\equiv b^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p},

and the result follows.
∎

Lemma 4.4

Let
\mathbb{U}_{p}
be the units of
\mathbb{Z}_{p}
, where
p
is a prime
number greater than 2.
Let

A=\left\{a\in\mathbb{U}_{p}:a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{p}\right\}.

Show that
A
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{U}_{p}
under modular multiplication
mod
p
.

Proof.

Consider
c,d\in A
.
It is easy to show that
cd\bmod p\in A
.
Thus the order of
A
divides the order of
\mathbb{U}_{p}
which is

p-1
. Since
p
is a prime number greater than two, the
p
is
odd and thus
(p-1)/2
is an integer.
We have shown that the order of
A
is a divisor of
p-1
.
We will show that the order of
A
can either be
p-1
or
(p-1)/2

first by showing that
A
has at least
(p-1)/2
elements.
We can then conclude that the order of
A
is
(p-1)/2
by showing
that it cannot be
p-1
.

Consider then
b=a^{2}\pmod{p}
.
We show that
b^{2}\in A
.
In order to show inclusion to
A
we must show that

b^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
This is shown as follows.

b^{\frac{p-1}{2}}=(a^{2})^{\frac{p-1}{2}}=a^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p},

by Fermat’s little theorem as
\gcd(a,p)=1
for prime
p
and

1\leq a<p
.
Furthermore,
b
is a quadratic residue mod
p
since there exists an

a
such that
a^{2}\equiv b\pmod{p}
.
Thus all quadratic residues are in
A
. There are
(p-1)/2
of them.
The order of
A
is thus at least
(p-1)/2
. It can thus
be
(p-1)/2
or
p-1
.
We proceed to dismissing
p-1
as a possibility.

.
A generator
g
of
\mathbb{U}_{p}
is not in
A
since

g^{\phi(p)}=g^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and

g^{(p-1)/2}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
. Thus
|A|<p-1
,
and therefore it must be
(p-1)/2
if it can’t be
p-1
.
Moreover consider
t\not\in A
.
We have
t^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
but since

t\not\in A
, we have

t^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}
,
and it should be

t^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{p}
,
as the only two square roots of

x^{2}\equiv 1
by Lagrange’s theorem for
prime
p
are
+1
and
-1
mod
p
.
Note that

u=t^{\frac{p-1}{2}}
is such that

u^{2}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
and thus if
u\not\equiv 1\pmod{p}

then it must be
u=t^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{p}
.
∎

4.3.1
Probabilistic Turing machines

Definition 4.3
(Probabilistic Turing machine)

A probabilistic Turing machine is a non deterministic
polynomial time Turing machine
T
such that
for each configuration of
T
there are at most two
possible follow-up configurations (non-deterministic steps),
and
T
chooses which
one of the two to pursue (take) by flipping a coin.

Thus the paths of different configurations resembles
a binary tree. For
k
coin flips and paths of length
k

a given path is pursued with probability equal
1/2^{k}
.

4.3.2
Class BPP

Definition 4.4
(Class BPP)

Let
0\leq\epsilon<1/2
. A (probabilistic polynomial
time) Turing Machine
M
decides
language
L
with error probability
\epsilon
if

•

for a
w\in L
we have

Pr[\mbox{M\ accepts\ w\ }]\geq 1-\epsilon
,

•

for a
w\not\in L
we have

Pr[\mbox{M\ rejects\ w\ }]\geq 1-\epsilon
,
or equivalently

Pr[\mbox{M\ accept \ w\ }]\leq\epsilon
.

BPP is the class of languages that are decided by a probabilistic
polynomial time Turing machine with error probability
\epsilon
,
as defined.

4.4
Fermat primality testing

4.4.1
A Fermat little theorem-based compositeness test

Theorem 4.4

Algorithm
5
is a probabilistic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is composite.

Proof.

We note that in Algorithm
5
we check for the
compositeness of
n
for a uniformly at random drawn
a

in the range
2,3,\ldots,n-2
in two ways:
(a) we first perform the trivial check of whether
\gcd(a,n)>1
,
and
(b) then check whether
a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
If any of the two checks succeeds we not only conclude that

n
is composite but
a
serves as a witness (proof) of the
compositeness of
n
.
The latter (b) is a consequence of Fermat’s little theorem.
If
n
is a prime number, then any
a
picked through line 1 is
such that
\gcd(a,n)=1
. Therefore by Fermat’s little theorem
we expect
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
If the control expression of line 2 evaluates to true i.e.
there is a common divisor other than one between
a
, which is

2\leq a<n-1
and
n
indeed
n
is a composite number.
If the control expression of line 4 evaluates to true i.e.

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
this also guarantees by Fermat’s
little theorem that the answer Composite is the correct answer.
However, an
answer through line 5 does not guarantee that
n
is prime:
we checked only for one
a
in Algorithm
5
and not for all

a
.
We prefer to call this algorithm FermatCompositenessTest
for the following reason: when the algorithm declares
n
as
Composite the answer is always correct. Otherwise it declares

n
as PseudoPrime. This means
n
can be Prime or Composite.
One test for one value of
a
is not enough to determine
reliably the primeness of
a
.
Note that neither
1
nor
n-1
are picked as choices of
a
.
For both such cases the answer for
a^{n-1}
is equivalent to

1
mod
n
and provides no useful information.

Input:

n>4
is odd;
a
is in
\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}

Output:

n
is composite or pseudoprime

Pick
a\in\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
uniformly at random ;

1

if

\gcd(a,n)\neq 1

then

2

return

\mathbf{Composite}

end if

3

if

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}

then

/*
Compositeness check */

4

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a witness of
n
’s compositeness */

else

5

return

\mathbf{PseudoPrime}

;

/*
n
is either prime or composite */

end if

6

Algorithm 5

FermatCompositenessTest(n)

∎

We strengthen the capability and reliability
of Algorithm
5
by
introducing Algorithm
6
a Fermat little theorem
based primality testing algorithm.

4.4.2
A Fermat little theorem-based primality test

Theorem 4.5

Algorithm
6
is a probabilistic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is composite or prime.

Proof.

Algorithm
6
is in fact
t
runs of
Algorithm
5
; the
t
runs are independent of
each other and each run picks potentially (but not necessarily)
a different value for
a
.
Whereas the previous algorithm picks one
a
this one
picks
t

a
’s from
\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
uniformly
at random.

Input:

n>4
is odd; number of runs is
t

Output:

n
is composite or pseudoprime

i=0
;

1

do

2

i=i+1
;

3

Pick
a\in\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
uniformly at random ;

4

if

\gcd(a,n)\neq 1

then

5

return

\mathbf{Composite}

end if

6

if

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}

then

/*
Compositeness check */

7

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a witness of
n
’s compositeness */

end if

8

while

i<t
;

9

return

\mathbf{PseudoPrime}

;

/*
n
is either prime or composite */

Algorithm 6

FermatPrimalityTest

If
n
is a prime, then any
1\leq a<n
has

\gcd(a,n)=1
. Therefore by Fermat’s tittle theorem
we have
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
If an
a
can be found such that

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
, then the
a
becomes
a witness of the compositeness of
n
.
We shall denote it as
\text{Fw}(a,n)
or say

a\in\text{Fw}(n)
to denote that
a
is one
of Fermat witnesses of the compositeness of
n
.
Line 6 of Algorithm
5
or
Line 9 of Algorithm
6
report correctly this,
if such an
a
has been picked.
Moreover, LIne 3 of Algorithm
5
or
Line 6 of Algorithm
6
also report the
case where
a
is such that
\gcd(a,n)\neq 1
, an obvious
proof of the compositeness of
n
.
When either algorithm reports PseudoPrime this means
one of two things: (a)
that
n
is indeed a prime number or
(b) the test is not comprehensive enough to
determine that
n
is indeed a composite number.
Sometimes we call such an
a
for which

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

a Fermat non-witness of the compositeness of
n
.
We shall denote it with
\text{Fnw}(a,n)
or say

a\in\text{Fnw}(n)
.
Therefore we have the following.

a\in\text{Fw}(n):a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n},\gcd(a,n)=1,

and

a\in\text{Fnw}(n):a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n},\gcd(a,n)=1

For a Carmichael number n, which is a composite number,
we have the following from a prior discussion.
If we denote with
\text{Carmichael}(n)
the set of
a

that satisfy the Carmichael
condition
a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
for
a
such that

\gcd(a,n)=1
we conclude that the Carmichael(n) set
contains alll those integers.

\forall a\in\mathbb{Z},a\in\text{Carmichael}(n):a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n},\gcd(a,n)=1.

Thus for a composite Carmichael number
n
every
a
such
that
\gcd(a,n)=1
is a Fermat non-witness Fnw(a,n).
A prime number
n
will be reported through line 12 as PseudoPrime.
A composite number
n
could be reported as composite immediately
through line 6 or through FermatLittleTheorem testing in Line 9 for
a properly chosen
a
.
But it is possible that the values
a
used would never lead
to determining that that
n
were a Composite number through line 6.
Furthermore the choice of a given
a
(or all
a
) might not allow
the determination of
n
as Composite through line 9 because
n

is a Carmichael number.
In that case
n
would be reported as PseudoPrime through line 12.
Im summary,
If an
a
is picked with
\gcd(a,n)\neq 1
,
and
a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
then
a
is a witness of the compositeness of
n
or an
Fw(a,n) either because of Line 5 or Line 8 of
Algorithm
6
.
Algorithm
6
checks in lines 5-6 the obvious possibility
that
n
is a composite number. Thus the test of lines 8-10
is applicable only to
a
such that
\gcd(a,n)=1
, and therefore
it can fail for Carmichael composite numbers only or a prime number

n
.
∎

Algorithm
6
is a probabilistic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is prime or composite.

Proposition 4.1
(Probabibility of successful reporting)

The probability that the output of Algorithm
6
is
Composite given that
n
is a composite number but not
a Carmichael number is at least
1-2^{-t}
.

Proof.

In other words, if
n
is a prime number Algorithm
6

returns Pseudoprime with probability one.
If
n
is a composite number but not
a Carmichael number then
Algorithm
6
return composite
with probability at least
1-2^{-t}
.
Suppose that
\exists a\in\mathbb{U}_{n}
such that

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}.

Consider the following two sets
B_{n}
and
C_{n}
as follows.

B_{n}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{U}_{n},a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}\right\},

C_{n}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{U}_{n},a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}\right\},

We have
|B_{n}|+|C_{n}|=\phi(n)=|\mathbb{U}_{n}|
.
For a composite
n
, we have
|D_{n}|>1
as it contains
the prime factors of
n
, minimally.
Note that
a\in\mathbb{U}_{n}
implies
\gcd(a,n)=1
.
Set
C_{n}
is a subgroup. Consider
k,l\in C_{n}
with

k^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

and

l^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
We conclude easily that

(kl)^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
to prove closure.
Sidelining the claims about the obvious properties,
consider
K=k^{-1}
the multiplicative inverse of
k

and
consider
L=l^{-1}
the multiplicative inverse of
l
.
We then have

Kk\equiv 1\pmod{n}\Rightarrow(Kk)^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

from which we conclude
K\in C_{n}
.
Similarly for
l\in C_{n}
we can show that
L\in C_{n}
.
Moreover, we can show
(kl)^{-1}\in C_{n}
.
Thus
C_{n}
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
and the order
of
C_{n}
divides the order of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
which is
\phi(n)
.
Thus if
C_{n}\neq\mathbb{U}_{n}
(e.g.
n
is not a Carmichael
number), then
|C_{n}|\leq|\mathbb{U}_{n}|/2

and
|B_{n}|\geq|\mathbb{U}_{n}|/2
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
(Running time of algorithm
6
)

The running time of
Algorithm
6
is

O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n))
, where
M(n)

is the cost of multiplying
n
-bit integers.

Proof.

Exponentiation involves
O(\lg{n})
multiplication.
Depending on how we
implement integer multiplication the overall time complexity
is
O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n))
, where
M(n)

is the computational cost (bit model) of
multiplying
n
-bit integers.
∎

Corollary 4.5
(FermatPrimalityTest
\in
BPP)

Algorithm
6
belongs to class BPP,
known as Bounded-error Probabilistic Polynomial
time class.

Proof.

We shall map (call)
L
and
w
to

Prime
and
n
respectively.
If
n
is a prime number, it is decided by Algorithm
6

that it is a Prime (and output PseudoPrime is printed) with
probability 1.

If
n
is a composite number, it is decided by
Algorithm
6

that it is a Composite number (and output Composite is printed)
and thus rejected being a Prime/PseudoPrime with probability
at least
1-1/2^{t}
by way of Proposition
4.1
.

In other words,
n
can be wrongly decided by Algorithm
6

that it is a Prime (and output PseudoPrime is printed)
with probability at most
1/2^{t}
.
∎

4.5
Solovay-Strassen primality testing

The primality testing algorithm by Solovay-Strassen uses
Euler’s property for the Jacobi symbol (composite
n
)
or Legendre symbol (prime
n
) rather than Fermat’s
tittle theorem.
Euler’s theorem for an odd prime number
p>2

states the following (Legendre symbol use).

\left(\frac{a}{p}\right)\equiv a^{\frac{p-1}{2}}\pmod{p},\forall a:1\leq a<p.

Therefore were we to test if
p
was a prime number
by finding an
a
that violates the condition above that would
prove the following:
(a)
p
is not a prime number, and
(b)
a
is a witness of the compositeness of
p
.
Note that for an odd composite number
n>2
the symbol below is a Jacobi
symbol.

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\equiv a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n},\forall a:1\leq a<n.

The way we introduced in the previous section the concept
of a Fermat witness or a Fermat non-witness, we do the
same in this section by introducing the concept
of an Euler witness and Euler non-witness.
We present an example to highlight the concepts.

Example 4.5.1

Consider
n=1387
,
n=561
and
n=63
.
Pick
a=2
. Examine whether
a=2
is a Fermat witness
or an Euler witness (for the Solovay-Strassen algorithm to follow)
of the compositeness of
n
.

Solution.

(a)
n=1387=19\cdot 73
.
Pick
a=2
, as instructed.

It is

a^{1387-1}\equiv 1\pmod{1387},

and

a^{\frac{1387-1}{2}}\equiv 51\pmod{1387}.

In the former case
a
is NOT a Fermat witness;
the latter case indicates that
a
is an Euler witness.
This is because
\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)
should be
1
,
-1
or
0
.

(b)
n=561=3\cdot 11\cdot 17
.
Pick
a=2
, as instructed.

It is

a^{561-1}\equiv 1\pmod{561},

and

a^{\frac{561-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{561}.

In the former case
a
is NOT a Fermat witness;
the latter case indicates that
a
is also NOT an Euler witness.

(c)
n=63=3^{2}\cdot 7
.
Pick
a=2
, as instructed.

It is

a^{63-1}\equiv 4\pmod{63},

and

a^{\frac{63-1}{2}}\equiv 2\pmod{63}.

In the former case
a
is a Fermat witness;
the latter case indicates that
a
is also an Euler witness.
∎

4.5.1
The Solovay-Strassen primality test

The primality testing algorithm by Solovay-Strassen uses
Euler’s theorem (property or criterion).

Therefore, as we mentioned earlier,
were we to test if
p
was a prime number
by finding an
a
that violates the condition above that would
prove the following:
(a)
p
is not a prime number, and
(b)
a
is a witness of the compositeness of
p
.
Note that for an odd composite number
n>2
the symbol below is a Jacobi
symbol.

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\equiv a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n},\forall a:1\leq a<n.

Algorithm
8

performs
t
times the tasks of
Algorithm
7
which is a simpler
probabilistic algorithm.
Algorithm
7
checks Euler’s theorem for
a single random value
a
.
Algorithm
8
checks Euler’s theorem for
a
t
uniformly at random selected values
a
.
The former is weak algorithm and for this we call
it a compositeness test algorithm.
The latter is a more reliable one and is referred
to as a primality test algorithm.

Theorem 4.6

Algorithm
8
is a probabilistic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is composite or not.

Proof.

The algorithm tests for two conditions a number of times. We denote
as
t
the numer of times these conditions are tested.
Every time these conditions are tested we pick a random
a

from the set
\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
of cardinality
n-1
.
The first condition
C_{1}(a,n)
checks whether
a
has a
common divisor with
n
. A positive answer serves makes
a

a witness of the compositeness of
n
. We call
then
a
an Euler (test) compositeness witness and denote it by
Ew(a,n) or the set of witnesses for
n
is denoted as Ew(n).

C_{1}(a,n):\gcd(a,n)>1.

The second condition is Euler’s criterion.

C_{2}(a,n):\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\not\equiv a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}.

Therefore
a
becomes a Euler test compositeness witness if
and only if the following applies.

a\in Ew(n)\Leftrightarrow C_{1}(a,n)\vee C_{2}(a,n).

By way of

\overline{(C_{1}(a,n)\vee C_{2}(a,n))}=\overline{C_{1}(a,n)}\wedge\overline{C_{2}(a,n)}

we obtain the following condition for Euler (test)
compositeness non-witnesses Enw(a,n) or the set Enw(n).

a\in Enw(n)\Leftrightarrow\overline{C_{1}(a,n)}\wedge\overline{C_{2}(a,n)}.

The proof of correctness of both algorithms follows from the
prior discussion and thus omitted.

Input:

n>4
is odd;
a
is in
\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}

Output:

n
is composite or pseudoprime

Pick
a\in\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
uniformly at random ;

1

if

\gcd(a,n)>1

then

/*
C_{1}(a,n)
*/

2

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a witness of
n
’s compositeness */

end if

3

Calculate

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)
,
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}
;

4

if

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\not\equiv a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}

then

/*
C_{2}(a,n)
*/

5

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a witness of
n
’s compositeness */

else

6

return

\mathbf{PseudoPrime}

;

/*
n
is either prime or composite */

end if

7

Algorithm 7

SolovayStrassenCompositenessTest

Input:

n>4
is odd; number of runs is
t

Output:

n
is composite or pseudoprime

i=0
;

1

2

do

3

i=i+1
;

4

Pick
a\in\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
uniformly at random ;

5

if

\gcd(a,n)>1

then

/*
C_{1}(a,n)
*/

6

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a witness of
n
’s compositeness */

end if

7

Calculate

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)
,
a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}
;

8

if

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\not\equiv a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}

then

/*
C_{2}(a,n)
*/

9

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a witness of
n
’s compositeness */

end if

10

while

i<t
;

11

return

\mathbf{PseudoPrime}

;

/*
n
is either prime or composite */

Algorithm 8

SolovayStrassenPrimalityTest

∎

Proposition 4.2
(Probability of successful reporting)

If
n
is an odd composite number greater than two,
then Algorithm
7
returns Composite with
probability at least
1/2
.
The probability that the output of Algorithm
8
is
Composite given that
n
is a composite number
is at least
1-2^{-t}
.

Proof.

Consider the following two sets
B_{n}
and
C_{n}
as follows,
describing in fact Ew(n) and Enw(n).

Ew(n)=B_{n}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{U}_{n},a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\not\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}\right\},

Enw(n)=C_{n}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{U}_{n},a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}\right\},

Consider also set
D_{n}
defined as follows.

D_{n}=\left\{a:1\leq a<n\wedge\gcd(a,n)\neq 1\right\}.

We have that if
a\in B_{n}
then
n
is composite
and of course
a\in Ew(n)
.
We have that if
a\in C_{n}
then Solovay-Strassen
can’t figure out whether
n
is prime or composite
and of course
a\in Enw(n)
.
We prove a sequence of claims to derive our result.

(a) Show that
C_{n}
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
.

If
a,b\in C_{n}
then it is trivial to show that

ab\in C_{n}
as well.

\displaystyle a\in C_{n}\Rightarrow a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n},

\displaystyle\wedge

\displaystyle b\in C_{n}\Rightarrow b^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)\pmod{n},

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(ab)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}=a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}b^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{ab}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(ab)\in C_{n}.

Let
A=a^{-1}
that is
Aa=aA=1\mod n
. We show that

A\in C_{n}
.

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\left(\frac{A}{n}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{aA}{n}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{1}{n}\right)\equiv 1\pmod{n}\Rightarrow\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{A}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

(4.12)

Furthermore,

\displaystyle a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle(Aa)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv A^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

(4.13)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle 1\equiv A^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\equiv A^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}

\displaystyle\xRightarrow{\text{by Eq.~(\ref{sseq1})}}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{A}{n}\right)\equiv A^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n},

with the latter implying
A\in C_{n}
.
Given that
C_{n}
is a subgroup of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
,
the order of
C_{n}
divides that of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
.
If the two are not equal then

|C_{n}|\leq\frac{|\mathbb{U}_{n}|}{2}.

We dismiss the possibility
C_{n}=\mathbb{U}_{n}
.

(b) Show
C_{n}\neq\mathbb{U}_{n}
.

The proof is by contradiction (contrapositive).
Let us assume that
C_{n}=\mathbb{U}_{n}
.
Moreover, let
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}
.
We distinguish two cases for
n
.

Case 1:
a_{1}=1
.

For convenience rewrite

n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}=p_{1}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}=p_{1}Q,

where
\gcd(p_{1},Q)=1
.

Let
g
be a generator of

\mathbb{Z}_{p_{1}}^{*}=\mathbb{U}_{p_{1}}
. From prior results
we know it exists for prime
p_{1}
. (Note
n
is odd.)
Moreover
g^{\phi(p_{1})}=g^{p_{1}-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{1}}

and therefore the following is also true

g^{\frac{p_{1}-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{p_{1}},

by Lagrange’s theorem.
Then by the Chinese Remainder Theorem (CRT) there exists
an
a
such that

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle g\pmod{p_{1}},

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{Q}.

We then obtain the following.

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{a}{p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{a}{Q}\right).

For
\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}}\right)
since
a\equiv g\pmod{p_{1}}
we have

\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{g}{p_{1}}\right)\equiv g^{\frac{p_{1}-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{p_{1}}.

For
\left(\frac{a}{Q}\right)
we have by way of
a\equiv 1\pmod{Q}

the following

\left(\frac{a}{Q}\right)\equiv\left(\frac{1}{Q}\right)\equiv 1\pmod{Q}.

Therefore we conclude the following

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)=\left(\frac{a}{p_{1}}\right)\left(\frac{a}{Q}\right)=(-1)(+1)=-1.

If
a\in C_{n}
and
\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)=-1
this implies

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\equiv-1\pmod{n},

Then, we have the following, noting that
n=p_{1}Q
and thus

Q|n
and
\gcd(p_{1},Q)=1
.

\displaystyle a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle n|a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}+1

(4.14)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle Q|a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}+1

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv-1\pmod{Q}

By way of

a\equiv 1\pmod{Q}
we also have the following.

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv 1\pmod{Q},

(4.15)

and therefore combining Eq.
4.14
and Eq.
4.15

we obtain the following

2\equiv 0\pmod{Q},

which is only possible for
Q
being equal to 2, but this
contradicts the fact that
n=p_{1}Q
is an odd integer.

Case 2:
a_{1}\geq 2
.

If
a_{1}\geq 2
it means
a_{1}-1\geq 1
.
For
C_{n}=\mathbb{U}_{n}
,
it means

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}\Rightarrow a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n},

and by squaring we obtain the following

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}.

This last equation implies
n|a^{n-1}-1
.
Since
p_{1}^{a_{1}}
is a factor of
n
we also have
that
p_{1}^{a_{1}}|a^{n-1}-1
,
or equivalently

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{1}^{a_{1}}}.

By prior results the last one implies

\phi(p_{1}^{a_{1}})|n-1\Rightarrow p_{1}^{a_{1}-1}(p_{1}-1))|n-1\xRightarrow{\text{by $a_{1}>1$}}p_{1}|n-1.

In addition to
p_{1}|n-1
we have
p_{1}|n
as

p_{1}
is a factor of
n
. The two imply

p_{1}|1
i.e.
p_{1}=1
which contradicts the fact
that
p_{1}
is a prime number, and an
odd integer and thus
p_{1}\geq 3
.

(c) Conclude
C_{n}\neq\mathbb{U}_{n}
.

By case 1 and case 2 we conclude that

C_{n}\neq\mathbb{U}_{n}
.
Then

|C_{n}|\leq|\mathbb{U}_{n}|/2\leq(n-1)/2

Note that for prime
N

|\mathbb{U}_{n}|=\phi(n)=n-1
.

The probability that the output of Algorithm
8
is
Composite given that
n
is a composite number
is at least
1-2^{-t}
as Algorithm
8

repeats Algorithm
7
a number of
t
times.
The result then follows.
∎

Conrad
[
3
]
establishes Proposition
4.3

which is equivalent to Proposition
4.2
.

Proposition 4.3
(Probability of successful reporting
alternative of
[
3
]
)

If
n
is an odd composite number greater than two,
then Algorithm
7
returns Composite with
probability at least
1/2
.

Proof.

Consider the following three sets
B_{n}
,
C_{n}
,
D_{n}

defined as follows, with the first two
describing in fact Ew(n) and Enw(n).

Ew(n)=B_{n}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{U}_{n},a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\not\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}\right\},

Enw(n)=C_{n}=\left\{a:a\in\mathbb{U}_{n},a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}\right\},

Consider also set
D_{n}
defined as follows.

D_{n}=\left\{a:1\leq a<n\wedge\gcd(a,n)\neq 1\right\}.

We have
|B_{n}|\neq 0
,
1\in C_{n}
and

n-1\equiv-1\pmod{n}\in C_{n}
as well.

We show through group theory

C_{n}\neq\mathbb{U}_{n}
,
then implying

|C_{n}|\leq|\mathbb{U}_{n}|/2\leq(n-1)/2

The part that show that
C_{n}
is a subgroup
of
\mathbb{U}_{n}
is borrowed from the previous proof.

Step 1: Pick a
b\in B_{n}
and show that
C_{n}b\subseteq B_{n}
.

Given that
|B_{n}|\neq 0
, pick
b\in B_{n}
with
\gcd(b,n)=1

and consider set

C_{n}b=\left\{c\cdot b:c\in C_{n}\right\}.

We will show below that
C_{n}\subseteq B_{n}
.

Let
c\in C_{n}
with
\gcd(c,n)=1
.
Then

a^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)\pmod{n}.

Consider the
b\in B_{n}
with
\gcd(b,n)=1
.
Then we have
\gcd(bc,n)=1
.
We now prove that
bc\not\in C_{n}
.
We prove it by contrapositive/contradiction as
follows.

Let
bc\in C_{n}
. Then

(bc)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{bc}{n}\right)\pmod{n}.

We then derive the following

\displaystyle(bc)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\frac{bc}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

(4.16)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)\left(\frac{c}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

Moreover, we have the following.

\displaystyle(bc)^{\frac{n-1}{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle b^{\frac{n-1}{2}}c^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\pmod{n}

(4.17)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle b^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\left(\frac{c}{n}\right)\pmod{n}

Since
\gcd(c,n)=1
we have
\left(\frac{c}{n}\right)\neq 0
and thus
by way of Eq.
4.16
and Eq.
4.17
we obtain

b^{\frac{n-1}{2}}\equiv\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)\pmod{n},

which implies
b\in C_{n}
contradicting the assumption
that
b\in B_{n}
instead.
Therefore it can’ be
bc\in C_{n}
.
Thus it must be
bc\in B_{n}
for all
c\in C_{n}
.
Therefore we have proved
that

C_{n}b\subseteq B_{n}.

We know pick two different
c_{1}\neq c_{2}\in C_{n}
.

c_{1}b\equiv c_{2}b\pmod{n}\Rightarrow(c_{1}-c_{2}\equiv 0\pmod{n},

given that
\gcd(b,n)=1
. Moreover for
c_{1},c_{2}<n
,
we conclude that
c_{1}=c_{2}
.
Therefore we have the following.

|C_{n}|=|C_{n}b|\leq|B_{n}|.

If
n
is compositive for an odd
n
as the statement
emphatically states, then
n
has a prime factor
p

for which
\gcd(p,n)>1
. Then
p\in D_{n}
and thus

|D_{n}|\geq 1
.
We then obtain the following

\displaystyle n-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle|B_{n}|+|C_{n}|+|D_{n}|

(4.18)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|C_{n}|+|C_{n}|+|D_{n}|

(4.19)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle|C_{n}|+|C_{n}|+1

(4.20)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle 2|C_{n}|+1

(4.21)

\displaystyle|C_{n}|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{n-2}{2}.

(4.22)

The latter
|C_{n}|\leq(n-2)/2\leq(n-1)/2
, as needed.
∎

Lemma 4.6
(Running time of algorithm
8
)

The running time of
Algorithm
8
is

O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n)+t\lg^{3}{n})
, where
M(n)

is the cost of multiplying
n
-bit integers, and

O(\lg^{3}{n})
is the cost of computing the Jacobi symbol

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)
for
n
and an
a<n
.

Proof.

Exponentiation involves
O(\lg{n})
multiplications.
Depending on how we
implement integer multiplication the overall time complexity
is
O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n)+t\lg^{3}{n})
, where
M(n)

is the computational cost (bit model) of
multiplying
n
-bit integers, and
\lg^{3}{n}
is the
cost of computing the Jacobi symbol

\left(\frac{a}{n}\right)
for
n
and an
a<n
.
∎

Corollary 4.6
(SolovayStrassenPrimalityTest
\in
BPP)

Algorithm
8
belongs to class BPP.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
4.2
or Proposition
4.3
.
We shall map (call)
L
and
w
to

Prime
and
n
respectively.
If
n
is a prime number, it is decided by Algorithm
8

that it is a Prime (and output PseudoPrime is printed) with
probability 1.

If
n
is a composite number, it is decided by
Algorithm
8

that it is a Composite number (and output Composite is printed)
and thus rejected being a Prime/PseudoPrime with probability
at least
1-1/2^{t}
by way of Proposition
4.2
.

In other words,
n
can be wrongly decided by Algorithm
6

that it is a Prime (and output PseudoPrime is printed)
with probability at most
1/2^{t}
.
∎

4.6
Riemann hypothesis associated primality tests

4.6.1
Perfect powers

One of the first steps in Miller’s algorithm it is
to detect whether an integer
n
is a perfect power or
not. We say that natural number
n
is a perfect power
if there exist positive integers
x,m
such that
n=x^{m}
.

Definition 4.5
(Perfect power)

Given a natural integer number
n
we say

n
is a perfect power if there exist
a natural integer number
x
and
a natural integer number
m>1

such that

n=x^{m}

Lemma 4.7
(Perfect power algorithm)

One can use binary search and thus time polylogarithmic
in
n
to determine
x,m
. This is NaivePerfectPower(n).

Proof.

Since
n=x^{m}\geq 2^{m}
we observe
that
m\leq\lg{n}
or

m\leq\lfloor\lg{n}\rfloor
.
Thus the integer candidate values for
m

are
2,3,\ldots,\lfloor\lg{n}\rfloor
.
If
A=\lg{n}
the number of values
m
we
need to search for is at most
A-1
.

Input:

n
greater than 2

Output:

x,m
such that
x^{m}=n
or
\mathbf{NotaPPower}

A=\lg{n}

;

/*
Bound for
m
is
A
*/

1

for

i=2,3,\ldots,\lfloor A\rfloor

do

2

3

m=i
;

4

x=BinarySearch([1,y],m)

;

/*
Determine
1<x\leq n
such that
x^{m}<n
,
(x+1)^{m}\geq n
*/

5

if

x^{m}==n

then

6

return

\mathbf{(x,m)}

;

/*
Perfect Power */

end if

7

8

if

(x+1)^{m}==n

then

9

return

\mathbf{(x+1,m)}

;

/*
Perfect Power */

end if

10

11

end for

12

13

return

\mathbf{NotaPPower}

14

Algorithm 9

NaivePerfectPower(n) : Determine if
n
is a perfect power

∎

4.6.2
Miller primality test under GRH : Miller1

Miller (
[
6
]
) describes two deterministic
algorithms that work correctly under the Generalized
Riemann Hypothesis (GRH). Miller cites the
Extended Riemann Hypothesis for a Dirichlet function;
the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis for Dirichlet
L
-functions
is equivalent to the Extended Riemann hypothesis for the
problem in hand.
The first algorithm, that will be referred to as Miller1,
appears on page 303 of
[
6
]
.
The second algorithm, that will be referred to as Miller2,
appears on page 308 of
[
6
]
.
Miller1 is the deterministic algorithm
turned by Rabin
[
8
]
into a probabilistic algorithm,
and analyzed in
[
8
]
.
This probabilistic algorithm will be referred to as
Rabin-Miller.
Monier
[
7
]
proved that the conditions used
in Miller1 and Miller2 are equivalent.
The probabilistic version of Miller2 will be referred
to as the Miller-Rabin algorithm. Because of the equivalence
of the conditions of Miller1 and Miller2, the Rabin analysis
originally for the probabilistic algorithm of the modified
Miller1 applies also to the modified Miller2.
Miller1 and also Algorithm
11
known as
Miller2, if the GRH is incorrect, might generate an
incorrect answer.
This uncertainty is not present in
Miller-Rabin or Rabin-Miller.

Theorem 4.7

(Miller
[
6
]
, pages 303-304)

Algorithm
10
, Miller1, is a deterministic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is prime or composite, under the GRH.

Proof.

Algorithm
10
appears on pages 303-304 of
[

]
.
Miller1 and also Algorithm

known as
Miller2, if the GRH is incorrect, might generate an
incorrect answer.

cited by Miller in
[

]
is
never explicitly calculated. Under GRH, Bach
[
2
]

calculated the constant

2
.
In Miller1 and Miller2 we thus use

from Bach
[

]
.

Input:

is odd;
n-1=2^{k}l
,

odd

is
\mathbf{Composite}
or
\mathbf{Prime}

;

;

Bach

]
calculated

*/

1

\text{PerfectPower}(n)

then

/*
Line 1 */

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
n
is a perfect power
n=p^{s}
,

*/

5

/*

4.6.3

Theorem 4.8

(Miller
[

]
, page 308)

Algorithm
11
, Miller2, is a deterministic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is prime or composite, under the GRH.

Proof.

Input:

n>4
is odd
n-1=2^{k}l
,

odd

n
is
\mathbf{Composite}
or
\mathbf{Prime}

n-1=2^{k}l
;

;

/*

]

*/

2

(n)

/*
Line 1 */

\mathbf{Composite}

n
is a perfect power

,

*/

;

/*

is a witness of

’s compositeness */

\mathbf{Prime}
