4.7
Probabilistic primality tests

4.7.1
The Miller-Rabin primality test

The following lemma is established. It is used
to establish Proposition
4.4
and
prove the correctness of Algorithm
13
.
A condition expressed by Eq.(
4.23
) will have
cases
P_{2a},P_{2b}
merged into Case
P_{2}
.
Therefore the condition of Eq.(
4.23
)
will appear as in Eq.(
4.24
)
in Lemma
4.8
.

\text{n is prime}\Rightarrow\forall x,1\leq x<n:\begin{cases}x^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }P_{1}(x,n)\\
\vee&\\
x^{l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }P_{2a}(x,n,m)\\
\vee&\\
x^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n},\quad\exists m:0<m<k&\text{Case }P_{2b}\\
\end{cases}

(4.23)

Lemma 4.8

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be an odd integer with
n>2
.
Let
n-1=2^{k}\cdot l
, where
k\geq 1
, and
l
odd.
For all
x
such that
1\leq x<n

we have the following.

\text{n is prime}\Rightarrow\forall x,1\leq x<n:\begin{cases}x^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }P_{1}(x,n)\\
\vee&\\
x^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n},\quad\exists m:0\leq m<k&\text{Case }P_{2}(x,n,m)\\
\end{cases}

(4.24)

Proof.

If
n
is a prime number then Fermat’s little theorem is invoked.
For any
x
such that
1\leq x<n
and we note that then

\gcd(x,n)=1
we have the following.

x^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}\Rightarrow x^{n-1}-1\equiv 0\pmod{n}.

We note that
n-1=2^{k}l
as stated therefore

n-1=\frac{n-1}{2}\cdot 2=2^{k-1}l+2^{k-1}l=2\cdot(2^{k-1}l).

Therefore we have the following

\displaystyle x^{n-1}-1=x^{2^{k}l}-1=x^{2\cdot 2^{k-1}l}-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}-1\right)

(4.25)

We continue unrolling the right-most term a total of
k
times.

\displaystyle x^{n-1}-1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}-1\right)

(4.26)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-2}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-2}l}-1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-2}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-3}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-3}l}-1\right)

\displaystyle\ldots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-2}l}+1\right)\ldots\left(x^{2l}+1\right)\left(x^{l}+1\right)\left(x^{l}-1\right).

Moreover,
x^{n-1}-1\equiv 0\pmod{n}
implies the following.

\displaystyle x^{n-1}-1

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\left(x^{2^{k-1}l}+1\right)\left(x^{2^{k-2}l}+1\right)\ldots\left(x^{2l}+1\right)\left(x^{l}+1\right)\left(x^{l}-1\right)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{n}

(4.27)

We perform a case analysis.

One possibility that generates
P_{1}
is that

n|x^{l}-1
, the right-most term.
Then
x^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
If this is not the case, then
n
might divide the second
term from the right that is,

n|x^{l}+1
. Then
x^{l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
This is what was called case
P_{2a}
or it is the first
case of
P_{2}
for
m=0
.
If this is not the case, then
n
might divide the third
term from the right that is,

n|x^{2l}+1
. Then
x^{2l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
This is the first case of
P_{2b}
with
m=1
.
Continuing like this, if it is not the case that

n
divides
x^{2^{k-2}l}+1
, then

n|x^{2^{k-1}l}+1
. Then
x^{2^{k-1}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
This is the last case of case
P_{2b}
with
m=k-1
.

Note that
x^{2^{k-1}l}
is the square of
x^{2^{k-2}l}

and so on, and
x^{2l}
is the square of
x^{l}
mod
n
.
Thus the sequence implied is as follows if listed
right to left.

x^{l}\bmod n,x^{2l}\bmod n,x^{2^{2}l}\bmod n,\ldots x^{2^{k-1}l}\bmod n

(4.28)

The terms as shown in this order can be generated by squaring
mod
n
starting with the left-most term
x^{l}
mod
n
.
If
x^{l}
is equivalent to one mod
n
so are all subsequent terms.
If
x^{2^{m}l}
is equivalent to
1
mod
n
so are all
subsequent terms
x^{2^{j}l}
,
m\leq j<k
because of squaring.
If
x^{2^{m}l}
is
-1
mod
n
all subsequent terms

x^{2^{j}l}
,
m<j<k
, are
1
mod
n
.
For the sequence
This implies that in the sequence of Eq. (
4.28
)
there is only one
-1
followed by (a suffix) of ones to the end
of it or the whole sequence is a sequence of ones..

In conclusion if
n
is prime then we have
P_{1}
or
P_{2}
that is

\text{n is prime}\Rightarrow\forall x,1\leq x<n:P_{1}(x,n)\vee\exists 0\leq m<k:P_{2}(x,n,m).

(4.29)

If
n
is a composite number then we have

\overline{P_{1}\vee P_{2}}=\overline{P_{1}}\wedge\overline{P_{2}}
.

\text{n is composite}\Rightarrow\exists x,1\leq x<n:\overline{P_{1}(x,n)}\wedge\forall m,0\leq m<k:\overline{P_{2}(x,n,m)}.

(4.30)

This is equivalent to the following one.

\text{n is composite}\Leftrightarrow\exists x,1\leq x<n:\begin{cases}x^{l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }\overline{P_{1}(x,n)}\\
\wedge&\\
x^{2^{m}l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n},\quad\forall m,0\leq m<k:&\text{Case }\overline{P_{2}(x,n,m)}\\
\end{cases}

(4.31)

Later one we will rename

\displaystyle C_{1}(x,n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{P_{1}(x,n)}

(4.32)

\displaystyle C_{2}(x,n,m)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\overline{P_{2}(x,n,m)}.

(4.33)

∎

We refine Lemma
4.8
to match the corresponding
lines of Algorithm
13
.

Proposition 4.4

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be an odd integer with
n>4
.
Let
n-1=2^{k}\cdot l
, where
k\geq 1
, and
l
odd.
Show then,
integer
n
is declared a
PseudoPrime
number if and only
if for every
a
such that
2\leq a<n-1
and
n\nmid a

either

\displaystyle P_{1}(a,n)=\overline{C_{1}(a,n)}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

(4.34)

or

\displaystyle P_{2}(a,n,m)=\overline{C_{2}(a,n,m)}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n},

(4.35)

for some integer
m
such that
0\leq m<k
.

PseudoPrime
means it is either a prime number or

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.

We note that the conditions of Proposition
4.4

are Eq. (
4.29
) of Lemma
4.8
for

x=a
.
Therefore the proof of correctness
directly follows from Lemma
4.8
.
The proof of correctness of Proposition
4.4

deals with the necessary and sufficient part,
whereas Lemma
4.8
did no elaborate on the latter.

Proof.

\Rightarrow
(Sufficient condition.)

This is Lemma
4.8
summarized.
Let
n
be a prime number. Then by Fermat’s
little theorem we have the following

a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

for all
a\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
n\nmid a
.
Thefore we have the following.

\displaystyle a^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a^{2^{k}l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle(a^{2^{k-1}l}-1)(a^{2^{k-1}l}+1)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{n}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle a^{2^{k-1}l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\pm 1\pmod{n}.

The last modular equation gives one of the following.

n|a^{2^{k-1}l}-1
or

n|a^{2^{k-1}l}+1
, for a prime number
p
.
We distinguish and examine the two cases separately.

Case 1.
If
a^{2^{k-1}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
, we are done.
This is the second case in the statement.

Case 2.
If
a^{2^{k-1}l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}
, then

a^{2^{k-1}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
, and we repeat the same
argument to conclude

n|a^{2^{k-2}l}-1
or

n|a^{2^{k-2}l}+1
.

Likewise, at some point
a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{p}
, for
some
m
such that
0\leq m<k
, and we stop by
way of the second case of the statement again, or we
exhaust the
2^{m}
to reach

a^{l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
,
or

a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
In the former case, we reach the second case of the statement
for
m=0
; in the latter case we reach the first case of
the statement. In either case we are done. Sufficient
condition proved.

\Leftarrow
(Necessary condition.)

We distinguish two cases.

Case 1.

Let

a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
. Then we have the following.

\displaystyle a^{l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle a^{2^{k}l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}\Rightarrow

\displaystyle a^{n-1}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}.

Case 2.

Let

a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
Then we also observe the following.

a^{2^{m}l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
,
since otherwise
n=2
, and
n
is trivially prime.
We then have the following.

\displaystyle a^{2^{m}l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-1\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle a^{n-1}=\left(a^{2^{m}l}\right)^{2^{k-m}}=\left(-1\right)^{2^{k-m}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n},

since
m<k
and thus
k-m>0
and
2^{k-m}\geq 2
.
∎

Algorithm
13

performs
t
times the tasks of
Algorithm
12
which is a simpler
probabilistic algorithm.
Algorithm
12
checks for a given
a

condition
C_{1}(a,n)
and for all values
m

condition
C_{2}(a,n,m)
. The range of values
m

depends on
n
since
n-1=2^{k}\cdot l
, where
k\geq 1

and
l
odd determines the range of
m
as
follows:
0\leq m<k
.
Algorithm
13
repeats
Algorithm
12
a number of times equal
to
t
by drawing uniformly at random an
a

from the range
\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
.
Algorithm
12
is weak algorithm and
for this we call
it a compositeness test algorithm.
The latter Algorithm
13
is a more reliable
one and is referred to as a primality test algorithm.

Theorem 4.9

Algorithm
13

is a probabilistic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is composite or not.

Proof.

Let
P_{1}(a,n)
and
P_{2}(a,n,m)
be the two conditions
of Proposition
4.4
as indicated in
Eq. (
4.29
) of Lemma
4.8
for
x=a
.
Let
C_{1}(a,n)
and
C_{2}(a,n,m)
be the two conditions of
Eq.(
4.32
) and Eq.(
4.33
) established respectively
from Eq. (
4.31
) of Lemma
4.8
for
x=a
.
They are also in negation form part of
Eq.(
4.34
) and Eq.(
4.35
) in Proposition
4.4
.

In line 2 of Algorithm
12
,
or line 4 of Algorithm
13
,
an
a
is picked uniformly at random such that
2\leq a<n-1
.

Step 1.

The condition
n\nmid a
of Proposition
4.4

translates into
\gcd(a,n)>1
since
a<n
,
and this maps to lines 3-5 of Algorithm
12
,
or lines 5-7 of Algorithm
13
.
If
n
is composite the algorithm terminates with the
correct answer Composite and
a
becomes a witness of
the compositeness of
n
.

We are left with a sequence of steps to determine other
wise the compositeness of
n
for an
a
such that

\gcd(a,n)=1
.

Step 2.

If

C_{1}(a,n)\equiv\overline{P_{1}(a,n)}:\quad a^{l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n},

and

C_{2}(a,n,m)\equiv\overline{P_{2}(a,n,m)}:a^{2^{m}l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n},\ \forall m,0\leq m<k,

report
n
to be composite and
a
to be the witness of its
compositeness. This is a consequence of Eq.(
4.31
)
of Lemma
4.8
.

Step 3.
Otherwise
n
is either prime or composite.

A single execution of Steps 1, 2, and 3 is
Algorithm
12
.
Repeat Step 1,2, and 3 a total of
t
times and this
leads to Algorithm
13
.

Algorithm
13
tests for the two conditions

C_{1}(a,n)
and
C_{2}(a,n,m)
a number of times.
We denote the number of times these conditions are tested as
t
.
Every time these conditions are tested we pick a random
a

from the interval/set
\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
of cardinality
n-1
.
If for the chosen
a

\overline{P_{1}(a,n)}
and

\overline{P_{2}(a,n,m)}
for all applicable
m

then
n
is composite and
a
is
the Rabin-Miller witness of the compositeness of
n
and
denoted as MRw(a,n), or the set of witnesses for
n
is
denoted as MRw(n). Otherwise
a
is a non-witness denoted
MRnw(a,n) and the corresponding set MRnw(n).
To simplify things we used

C_{1}(a,n)=\overline{P_{1}(a,n)}

and

C_{2}(a,n,m)=\overline{P_{2}(a,n,m)}
.

MRw(a,n)\equiv a\in MRw(n)\Leftrightarrow C_{1}(a,n)\wedge\left(\wedge_{m=0}^{k-1}C_{2}(a,n,m)\right).

We obtain the following condition for
non witnesses MRnw(a,n) or set MRnw(n).

MRnw(a,n)\equiv a\in MRnw(n)\Leftrightarrow\overline{C_{1}(a,n)}\vee\left(\vee_{m=0}^{k-1}\overline{C_{2}(a,n,m)}\right)\Leftrightarrow P_{1}(a,n)\vee\left(\vee_{m=0}^{k-1}P_{2}(a,n,m)\right).

Proposition 4.5
(Probability of successful reporting)

If
n
is an odd composite number greater than four
then Algorithm
13
returns Composite with
probability at least
3/4
.
The probability that the output of Algorithm
13
is
Composite given that
n
is a composite number
is at least
1-4^{-t}
.

Proof.

It was proved by Rabin
[
8
]
and independently
by Monier
[
7
]
that for a composite odd
n
,

|MRw(a)|
is at least
3\phi(n)/4
and
|MRnw(a)|

is thus at most
\phi(n)/4
.
But the result is applicable to Miller1.
Algorithm
13
uses the conditions of
Miller2.
Monier
[
7
]
proved that the conditions used
in Miller1 and Miller2 are equivalent.
So we can use for this algorithm the claims of
Rabin
[
8
]
and also of Monier
[
7
]
.
Later, we introduce RabinMiller which was
analyzed by Rabin
[
8
]
.
∎

Lemma 4.9
(Running time of algorithm
13
)

The running time of
Algorithm
13
is

O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n))
, where
M(n)

is the cost of multiplying
n
-bit integers.

Proof.

Exponentiation involves
O(\lg{n})
multiplications.
Depending on how we
implement integer multiplication the overall time complexity
is
O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n))
, where
M(n)

is the computational cost (bit model) of
multiplying
n
-bit integers.
∎

4.7.3
Equivalence of Miller1 and Miller2 conditions

Algorithm
10
was utilized by Rabin
to derive the probabilistic primality testing
algorithm of
[
8
]
that we will refer to as Rabin-Miller to
distinguish it from Miller-Rabin.

Algorithm
10
does not use the
conditions of Eq.(
4.34
) and Eq.(
4.35
),
where

\displaystyle P_{1}(a,n)=\overline{C_{1}(a,n)}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

(4.36)

\displaystyle P_{2}(a,n,m)=\overline{C_{2}(a,n,m)}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n},

(4.37)

It uses instead the following conditions of Eq.(
4.41
)
and Eq.(
4.40
), where

\displaystyle R_{1}(a,n)=\overline{M_{1}(a,n)}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n},

(4.38)

\displaystyle R_{2}(a,n,m)=\overline{M_{2}(a,n,m)}

\displaystyle:

\displaystyle d_{m}\neq 1\wedge d_{n}\neq n.

(4.39)

where
d_{m}=\gcd(a^{2^{m}\cdot l}-1,n)
,
m\in\mathbb{N}
.

Then instead of using Eq.(
4.24
) and Eq.(
4.31
)
it establishes a new set of conditions.
We first rewrite Eq.(
4.24
) and Eq.(
4.31
), the
conditions used by Miller 2.

\text{n is prime}\Rightarrow\forall a,1\leq a<n:{\small\begin{cases}a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }P_{1}(a,n)\\
\vee&\\
a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n},\hskip 9.24994pt\exists m:0\leq m<k&\text{Case }P_{2}(a,n,m)\\
\end{cases}}

\text{n is composite}\Leftrightarrow\exists a,1\leq a<n:{\small\begin{cases}a^{l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }\overline{P_{1}(a,n)}\\
\wedge&\\
a^{2^{m}l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n},\hskip 9.24994pt\forall m,0\leq m<k:&\text{Case }\overline{P_{2}(a,n,m)}\\
\end{cases}}

Then we write the new set of conditions used by Miller1.

\text{n is prime}\Rightarrow\forall a:\quad n\nmid a\wedge\quad{\small\begin{cases}a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }M_{1}(a,n)=\overline{R_{1}(a,n)}\\
\wedge&\\
\forall m,0\leq m<k:d_{m}=1\vee d_{m}=n&\text{Case }M_{2}(a,n,m)=\overline{R_{2}(a,n,m)}\\
\end{cases}}

(4.40)

\text{n is composite}\Leftrightarrow\exists a:\quad n|a\quad\vee\quad{\small\begin{cases}a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }R_{1}(a,n)\\
\vee&\\
\exists m,0\leq m<k:d_{m}\neq 1\wedge d_{m}\neq n&\text{Case }R_{2}(a,n,m)\\
\end{cases}}

(4.41)

Proposition 4.6
(Equivalence of condition in Miller1 and Miller 2)

Let
n
be an odd number
n>4
and that

n-1=2^{k}\cdot l
,
k\geq 1
and
l
is odd.
The conditions of Eq.(
4.40
) and Eq.(
4.41
)
utilized by MillerRabin are equivalent to
the conditions of Eq.(
4.24
) and Eq.(
4.31
)
utilized by RabinMiller.

Proof.

For both set of conditions we prove the equivalence
for
n
being a prime number. The case
n
is composite
can be proved by symmetry using De Morgan’s Law.
Consider the sequence of the
d
values forming the following
row vector in that case.

(d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{k-1},d_{k}).

We will show first that if a
d_{j}
value is
n
then all
the values to its right will also be
n
that is

d_{i}=n
for all
i>j
.
Thus
1
s can extend only to the left of the leftmost
n
.
As a conclusion the row vector can be: (a) all
n
, or
(b) a sequence of consecutive 1s followed by consecutive

n
s,
and (c) the rightmost position is
n
.

Condition
M_{1}(a,n)
.
By way of
M_{1}(a,n)
we have the
following

\displaystyle a^{n-1}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle n|a^{n-1}-1\Rightarrow n|a^{2^{k}l}-1

(4.42)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle a^{2^{k}l}-1\equiv 0\pmod{n}\Rightarrow\gcd(a^{2^{k}l}-1,n)=n

\displaystyle\Rightarrow

\displaystyle M_{a}(a,k):d_{k}=n.

Therefore we obtain that the right-most position
of the row vector has
d_{k}=n
.

(d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{k-1},n=d_{k}).

Condition
M_{2}(a,n,m)
case (i):
for some
0\leq j<k
:
d_{j}=n
.

We prove that a
n
in some position
j
is followed
to the right with a block of
n
s.
Consider that for some
m
,
call it
j
, we have for
M_{2}(a,n,j)
the following:

d_{j}=n
.
This implies

d_{j}=n=\gcd(a^{2^{j}l}-1,n)
, and also

a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
Since
a^{2^{j+1}l}=(a^{2^{j}l})^{2}
,

a^{2^{j+1}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
,
and therefore

\gcd(a^{2^{j+1}l}-1,n)=d_{j+1}=n
, thus having

d_{j+1}=n
.
By induction, all
i>j
have
d_{i}=n
as well.
Therefore we obtain the following refinement for case (i).

(d_{0},d_{1},\ldots,d_{j-1},d_{j}=n,d_{j+1}=n,\ldots,d_{k-1}=n,d_{k}=n).

If
j=0
we have an all
n
row-vector.

(d_{0}=n,d_{1}=n,\ldots,d_{k-1}=n,d_{k}=n).

We conclude that
d_{j}=n
is equivalent to

a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.

Condition
M_{2}(a,n,m)
case (ii): if the left-most

n
is at position
j>0
i.e.
d_{j}=n
there is a

d_{j-1}=1
.

Consider that for some
m
,
call it
j
, we have for
M_{2}(a,n,j)
the following:

d_{j}=n
and it is the leftmost
n
entry of the row vector.
The latter implies

\gcd(a^{2^{j}l}-1,n)=d_{j}=n
or
a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
Let us consider
m=j-1
. We have
d_{j-1}=1
.

Let us set
x=a^{2^{j-1}l}
.
Then
x^{2}=a^{2^{j}l}
. Since
a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
,
we have
x^{2}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.

\displaystyle x^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{n}

\displaystyle(x-1)(x+1)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{n}

\displaystyle(x-1)(x+1)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

\displaystyle(x+1)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle-1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}},

and given
\gcd(p_{u}^{a_{u}},p_{v}^{a_{v}})=1
, we have

x\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
We need to explain the step that led to the dismisal
of
(x-1)
. We claimed that

\gcd(p_{i}^{a_{i}},x-1)=1
.
This is because of the following.

\displaystyle 1=d_{j-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(a^{2^{j-1}l}-1,n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(x-1,n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(x-1,p_{i}^{a_{i}})\forall i

In conclusion
a^{2^{j-1}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
On the left of a 1 we can only have 1. If there
was an
n
then to its right there should have been
an
n
and not an
1
.
We conclude that
d_{j}=1
is equivalent to

a^{2^{j}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
Now that we examined the cases for
M_{1}(a,n)
and

M_{2}(a,n,m)
, we move to
P_{1}(a,n)
and
P_{2}(a,n,m)
.

Mapping
M_{1},M_{2}
to
P_{1},P_{2}
.

A row vector that has
n
in the left-most position
means

d_{0}=n
which is equivalent to

a^{2^{0}l}\equiv a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
This is case
P_{1}(a,n)
.
A row vector that has
1
in the left-most position
m=0

and
n
in position
m=1
, by prior discussion (case (ii))

a^{l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
which is
P_{2}(a,n,0)
.
If the right-most 1 is in position
j-1
then

a^{2^{j-1}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
which is
P_{2}(a,n,j-1)
.
The rightmost
m
for a
d_{j}=1
is
j=k-1
. Since
d_{k}=n

this maps to
a^{2^{k-1}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
which is
P_{2}(a,n,k-1)
.
There is a 1-1 map of
M_{1}(a,n)\mapsto P_{1}(a,n)

and
M_{2}(a,n,m)\mapsto P_{2}(a,n,m)
.
This concludes the proof.

Supplement 1:
Show
a^{2^{j+1}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

if
a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
Let
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}\ldots p_{r}^{a_{r}}
be the prime decomposition
of
n
.

a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
implies for every
i=1,\ldots,r

that

a^{2^{j}l}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}
.

\displaystyle a^{2^{j}l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

\displaystyle(a^{2^{j}l}-1)^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

\displaystyle a^{2^{j+1}l}+1-2a^{2^{j+1}}l

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

\displaystyle a^{2^{j+1}l}+1-2

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 0\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}

\displaystyle a^{2^{j+1}l}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}},

and given
\gcd(p_{u}^{a_{u}},p_{v}^{a_{v}})=1
, we have

a^{2^{j+1}l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
i.e.
d_{j+1}=n
.

∎

4.7.4
The Rabin-Miller primality test

Rabin
[
8
]
converted Algorithm
10

into a probabilistic primality test algorithm.

Proposition 4.7
(Rabin
[
8
]
)

Algorithm
15
is a probabilistic algorithm
for testing whether
n
is prime or composite.

Proof.

Let
W(a,n)

[
8
]
indicate that
a
is a witness of the compositeness
of
n
. Then denote by
W(n)
the set of all
W(a,n)
witnesses.
Later on we we will use the terminology
MRw(a,n)
and
MRw(n)
.
for
W(a,n)
and
W(n)
respectively.

W(n)=\left\{a:W(a,n)\right\}.

It was proved in
[
8
]
that for
n>4
and
n
composite

|W(n)|\geq\frac{3(n-1)}{4}

In a theorem to follow, it will be shown that

|MRnw(n)|\leq\frac{\phi(n)}{4}\leq\frac{n-1}{4},

that implies

|MRw(n)|\geq\frac{3(n-1)}{4}.

The proof of correctness appears in
[
6
]
and the
derivations above
[
8
]
.
We present later a proof that is based on
[
5
]
,
with the latter originating from the paper of
[
7
]
.

Input:

n>4
is odd
n-1=2^{k}l
,
l
odd; number of runs is
t

Output:

n
is
\mathbf{Composite}
or
\mathbf{PseudoPrime}

n-1=2^{k}l
;

i=0
;

1

if

PerfectPower(n)

then

/*
Line 1 */

2

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
[
8
]
is unclear about this test */

end if

3

4

do

5

i=i+1
;

6

Pick
a\in\{2,3,\ldots,n-2\}
uniformly at random ;

7

8

if

(a|n)||(\gcd(a,n)>1)

then

/*
Line 2 (i) */

9

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
[
8
]
is unclear about this test */

end if

10

11

x=a^{l}\pmod{n}
;

12

Calculate using repeated doubling
a^{2^{i}l}\bmod n
, for
i=1,\ldots,k

;

/*
a^{2^{k}l}\bmod n
is
a^{n-1}\bmod n
*/

13

if

a^{n-1}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}

then

/*
Line 2 (ii) */

14

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a
W(a,n)
*/

end if

15

16

if

\exists m,0\leq m<k:\gcd((a^{2^{k}l}\bmod n)-1,n)\neq 1,n

then

/*
Line 2 (iii) */

17

return

\mathbf{Composite}

;

/*
a
is a
W(a,n)
*/

end if

18

19

while

i<t
;

20

return

\mathbf{PseudoPrime}

;

/*
Line 3 */

21

Algorithm 15

Rabin-Miller primality test algorithm

∎

Proposition 4.8
(Probability of successful reporting)

If
n
is an odd composite number greater than four
then Algorithm
15
returns Composite with
probability at least
3/4
.
The probability that the output of Algorithm
15
is
Composite given that
n
is a composite number
is at least
1-4^{-t}
.

Proof.

It was proved by Rabin
[
8
]
and independently
by Monier
[
7
]
that for a composite odd
n
,

|MRw(a)|
is at least
3\phi(n)/4
and
|MRnw(a)|

is thus at most
\phi(n)/4
.
∎

Lemma 4.10
(Running time of algorithm
15
)

The running time of
Algorithm
15
is

O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n))
, where
M(n)

is the cost of multiplying
n
-bit integers.

Proof.

Exponentiation involves
O(\lg{n})
multiplications.
Depending on how we
implement integer multiplication the overall time complexity
is
O(t\cdot\lg{n}\cdot M(n))
, where
M(n)

is the computational cost (bit model) of
multiplying
n
-bit integers.
∎

4.7.5
Examples on witnesses

Example 4.7.1

Consider
n=9
, a composite and odd integer
n>4
.
It is
n-1=2^{k}\cdot l=2^{3}\cdot 1
, with
l

being an odd integer.
Determine the MRw(n) and MRnw(n).

Solution.

We note that
n=9
, we have
n-1=2^{k}\cdot l
, where

k=3
and
l=1
.
The first column is
a
.
The second column shows
a^{l}\bmod n
.
The third column indicates whether
C_{1}(a,n)
is the case i.e.

a^{l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
A
+
indicates so, a
-
indicates the opposite i.e.

a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
The fourth column indicates whether
C_{2}(a,n,0)
is the case i.e.

a^{l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
A
+
indicates so, a
-
indicates the opposite i.e.

a^{l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.

The fifth column shows
a^{2^{1}\cdot l}\bmod n
.
The sixth column indicates with a
+

whether
C_{2}(a,n,1)
is the case i.e.

a^{2^{1}\cdot l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}
;
otherwise a
-
is shown.
The seventh column shows
a^{2^{2}\cdot l}\bmod n
.
The eighth column indicates with a
+

whether
C_{2}(a,n,2)
is the case i.e.

a^{2^{2}\cdot l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}
;
otherwise a
-
is shown.
In the ninth column a
+
indicates that
a

is a witness, and an
-
that
a
is a non-witness
to the compositeness of
n
.

For
a\in MRw(n)
we need only
+
’s for the corresponding row.
For
a\in MRnw(n)
we need at least one
-
or equivalently
an
n
in the MRw(n) column.

\begin{array}[]{llcclclcc}a&a^{l}:&C_{1}(a,n)&C_{2}(a,n,0)&a^{2^{1}\cdot 1}:&C_{2}(a,n,1)&a^{2^{2}\cdot 1}:&C_{2}(a,n,2)&MRw(n)\\
1&1&-&+&1&+&1&+&n\\
2&2&+&+&4&+&7&+&y\\
3&3&+&+&0&+&0&+&y\\
4&4&+&+&7&+&4&+&y\\
5&5&+&+&7&+&4&+&y\\
6&6&+&+&0&+&0&+&y\\
7&7&+&+&4&+&7&+&y\\
8&8&+&-&1&+&1&+&n\\
\end{array}

We observe the number of MRw(a,n) is
\geq\frac{3(n-1)}{4}=6
.
The number of MRnw(a,n) is
\leq\frac{(n-1)}{4}=2
.
∎

Example 4.7.2

Consider
n=13
, a non-composite (!!) and odd integer
n>2
.
It is
n-1=2^{2}\cdot 3
. Using the prior definition
we have
k=2
and
l=3
, with
l
being a odd number.
Determine the MRw(n) and MRnw(n).

Solution.

We note that
n=13
, we have
n-1=2^{2}\cdot 3
, where

k=2
and
l=3
.
The first column is
a
.
The second column shows
a^{l}\bmod n
.
The third column indicates whether
C_{1}(a,n)
is the case i.e.

a^{l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
A
+
indicates so, a
-
indicates the opposite i.e.

a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.
The fourth column indicates whether
C_{2}(a,n,0)
is the case i.e.

a^{l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
A
+
indicates so, a
-
indicates the opposite i.e.

a^{l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.

The fifth column shows
a^{2^{1}\cdot l}\bmod n
.
The sixth column indicates with a
+

whether
C_{2}(a,n,1)
is the case i.e.

a^{2^{1}\cdot l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}
;
otherwise a
-
is shown.
In the seventh column a
+
indicates that
a

is a witness, and an
-
that
a
is a non-witness
to the compositeness of
n
.

For
a\in MRw(n)
we need only
+
’s for the corresponding row.
For
a\in MRnw(n)
we need at least one
-
or equivalently
an
n
in the MRw(n) column.
There are no witnesses to the compositeness of
13
; we know
that
13
is a prime number not a composite number!
We observe the number of MRw(a,n) is
0
.
The number of MRnw(a,n) is thus
n-1=12
.

\begin{array}[]{llcclcc}a&a^{l}:&C_{1}(a,n)&C_{2}(a,n,0)&a^{2^{1}\cdot 1}:&C_{2}(a,n,1)&MRw(n)\\
1&1&-&+&1&+&n\\
2&8&+&+&12&-&n\\
3&1&-&+&1&+&n\\
4&12&+&-&1&+&n\\
5&8&+&+&12&-&n\\
6&8&+&+&12&-&n\\
7&5&+&+&12&-&n\\
8&5&+&+&12&-&n\\
9&1&-&+&1&+&n\\
10&12&+&-&1&+&n\\
11&5&+&+&12&-&n\\
12&12&+&-&1&+&n\\
\end{array}

∎

4.7.6
Solovay-Strassen and Miller-Rabin failure probability bounds

Both in the
t
-round
probabilistic version of the Solovay-Strassen test
and
probabilistic version of the Rabin-Miller test
we derive bounds on the probability of

P_{SolStr}(E_{1}/E_{2})\leq\frac{1}{2^{t}},

P_{RabMil}(E_{1}/E_{2})\leq\frac{1}{4^{t}},

where

E_{1}:\text{event that $t$ runs of the algorithm produce NO witnesses},

E_{2}:\text{$n$ is a composite number},

and of course

E_{2}^{\prime}:\text{$n$ is a prime number}.

However we are interested in the probability

P_{SolStr}(E_{2}/E_{1}),

and

P_{RabMil}(E_{2}/E_{1}),

instead.

Proposition 4.9
(
[
3
]
)

For terms as previously defined,
by using Bayes theorem we conclude (
[
3
]
) the following.

P_{SolStr}(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})\geq 1-\frac{\ln{n}-1}{2^{t}}

and

P_{RabMil}(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})\geq 1-\frac{\ln{n}-1}{4^{t}},

or equivalently

P_{SolStr}(E_{2}/E_{1})\leq\frac{\ln{n}-1}{2^{t}}

and

P_{RabMil}(E_{2}/E_{1})\leq\frac{\ln{n}-1}{4^{t}},

Proof.

Bayes theorem simple form.

The simple form of Bayes theorem for two event
A
and
B
with
P(B)\neq 0
takes the
following form.

P(A/B)=\frac{P(B/A)P(A)}{P(B)}.

A proof is based on conditional probability. Therefore for events
A,B
we
have the following.

P(A\cap B)=P(A/B)P(B),\quad\quad P(A\cap B)=P(B/A)P(A).

Equating the right-hand sides and solving for
P(A/B)
given
P(B)\neq 0
gives
the desired result.

Bayes theorem for two mutually exclusive and exhaustive events
A
and
A^{\prime}
.

We denote
A^{\prime}
the complement of
A
with
A\cap A^{\prime}=\emptyset
and

A\cup A^{\prime}=\Omega
that is
A
and
A^{\prime}
are mutually exclusive and exhaustive.
Then

P(A/B)=\frac{P(B/A)P(A)}{P(B)}=\frac{P(B/A)P(A)}{P(B/A)P(A)+P(B/A^{\prime})P(A^{\prime})}.

(4.43)

Likewise we can also have the following.

P(A^{\prime}/B)=\frac{P(B/A^{\prime})P(A^{\prime})}{P(B/A)P(A)+P(B/A^{\prime})P(A^{\prime})}.

(4.44)

We map
A,A^{\prime},B
to the events
E_{2},E_{2}^{\prime},E_{1}
.

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle E_{2},\quad\text{$n$ is Composite event},

\displaystyle A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle E_{2}^{\prime},\quad\text{$n$ is Prime event},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle E_{1}.\quad\text{NoWitness event in $t$ rounds}.

Lemma 4.11

For complementary events
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
and
E_{1}\cap E_{2}^{\prime}
we have the following.

P(E_{2}/E_{1})=1-P(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})

(4.45)

For complementary events
E_{1}\cap E_{2}
and
E_{1}\cap E_{2}^{\prime}
we
have

\displaystyle P(E_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(E_{1}\cap E_{2})+P(E_{1}\cap E_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\frac{P(E_{1})}{P(E_{1})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{P(E_{1}\cap E_{2})+P(E_{1}\cap E_{2}^{\prime})}{P(E_{1})}

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{P(E_{1}\cap E_{2})}{P(E_{1})}+\frac{P(E_{1}\cap E_{2}^{\prime})}{P(E_{1})}

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle P(E_{2}/E_{1})+P(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1}),

and after solving for
P(E_{2}/E_{1})
the lemma follows.

Furthermore we have the following for the Solovay-Strassen algorithm

P(E_{1}/E_{2})\leq\frac{1}{2^{t}}.

(4.46)

By Equation
4.44
we have the following with the substitutions obtained through the
three mappings for
A,A^{\prime}
and
B
.

P(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})=\frac{P(E_{1}/E_{2}^{\prime})P(E_{2}^{\prime})}{P(E_{1}/E_{2})P(E_{2})+P(E_{1}/E_{2}^{\prime})P(E_{2}^{\prime})}.

(4.47)

By the prime number theorem, quoting the density of primes, we have the following.

P(E_{2}^{\prime})\approx\frac{1}{\ln{n}}.

(4.48)

Furthermore,

P(E_{2})=1-P(E_{2}^{\prime})\approx 1-\frac{1}{\ln{n}}.

(4.49)

Moreover,

P(E_{1}/E_{2}^{\prime})=\frac{P(E_{1}\cap E_{2}^{\prime}}{P(E_{2}^{\prime})}=1,

(4.50)

since no witness for the compositeness of
n
is to be generated for
n
a
prime number.
Therefore Eq. (
4.47
) by way of Eq.(
4.48
), Eq.(
4.49
), Eq.(
4.50

yields the following.

\displaystyle P(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{P(E_{1}/E_{2}^{\prime})P(E_{2}^{\prime})}{P(E_{1}/E_{2})P(E_{2})+P(E_{1}/E_{2}^{\prime})P(E_{2}^{\prime})}

(4.51)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1\cdot\frac{1}{\ln{n}}}{P(E_{1}/E_{2})\cdot(1-\frac{1}{\ln{n}})+1\cdot\frac{1}{\ln{n}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{P(E_{1}/E_{2})\cdot(\ln{n}-1)+1}\text{by way of Eq.~(\ref{lbSS})}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\frac{\ln{n}-1}{2^{t}}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle{1-\frac{\ln{n}-1}{2^{t}}},

where in the last equation one can use
1/(1+x)\geq 1-x
for
0<x<1
.
Therefore one can also write

P(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})\geq 1-\frac{\ln{n}-1}{2^{t}}\geq 1-\frac{\ln{n}}{2^{t}},

(4.52)

and by using Lemma
4.11
one can obtain the following.

P(E_{2}/E_{1})\leq\frac{\ln{n}-1}{2^{t}}\leq\frac{\ln{n}}{2^{t}}.

(4.53)

The corresponding results for the
Rabin-Miller probabilistic primality test then become
as follows.

P(E_{2}^{\prime}/E_{1})\geq 1-\frac{\ln{n}-1}{4^{t}}\geq 1-\frac{\ln{n}}{4^{t}},

(4.54)

P(E_{2}/E_{1})\leq\frac{\ln{n}-1}{4^{t}}\leq\frac{\ln{n}}{4^{t}}.

(4.55)

This completes the proof of the lemma.
∎

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be an odd composite integer with
n>10
,
such that
n-1=2^{k}l
, where
l
is an odd integer.
Furthermore let

n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{r}^{a_{r}}
be the prime
decomposition of
n
, and
let
d(n)=r
indicate the number of distinct prime divisors
of
n
.

Let
b(n)
be the largest integer value such that

2^{b(n)}|p-1
, for every prime number
p
such that

p|n
.

It has already been shown that
n
is a prime number if and only
if for every
a
such
n\nmid a

either

P_{1}(a,n):a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n},

or

P_{2}(a,n,m):a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n},

for some integer
m
such that
0\leq m<k
.

Show that if
a\in MRnw(n)

then the following holds

a^{2^{b(n)-1}\cdot l}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n}.

(4.56)

Proof.

We reconsider the following from an earlier discussion.
If
a\in MRw(n)
, that is,
a
is a
MRw(a,n)
or
a
is a Miller-Rabin
witness of the compositeness of
n
, then the following apply.

\text{n is composite}\Leftrightarrow\exists a:\quad n|a\quad\vee\quad\begin{cases}a^{l}\not\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }C_{1}(a)=\overline{P_{1}(a,n)}\\
\text{and}&\\
\forall m,0\leq m<k:a^{2^{m}l}\not\equiv-1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }C_{2}(a,n,m)=\overline{P_{2}(a,n,m)}\\
\end{cases}

(4.57)

On the other hand if
a\not\in MRw(n)
that is,

a\in MRnw(n)
or
a
a Miller-Rabin NON-witness of the
compositeness of
n
the following apply then.
Integer
n
can be a prime number or not: we classify it as a
PseudoPrime then.

\text{n is prime}\Leftrightarrow\forall a:\quad n\nmid a\quad\wedge\quad\begin{cases}a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }P_{1}(a,n)\\
\text{or}&\\
\exists m,0\leq m<k:a^{2^{m}l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}&\text{Case }P_{2}(a,n,m)\\
\end{cases}

(4.58)

Consider
a\in MRnw(n)
. There can be one of two cases
then:
P_{1}(a,n)
or
P_{2}(a,n,m)
for some
m
.

Case 1.
If
P_{1}(a,n)
is the case then

a^{l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
and we are done since then

a^{2^{b(n)-1}\cdot l}\equiv 1\pmod{n}
.

Case 2.
Otherwise
P_{2}(a,n,m)
is the case for
some
m
such that
0\leq m<k
. Call that
m
,
j
that
is for
m=j
we have the following:

a^{2^{j}\cdot l}\equiv-1\pmod{n}
.
For any prime number
p
dividing
n

(e.g.
p_{1},p_{2},\ldots,p_{r}
) we also
have the following.

a^{2^{j}\cdot l}\equiv-1\pmod{p}.

(4.59)

Consider
u=ord_{p}(a)
. The
u
is the smallest positive
integer such that
a^{u}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
From Eq. (
4.59
), and the definition of
u

we conclude
u|2^{j}\cdot l
.
From Eq. (
4.59
) we obtain

a^{2^{j+1}\cdot l}\equiv-1\pmod{p}
, and
from the definition of
u
we further obtain
that
u|2^{j+1}\cdot l
,
Furthermore
u\nmid 2^{j}\cdot l
since otherwise
it would have been
a^{2^{j}\cdot l}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
.
Therefore
u
has
2^{j+1}
as the largest power of two dividing
it (
u
) that is
2^{j+1}|u
.
Furthermore for prime
p
we have by Fermat’s little theorem

a^{p-1}\equiv 1\pmod{p}
, which implies
u|p-1
,
and therefore
2^{j+1}|p-1
.

The latter implies
j+1\leq b(n)
.

We then have two cases.

Case 2a:
j+1=b(n)
.

Then
j=b(n)-1
and therefore by Eq. (
4.59
)
we have

a^{2^{b(n)-1}\cdot l}\equiv-1\pmod{p}.

Case 2b:
j+1<b(n)
.

Then
j<b(n)-1
. By Eq. (
4.59
) and squaring
we obtain the following.

a^{2^{j+1}\cdot l}\equiv 1\pmod{p},

which implies

a^{2^{b(n)-1}\cdot l}\equiv 1\pmod{p}.

The latter concludes the proof of this proposition.
∎

4.7.7
Proving the Rabin bound

We present a proof of Proposition
4.5

that is based on
[
5
]

originating from the paper of
[
7
]
.
Proposition
4.5
will utilize the result
of Theorem
4.11
.
Moreover, Theorem
4.11
relies
on a series of propositions itself.

Theorem 4.11

( MillerRabin bound)

In a follow-up problem the following will be shown.

|C(n)|\leq\phi(n)/4,

which would then imply

|MRw(n)|\geq(n-1)-\phi(n)/4.

Proof.

It follows by way of
Proposition
4.10
shown earlier,
Proposition
4.11
,
Proposition
4.12
, and
Proposition
4.13
below.
∎

Proposition 4.10

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
be an odd composite integer with
n>10
,
such that
n-1=2^{k}l
, where
l
is an odd integer.
Furthermore let

n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{r}^{a_{r}}
be the prime
decomposition of
n
, and
let
d(n)=r
indicate the number of distinct prime divisors
of
n
.
Let
b(n)
be the largest integer value such that

2^{b(n)}|p-1
, for every prime number
p
such that

p|n
.

(a) Let

C(n)=\left\{a\in\mathbb{U}_{n}:a^{2^{m}\cdot l}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n}\right\}

(4.60)

Show that if
a\in MRnw(n)

then
a\in C(n)
.

(b) Furthermore show that
MRnw(n)\subseteq C(n)
.

Proof.

Both (a) and (b) follow trivially from the prior discussion.
∎

Proposition 4.11
(Auxiliary result 1)

Let
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{r}^{a_{r}}
be the prime
decomposition of composite
n
,
let
d(n)=r
indicate the number of distinct prime divisors
of
n
.
Furthermore, let
n-1=2^{k}l
, where
l
is an odd integer.
Let
y\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\gcd(y,n)=1
and let

b\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
.

(a)
Then, the following modular equation

x^{v}\equiv y\pmod{p_{i}^{a^{i}}},

(4.61)

has a solution
x
mod
p_{i}^{a_{i}}
if

d_{i}=\gcd(v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}}))|ind_{g_{i}}(y),

and if there is at least one solution, conclude then
that the number of solutions is
d_{i}
.

(b)
Using CRT then the number of solutions of

x^{v}\equiv y\pmod{n},

(4.62)

is given by the following expression

\prod_{i=1}^{r}d_{i}=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\gcd(v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}}))=\prod_{i=1}^{r}\gcd(v,(p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}(p_{i}-1))).

(4.63)

Proof.

(a) Let
g_{i}
be a generator of
\mathbb{U}_{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}
.
Let
ind_{g_{i}}(x)
and
ind_{g_{i}}(y)
be the indices of

x,y
respectively.

\displaystyle x^{v}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle y\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle x^{v}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle y\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\ \forall i=1,\ldots,r\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle g_{i}^{ind_{g_{i}}(x)v}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle g_{i}^{ind_{g_{i}}(y)}\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle{ind_{g_{i}}(x)v}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle{ind_{g_{i}}(y)}\pmod{\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})}

(4.64)

Viewing the last equation Eq.(
4.64
) as a modular equation and
invoking the results associated with
Eq.(
1.30
) we obtain the following.
A solution for Eq.(
4.64
) exists if and only if

d_{i}|ind_{g_{i}}(y),

where

d_{i}=\gcd(v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})).

If one solution exists, then the number of solution
mod
p_{i}^{a_{i}}
is equal to
d_{i}
.

(b) Follows by the CRT and
the fact that

\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})=(p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}(p_{i}-1))
.
∎

Proposition 4.12
(Auxiliary result 2)

Let
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{r}^{a_{r}}
be the prime
decomposition of composite
n
,
let
d(n)=r
indicate the number of distinct prime divisors
of
n
.
Furthermore, let
n-1=2^{k}l
, where
l
is an odd integer.
Let
b(n)
be the largest integer value such that

2^{b(n)}|p-1
, for every prime number
p
such that

p|n
.

We reintroduce Eq. (
4.60
).

C(n)=\left\{a\in\mathbb{U}_{n}:a^{2^{m}\cdot l}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n}\right\}

(4.65)

Show the following.

c=|C(n)|=2\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}2^{(b(n)-1)}\gcd(p-1,l)=2\cdot 2^{(b(n)-1)\cdot d(n)}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}\gcd(p-1,l).

(4.66)

Proof.

For
b(n)
let us define
v=2^{b(n)-1}\cdot l
.
Equation (
4.60
) leads us to count the number
of
a
of
C(n)
that satisfy
modular equation

a^{v}\equiv 1\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow a^{v}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r

(4.67)

or modular equation

a^{v}\equiv-1\pmod{n}\Leftrightarrow a^{v}\equiv-1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r

(4.68)

Case 1: Number of solutions of Eq.(
4.67
).

We start with the former.
Eq.(
4.67
) by Eq.(
4.61
) has a number of solutions equal to

d_{i}=\gcd(v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})),

as long as

d_{i}|ind_{g_{i}}(y)=d_{i}|ind_{g_{i}}(1)

which is the case
for
ind_{g_{i}}(1)=\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})
.
Then

\displaystyle\forall i=1,2,\ldots r\quad:\gcd(v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(v,p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}(p_{i}-1))

(4.69)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(v,(p_{i}-1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(2^{b(n)-1}\cdot l,(p_{i}-1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{b(n)-1}\cdot\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1)).

The term
p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}
was removed from the first
equation above, because

p_{i}\nmid v
. If it were

p_{i}|v
then since
v|n-1
we
would have had
p_{i}|n-1
. Since

p_{i}|n
the last two divisibility results
would lead to
p_{i}|1
i.e.
p_{i}=1
.
This violates the assumption of the unique factorization
of
n
where
p_{i}
are prime numbers and in fact greater
than two.

By the CRT and combining for
i=1,2,\ldots,r
,
Eq. (
4.69
) we obtain
that for modular equation

a^{v}\equiv 1\pmod{n}

the number of its solutions is as follows.

\prod_{i=1}^{r}2^{b(n)-1}\cdot\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1))=2^{(b(n)-1)r}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1))=2^{(b(n)-1)d(n)}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1)).

Case 2: Number of solutions of Eq.(
4.68
).

We continue with the latter
Eq.(
4.68
) to establish the number of
solutions of

a^{v}\equiv-1\pmod{n}

by establishing the number of solutions of

a^{v}\equiv-1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r.

Note that the latter implies

a^{2v}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r.

Therefore we find the number of solutions of

a^{2v}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\wedge\quad a^{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r.

We start with the former.
The
d_{i}
is slightly different.

d_{i}=\gcd(2v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}})),

We note that we need

d_{i}|ind_{g_{i}}(y)=d_{i}|ind_{g_{i}}(1)

which is the case as before for Case 1.
Then

\displaystyle\forall i=1,2,\ldots r\quad:\gcd(2v,\phi(p_{i}^{a_{i}}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(2v,p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}(p_{i}-1))

(4.70)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(2v,(p_{i}-1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\gcd(2^{b(n)}\cdot l,(p_{i}-1))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2^{b(n)}\cdot\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1)).

a^{2v}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\wedge\quad a^{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r.

Therefore the number of solutions of

a^{2v}\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\wedge\quad a^{v}\not\equiv 1\pmod{p_{i}^{a_{i}}}\quad\forall i=1,2,\ldots,r

is equal to

2^{b(n)}\cdot\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1))-2^{b(n)-1}\cdot\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1))=2^{b(n)-1}\cdot\gcd(l,(p_{i}-1)),

just like case 1.
This concludes the case and the result.
∎

Proposition 4.13
(Auxiliary result 3)

Let
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\ldots p_{r}^{a_{r}}
be the prime
decomposition of composite
n
, and
let
d(n)=r
indicate the number of distinct prime divisors
of
n
.
Furthermore, let
n-1=2^{k}l
, where
l
is an odd integer.
Let
b(n)
be the largest integer value such that

2^{b(n)}|p-1
, for every prime number
p
such that

p|n
.

For

C(n)=\left\{a\in\mathbb{U}_{n}:a^{2^{m}\cdot l}\equiv\pm 1\pmod{n}\right\}

Then, we have the following.

c=|C(n)|\leq\frac{\phi(n)}{4},

(4.71)

for all odd composite
n>10
.

Proof.

From Eq.(
4.66
) we have the following.

\displaystyle c=|C(n)|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}2^{(b(n)-1)}\gcd(p-1,l)

(4.72)

\displaystyle\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{r}\frac{p_{i}^{a^{i}-1}(p_{i}-1)}{2^{(b(n)-1)}\gcd(p-1,l)}

(4.73)

We note a few thing related to Eq.(
4.73
).
Since
2^{b(n)-1}|2^{b(n)}

and

2^{b(n)}|p-1
for each
i
, we have that

\frac{(p_{i}-1)}{2^{b(n)-1}}
is an integer and also an
even number. Moreover
\gcd(l,p_{i}-1)|p_{i}-1

and
\gcd(l,p_{i}-1)
is an odd number,
since
l
is odd.
Thus the product term of Eq.(
4.73
) is an integer
and a multiple of two and multiplied by the outside

1/2
still yields an integer.
We now perform a case analysis.

Case 1:
r=d(n)\geq 3
.

Then

\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot 2\cdot 2=4.

Case 2:
r=d(n)=2
, and
n
is not squarefree.

Then for some
p_{i}
we have
a_{i}\geq 2
. In the numerator
of Eq.(
4.73
)
p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}
contributes a

p_{i}^{a_{i}-1}\geq p_{i}\geq 3
.

\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(3\cdot 2\right)\cdot 2=6.

Case 3:
r=d(n)=2
, but
n
is squarefree.

Let
n=pq
where
p<q<n
. We have that

2^{b(n)}|p-1

and

2^{b(n)}|q-1
.
We distinguish two subcases.

Case 3a:
2^{b(n)+1}|q-1
.

This means
q-1=4\cdot 2^{b(n)-1}\cdot R
.
Then we have as follows.

\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{q-1}{2^{b(n)-1}\gcd(l,q-1)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot 4=4.

Case 3b:
2^{b(n)+1}\nmid q-1
but
2^{b(n)1}|q-1
.

Then we have as follows.

\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{q-1}{2^{b(n)-1}\gcd(l,q-1)}\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{2\cdot Q}{\gcd(l,q-1)}

(4.74)

Note that
n-1=2^{k}l
, where
b(n)\leq k
.
Moreover,
q-1=2^{b(n)}Q
, where
Q
can be even or odd.
Furthermore,

n-1=pq-1=p(q-1)+(p-1)\Rightarrow n-1\equiv p-1\pmod{q-1}\Rightarrow q-1\nmid n-1.

The latter implies that there is a prime divisor of
q-1

that does not divide
n-1
. Since
b(n)\leq k
, this divisor
is not a two. So it must be an odd number and it is at least 3.
Therefore
q-1=2Q=2\cdot 3\cdot P
.
Therefore we refine the previous bound of Eq.(
4.74
)
as follows.

\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{2\cdot Q}{\gcd(l,q-1)}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot 2\cdot\frac{2\cdot 3\cdot P}{\gcd(l,q-1)}\geq 6.

(4.75)

Case 4:
d(n)=1
and
n
is not squarefree i.e.
n=p^{a}
,
for some
a\geq 2
and
p\geq 3
.

A minimal such value is for
a=2
and
p=3
and it is
n=3^{2}=9
.
We then have he following.

\frac{\phi(n)}{|C(n)|}\geq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p^{a-1}(p-1)}{2^{b(n)-1}\gcd(l,p-1)}

Note that
p-1=2^{k}\cdot l
. Moreover
b(n)=k
.
Then
\gcd(l,p-1)=\gcd(l,2^{k}\cdot l)=l
.
Therefore

\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p^{a-1}(p-1)}{2^{b(n)-1}\gcd(l,p-1)}=\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{p^{a-1}\cdot 2^{k}\cdot l}{2^{k-1}l}=p^{a-1}.

If
p=3
and
a=2
this
p^{a-1}
is a
3
.
We discard this possibility by requiring

n>9
and for composite and odd
n
this becomes
n>10
.
In all other cases
p^{a-1}
is at least
5
.
∎

Chapter 5
Multiplicative functions

5.1
Multiplicative functions

Definition 5.1

A function
f
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is multiplicative if

f(ij)=f(i)f(j)\quad\forall i\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\forall j\in\mathbb{Z}_{+},\text{where}\ \gcd(i,j)=1.

Lemma 5.1

If
f
, as previously defined, is multiplicative and
not zero then
f(1)=1
.

Proof.

This derives for
i=j=1
and
f(1)=f(1)f(1)
,
provided that
f(1)\neq 0
.
Otherwise, since
f
is not zero then there
exists a
k
such that
f(k)\neq 0
. Then

f(k\cdot 1)=f(k)f(1)
and dividing
by
f(k)\neq 0
we reach the same conclusion.
Note that if
f(1)=0
then
f(k\cdot 1)=f(k)f(1)=0
,
and therefore
f(k)=0
for all
k
.
∎

Proposition 5.1

If
n
is as follows,

n=\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{a_{i}},

then
f(n)=\prod_{i}f(p_{i}^{a_{i}})
,
where
f
is a multiplicative function.

Proof.

One can use induction on
k
. If
k=1

Then obviously,
f(n)=f(p_{1}^{a_{1}}=\prod_{i=1}^{1}f(p_{1}^{a_{1}})
.
Now consider that the result is true of
k
or less and we want
to show it for
k+1
., given that
\gcd(p_{l},p_{m})=1
for all

l,m=1,\ldots,k+1
we have

\displaystyle f(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\prod_{i=1}^{k+1}p_{i}^{a_{i}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{a_{i}}\cdot p_{k+1}^{a_{k+1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{a_{i}})\cdot f(p_{k+1}^{a_{k+1}})

and then applying the inductive hypothesis for
k=k
on the first
term of the right hand side.
∎

Proposition 5.2

If
f
is a multiplicative function, and

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d),

then
g(n)
is also multiplicative.

Proof.

For
f
multiplicative and
p,q
such that
\gcd(p,q)=1

we have
f(pq)=f(p)f(q)
. If
d|pq
and given
\gcd(p,q)=1

then either
d|p
or
d|q
or
d=d_{1}d_{2}
and

d_{1}|p
and
d_{2}|q
and
\gcd(d_{1},d_{2})=1
.
By the multiplicativity of
f
,
f(d_{1}d_{2})=f(d_{1})f(d_{2})
.
Then we have the following.

\displaystyle g(pq)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|pq}f(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|p,d_{2}|q}f(d_{1}d_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|p,d_{2}|q}f(d_{1})f(d_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|p}f(d_{1})\sum_{d_{2}|q}f(d_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g(p)g(q).

∎

Proposition 5.3

If
f
,
g
are multiplicative functions, and

M(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)g(n/d),

then
M(n)
is also multiplicative.

Proof.

For
f,g
multiplicative and
p,q
such that
\gcd(p,q)=1

we have
f(pq)=f(p)f(q)
and also
g(pq)=g(p)g(q)
.
If
d|pq
and given
\gcd(p,q)=1

then either
d|p
or
d|q
or
d=d_{1}d_{2}
and

d_{1}|p
and
d_{2}|q
and
\gcd(d_{1},d_{2})=1
.
(The latter case absorbs the two former ones that imply then

d_{2}=1
or
d_{1}=1
respectively.)
Moreover
\gcd(p/d_{1},q/d_{2})=1
.
Then we have the following.

\displaystyle M(pq)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|pq}f(d)g(\frac{pq}{d})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|p,\ d_{2}|q}f(d_{1}d_{2})g(\frac{pq}{d_{1}d_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|p,\ d_{2}|q}f(d_{1})f(d_{2})g(\frac{p}{d_{1}})\ g(\frac{q}{d_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|p}f(d_{1})g(\frac{p}{d_{1}})\sum_{d_{2}|q}f(d_{2})\ g(\frac{q}{d_{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle M(p)M(q).

∎

5.2
Totient function redefined

Definition 5.2
(Euler’s totient function)

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
, we define
\phi(n)
to be
the number of positive integers less than
n
that
are relatively prime to
n
.

\phi(n)=\sum_{1\leq i<n,\ \gcd(i,n)=1}1.

Proposition 5.4

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have the following.

n=\sum_{d|n}\phi(d).

Proof.

Let
n_{d}
be the number of integers
i
such that

\gcd(i,n)=d
or equivalently
\gcd(i/d,n/d)=1
.
By definition the number of
i/d
is
\phi(n/d)

and so is
n_{d}
i.e.
n_{d}=\phi(n/d)
.
If
d|n
then there exists an integer in
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
,

d_{1}
, such that
n=dd_{1}
.
Moreover,
d_{1}|n
.

\displaystyle n

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}n_{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\phi(\frac{n}{d})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n,dd_{1}=n}\phi(d_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|n}\phi(d_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\phi(d)

In the last derivation we renamed variable
d_{1}
into
a new variable
d
.
∎

Proposition 5.5

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have the following.

1.

For
n
a prime number
\phi(n)=n-1
. Moreover
if
\phi(n)=n-1
then
n
is a prime number.

2.

For
\gcd(p,q)=1
, Euler’s
\phi
function is
multiplicative and therefore

\phi(pq)=\phi(p)\phi(q),\quad\gcd(p,q)=1.

Proof.

(a)
Say
n
is a prime number. Then all
1,2,\ldots,n-1

are relatively prime to
n
and therefore
\phi(n)=n-1
,
as required.

Assume now that there is a composite number
n
such that

\phi(n)=n-1
. If
n
is a composite number there exists
a
d\neq 1,n
such that
d|n
.
Then in

n=\sum_{d|n}\phi(d),

there are three contributions:
\phi(1),\phi(d),\phi(n)

that re non-zero. For
\phi(1)=1
, and
\phi(d)\geq 1
imply

\phi(n)\leq n-2
and this contradicts the

\phi(n)=n-1
. Thus
n
is not compositive and it must be
prime number.

(b)
In Proposition (
5.2
) we had

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d),

and in Proposition (
5.4
) we had

n=\sum_{d|n}\phi(d).

Consider
g(n)=n
. Then
g
is a multiplicative function.
Consider
f(d)=\phi(d)
.
By way of Proposition (
5.2
), with the stated
substitutions, if we trace the proof there forward and backwards we
conclude that if
g(n)
is multiplicative then

f(n)
is multiplicative.
This establishes that
\phi(n)
is a multiplicative function.
∎

5.3
Möbius function

Definition 5.3
(Möbius function)

\mu(n)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }n=1,\\
0&\text{if n contains a square factor},\\
(-1)^{k}&\text{if n is the product of k distinct primes}.\end{cases}

Proposition 5.6

Let
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
. Then we have the following.

1.

\mu(n)
is multiplicative that is, for
p,q\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}

with
\gcd(p,q)=1
the following applies.

\mu(pq)=\mu(p)\mu(q).

2.

We have the following equality for the Möbius function.

I(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d),

where

I(n)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }n=1,\\
0&\text{if }n>1.\\
\end{cases}

Proof.

(1) Consider
p=q=1
.
Then
\mu(pq)=\mu(p)\mu(q)=1
.
Consider
r^{2}|p
. Since
\gcd(p,q)=1
then

r^{2}|pq
. Likewise for the
r^{2}|q
case.
Then
\mu(pq)=\mu(p)\mu(q)=0
.
Therefore we may assume that both
p,q
are square free.
Thus
p=p_{1}\ldots p_{k}
and
q=q_{1}\ldots q_{l}
.
Moreover
\gcd(p,q)=1
and thus
\gcd(p_{i},q_{j})=1
.

\displaystyle\mu(pq)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(p_{1}\ldots p_{k}q_{1}\ldots q_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k+l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(-1)^{k}(-1)^{l}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(p_{1}\ldots p_{k})\mu(q_{1}\ldots q_{l})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(p)\mu(q),

as needed.

(2) If
n=1
then
I(n)=1
.
Moreover

I(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)

is true as the left-hand side is
I(n)=I(1)=1

and the right-hand side for
n=1
has only one term
for
d=1
and
\mu(d)=\mu(1)=1
.
Thus the expression is true.
Moreover if
n>1

by way of Proposition (
5.2
),
and the multiplicativity of
\mu(n)

consider only the case where
n
is a prime power,

n=p^{k}
, with the general case proven by induction.
We have the following.

I(p^{k})=\sum_{d|p^{k}}\mu(d)=\mu(1)+\mu(p)+\mu(p^{2})+\ldots+\mu(p^{k})=1+(-1)+0+\ldots+0=1+(-1)=0

Noting that
p^{2},p^{3},\ldots
are not square-free,
the result follows.
An alternative proof of the
n\neq 1
is as follows.
As prime powers greater than 1 lead to a zero, sum the
only possibility left is for
n=p_{1}\ldots p_{k}
.
Then we have the following.

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu(p_{1})+\mu(p_{2})+\ldots+\mu(p_{k})+\mu(p_{1}p_{2})+\ldots+\mu(p_{1}p_{2}p_{3})+\ldots+\ldots+\mu(p_{1}\ldots p_{k})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=0}^{k}{k\choose i}(-1)^{i}=(1-1)^{k}=0.

∎

The following result will be proved again using
Möbius inversion formula as a corollary of the
corresponding theorem.

Lemma 5.2

For any
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have the following.

\phi(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\frac{n}{d}.

Proof.

We have by definition the following.

\phi(n)=\sum_{1\leq i<n,\ \gcd(i,n)=1}1\Rightarrow n=\sum_{d|n}\phi(d).

We also have the following equality for the
Möbius function.

I(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d),

\displaystyle\phi(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1,\ \gcd(i,n)=1}^{n}1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}I(\gcd(i,n))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ \sum_{d|\gcd(i,n)}\mu(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ \sum_{d|i,\ d|n}\mu(d)

For a given and fixed
d
such
d|n

the number of values
i
such that
d|i
i.e. that
are multiple of
d
is exactly
n/d
.
For
d|i
we have
i=da
and since
i\leq n
we have

da\leq n
and therefore
a\leq n/d
.
We can then rewrite the last double sum as follows.

\displaystyle\phi(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ \sum_{d|i,\ d|n}\mu(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\sum_{a=1}^{\frac{n}{d}}\mu(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\sum_{a=1}^{\frac{n}{d}}1

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\frac{n}{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n}{d}\sum_{d|n}\mu(d).

∎

Proposition 5.7

For
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have the following.

1.

For
n
a prime power
n=p^{k}
where
k>1
we
have

\phi(n)=\phi(p^{k})=p^{k}-p^{k-1}=n(1-1/p).

2.

For
n
a composite number with prime decomposition
as follows

n=\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{a_{i}},

we have the following.

\phi(n)=n\prod_{p|n}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)=p_{1}^{a_{1}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{1}}\right)p_{2}^{a_{2}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{2}}\right)\ldots p_{k}^{a_{k}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{k}}\right).

Proof.

(1) For
n=p^{k}
the only divisors of
n
are multiples of
p

and there are
n/p=p^{k-1}
of them. Therefore the number of
integers relatively prime to (i.e. non divisors of )
n
are

p^{k}-p^{k-1}
, as needed.

(2)
Use induction and the multiplicativity of
\phi(n)
.
This completes the proof.

Or use induction as follows, but we will need a result
to be proven later.
For
n=1
, there is no product and thus
\phi(1)=1
.
For
n\geq 2
, given that

n=\prod_{i=1}^{k}p_{i}^{a_{i}},

we need to show

\displaystyle\phi(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\prod_{p|n}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\prod_{i=1}^{k}\left(1-\frac{1}{p_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\left(1-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{p_{i}}+\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{p_{i}p_{j}}+\ldots+(-1)^{k}\sum\frac{1}{p_{1}\ldots p_{k}}\right).

The parenthesized expression is

\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\cdot\frac{1}{d}.

Therefore

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\prod_{p|n}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\left(1-\sum_{i}\frac{1}{p_{i}}+\sum_{i,j}\frac{1}{p_{i}p_{j}}+\ldots+(-1)^{k}\sum\frac{1}{p_{1}\ldots p_{k}}\right).

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\frac{1}{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\frac{n}{d}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\phi(n),

where the last derivation remains to be shown.
∎

5.4
Dirichlet product

Definition 5.4

A function
f
on
\mathbb{Z}_{+}
is arithmetic
if its range is a subset of the composite numbers.

Definition 5.5
(Dirichlet product)

For two arithmetic functions
f,g
the Dirichlet product
is defined as follows

(f*g)(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)g(\frac{n}{d})=\sum_{ab=n}f(a)g(b).

Theorem 5.1

The Dirichlet product is commutative and associative that is

f*g=g*f
and
(f*g)*h=f*(g*h)
for three arithmetic functions

f,g,h
.

Proof.

Commutativity follows from the definition.

(f*g)(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)g(\frac{n}{d})=\sum_{ab=n}f(a)g(b).=\sum_{d|n}g(d)f(\frac{n}{d})(g*f)(n).

Associativity follows similarly, after observing.

((f*g)*h)(n)=(\sum_{ab=d}f(a)g(b))\sum{dc=n}h(c)=\sum_{abc=n}f(a)g(b)h(c).

∎

5.5
Unit function

Definition 5.6
(Unit function)

U(n)=1,\ \forall n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}.

Corollary 5.1

For all arithmetic functions
f
we have

f*I=I*f=f

Proof.

(f*I)(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)I(\frac{n}{d})=f(n).

Function
I(\frac{n}{d})
is 1 for
n=d
and 0 otherwise.
Therefore the sum has one non-zero term for
d=n
and then

f(d)=f(n)
.
∎

5.6
Dirichlet and Möbius inversions

Theorem 5.2

(Dirichlet inverse)

Given an arithmetic function
f
, where
f(1)\neq 0
,
there exists the inverse
f^{-1}
of
f
such that

f*f^{-1}=f^{-1}*f=I.

Moreover the calculation of
f^{-1}
is recursive
as follows for
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle f^{-1}(1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{f(1)}

\displaystyle f^{-1}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{f(1)}\sum_{d|n,d\neq n}f(\frac{n}{d})f^{-1}(d),\quad n>1.

Proof.

Use induction.
For
n=1
we have

(f*f^{-1})(1)=I(1),

or equivalently
1=f(1)f^{-1}(1)
and the result follows.
for
f(1)\neq 0
. For
n>1
let us assume that
f^{-1}(i)

has been calculated for
i<n
.
Then

(f*f^{-1})(n)=I(n)

and thus by definition of the Dirichlet product

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}f(\frac{n}{d})f^{-1}(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(1)f^{-1}(n)+\sum_{d|n,d\neq n}f(\frac{n}{d})f^{-1}(d)

\displaystyle f^{-1}(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{f(1)}\sum_{d|n,d\neq n}f(\frac{n}{d})f^{-1}(d).

∎

Theorem 5.3

(Möbius inversion)

Let
f
be any arithmetic function and if

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)

then

f(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})g(d)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)g(\frac{n}{d}).

Proof.

1. By use of Dirichlet products.

An easy proof follows by using Dirichlet products.
Function
g(n)
is in fact

g(n)=(f*U)(n)

Then we have the following

g*\mu=(f*U)*\mu)=f*(U*\mu)=f*(\mu*U)=f*I=f,

with the latter being equivalent to

\sum_{d|n}g(d)\mu(\frac{n}{d})=f(n),

after noting that for the Möbius function

I(n)=(\mu*U)(n),

and the
*
in this proof indicates a Dirichlet product.

2. By direct methods: sums.

For

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d),

we note
the following that will be used in the sums to follow.

n=d\frac{n}{d}=dn_{1}=d_{1}\frac{d}{d_{1}}n_{1}=d_{1}d_{2}n_{1},

where

\frac{n}{d}=n_{1}\Rightarrow n=dn_{1},

and

\frac{d}{d_{1}}=d_{2}\Rightarrow d=d_{1}d_{2}.

Furthermore, note that

I(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d).

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)g(\frac{n}{d})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})g(d)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})\sum_{d_{1}|d}f(d_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{1}d=n}\mu(n_{1})\sum_{d_{1}d_{2}=n}f(d_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n_{1}d_{1}d_{2}=n}\mu(n_{1})f(d_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}n_{1}d_{2}=n}f(d_{1})\sum_{n_{1}d_{2}=\frac{n}{d_{1}}}\mu(n_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|n}f(d_{1})\sum_{n_{1}|\frac{n}{d_{1}}}\mu(n_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}|n}f(d_{1})I(\frac{n}{d_{1}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d_{1}=n}f(d_{1})=f(n).

∎

A corollary of the Möbius inversion formula establishes
a result for
\phi(n)
as follows.

Corollary 5.2

For every
n\in\mathbb{Z}_{+}
we have the following.

\phi(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\frac{n}{d}.

Proof.

From the Möbius inversion formula and
f(n),g(n)

arithemtic functions as follows, we have the following.
If

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)

then

f(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})g(d)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)g(\frac{n}{d}).

Let
f(n)=\phi(n)
and let
g(n)=n
.
From Proposition (
5.4
) we have

n=\sum_{d|n}\phi(d)\Leftrightarrow g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d).

The precondition of Proposition (
5.3
)
is true. Therefore the conclusion is as follows.

\displaystyle f(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)g(\frac{n}{d})\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\phi(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)g(\frac{n}{d})\Leftrightarrow

\displaystyle\phi(n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)\frac{n}{d}.

∎

Theorem 5.4

(Möbius inversion converse)

Let
f
be any arithmetic function and if

f(n)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})g(d)=\sum_{d|n}\mu(d)g(\frac{n}{d}),

then

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d).

Proof.

Function
f(n)
is in fact

f(n)=(g*\mu)(n),

and we want to show the following.

g=f*U\Leftrightarrow g(n)=(f*U)(n).

Then we have the following

f*U=(g*\mu)*U=g*(\mu*U)=g*I=g,

with the latter being equivalent to

g(n)=\sum_{d|n}f(d)U(\frac{n}{d})=\sum_{d|n}f(d).

∎

Consider the two functions

a(n)=\sum_{d|n}1,

and

b(n)=\sum_{d|n}d.

Both can be inverted and

1=\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})a(d),

and

n=\sum_{d|n}\mu(\frac{n}{d})b(d).
